## THREE FILES FROM THE 30th ROUND — DEGREE, CAUSATION, FOCUS

Double enquiry at rank (a), 2026-08-16. Both translators had stumbled at
the same three places and bent the source text to get out of it. **The
three do not have the same status**, and that is the main result:

| | what it was | what must be done |
|---|---|---|
| the degree of the adverb | **a false problem — the kit's rule was wrong** | correct the rule, absolve the translators |
| focus | a **compensated gap** — V2 does the work | write out the four devices |
| the inanimate causative | the **only real gap**, and it is not syntactic | declare that the turn does not exist |

⚠⚠ **TWO OF THESE THREE BLOCKS CAME FROM PROHIBITIONS OF THE KIT THAT
WERE STRICTER THAN THE ATTESTED LANGUAGES.** That is the round's lesson in
method, and it is an unpleasant one: *when two independent translators
converge on the same "infraction", the first hypothesis to test is that
the rule is wrong.* Here it was wrong two times out of three.

### 1 — THE DEGREE OF THE ADVERB: `-or` / `-ost`

**The kit was wrong.** It declared that it had "no regular adverbial
comparative" and gave †*oftor* as a forge. Germanic has an **inherited
adverbial series, distinct from the adjective's and productive**:
Proto-Germanic `*-ōz` → **`-or`**, `*-ōst` → **`-ost`**, invariable.

· comparative: **-or** — *hardor*, *swīthor*, *hauhor*, *snellor*;
· superlative: **-ost** — *hardost*, *swīthost*, *hauhost*, *snellost*;
· **never inflected, never agreeing.**

⚠⚠ **AND THIS IS WHERE "LITTLE" BELONGS, NOT AT THE § ON THE PARTITIVE
QUANTIFIER** — adverbial `luttil` is the **positive of this series**, and
it states a deficit ("he sleeps little"); *luttilu*, 4,900 lines below,
states a positive quantity ("he sleeps **a** little"), **which is nearly
the opposite**. *Both translators of the 43rd round stumbled here; one
found the right section as a last resort, the other never found it and
wrote a different form — whence the round's only non-lexical structural
divergence.*

**Suppletives** — *filu / **mair** / **maist*** · *luttil / **min** /
**minnist*** · *wela / **bet** / **betst*** · *ubilo / **wirs** /
**wirsist*** · *lango / **leng** / **lengist*** · *air / — / **airist***.

⚠⚠ **THE ERROR WAS ONE OF CATEGORY, AND IT MUST BE SAID SO AS NOT TO BE
REPEATED.** The kit stated "an adjective cannot modify a verb", which is
right, **and applied it to words that are not adjectives**: *mair*, *bet*,
*wirs*, *min*, *leng*, *air* are not neuter adjectives, they are
**inherited adverbial comparatives** — a frozen old case — invariable by
nature. The boundary is not between two paradigms, it is **between
inflection and non-inflection**:
> **The INFLECTED adjective does not modify the verb. The UNINFLECTED
> form *is* the adverb.**
That is why the Old Saxon dictionary labels *betst* "Adj. Superl.,
**Adv.**": it is one form, adverbial as soon as it is bare.

⚠⚠ **AND `allero thingō minnist` IS THE ATTESTED TURN, NOT AN
INFRACTION.** Reinforcing an adverbial superlative with a partitive
genitive plural is Germanic and verified in the corpus: Gothic **1 Tim
2:1 — *frumist allis*** "before all", literally "the first of all". The
same type in OE *ealra swīþost*; and OHG *meist* takes the same
reinforcement, *allero meist*. **Both translators independently
recovered an attested Gothic construction**; it is the best signal this
round gave.
· "the least" on a verb → **`minnist`**, reinforceable as *allero thingō
  minnist* or *alles minnist*;
· "the most" on a verb → **`maist`**, reinforceable likewise;
· "he runs the fastest" → *hē hlaupid swīthost*;
· "he comes more often" → *hē quimid oftor*.


⚠ **AN HONEST BOUND ON †*oftor*, AND IT COMES FROM THE MORE CAUTIOUS OF
THE TWO ENQUIRERS.** Checked in Köbler's Old Saxon dictionary: the corpus
attests *oft* and *ofto*, **never *oftor***. The form is regularly
derivable and Old English has it — but it is not attested on the
continent. **It is marked reconstructed, and no longer forbidden**; and
the partitive genitive *mair stundono* remains good, not as a makeshift
but as another construction.
Direct proof that an adverb is compared before a verb (*Heliand* 3904):
OS ***rōmodun te rehta bet than thie rīkeon man*** "they strove after
right **better than** the mighty men".

### 2 — FOCUS: NO CLEFT, AND THAT IS NOT A POVERTY

**Frenkisk has no cleft sentence.** The turn "it is X who…" exists
neither in Gothic, nor in Old Saxon, nor in Old High German, nor at rank
(a): it is an innovation that appears marginally in late Old English and
is not grammaticalised until Middle English.

⚠⚠ **AND THE CONTACT ARGUMENT TURNS ROUND — I had put it backwards.** I
had hoped Gallo-Romance contact would licence the cleft. **At 550
Gallo-Romance has none either**: *ce est … que* is documented from Old
French, the 9th century at the earliest. **One cannot borrow a
construction the source language does not yet possess.**

⚠ **A STRUCTURAL REASON, AND IT IS THE STRONGEST**: a **V2** language
with a multifunctional prefield **does not need** a cleft. English
developed its own **in compensation for the loss of V2** (Los, *A
Historical Syntax of English*, 2015). Frenkisk has the prefield: that is
its focusing device. *The absence of the cleft is not a shortcoming, it
is the consequence of having the other device.*

**The four devices, and all of them are attested**:
· **the prefield** — the focused constituent in front, verb second,
  subject pushed behind. This is the basic device;
· **left dislocation with a demonstrative resumptive** — *thie man, thē…*,
  *that thing, that…*. This is the closest functional equivalent of a
  cleft;
· **the correlated free relative** — *that … , that ist …* "what …, that
  is …". Structurally native: a relative with no antecedent, subject of a
  copula;
· **the negative exception** — *ni … nibu / ak* "nothing … but", which
  gives **exhaustive** focus, precisely what a cleft does.
Plus the reinforcements: *self*, *aino* "alone", *gawisso*.

### 3 — THE CAUSATIVE: NO INANIMATE CAUSER

⚠⚠ **THIS IS NOT A GAP IN SYNTAX, IT IS A GAP IN ONTOLOGY**, and the
first enquirer's phrasing is the right one: *in this language courage does
not act upon people; people act with courage.* Old Germanic **does** have
the analytic causative — Gothic Mk 7:37, *gataujiþ gahausjan* "he makes
them hear" — but **it does not put an abstract in the agent position**.
Abstracts there are circumstantials, not subjects.

· **`haitan` + infinitive stays bounded to the ORDER GIVEN**, and both
  enquirers confirm the kit's note: it is a *verbum iubendi*, its subject
  is God, Christ, a king — never an abstract. The weakening towards a
  neutral causative is **Middle Dutch and Middle High German**. *Do not
  extend it.*
· **`lātan` + infinitive is PERMISSIVE at this date**, not factitive — "not
  to prevent". Rendering it "to make someone do" would be a misreading by
  six centuries.
· **`dōn` + `that` + finite clause is ADMITTED** for a personal causer — a
  calque of Latin *facere ut*, alive in Merovingian Latin.
  ⚠ But **`dōn` + bare infinitive is set aside**: its grammaticalisation
  is a fact of the 12th–13th century in Middle Dutch.

· the `-jan` causatives (*settian*, *leggian*, *drenkian*) are a
  **closed, lexicalised class**, not a device.

**What one writes for an inanimate cause** — and it is better than the
flat instrumental the translators wrote: the **correlative
conditional**, which gives the bite without putting the abstract in
subject position.
> *Of gī luttil mōd hebbiad, thanne findid gī it.*
> or, in pure parataxis, more Germanic still:
> *Hebbiad luttil mōd — sō findid gī it.*
The correlative `of … thanne`, or the resumptive `sō`, does all the work
the source language entrusts to "will make".

### ⚠⚠ THE COMMON PRINCIPLE, AND IT HOLDS FOR ALL THREE

The three source-language turns — *make* + infinitive, *the most* + verb,
*it is … that* — are **biclausal** constructions that create, above the
sentence, a position for a role that case and order do not carry. They
are **late Romance grammaticalisations**. Germanic answers all three by
the same means:

> **WHERE ROMANCE CONSTRUCTS, GERMANIC CORRELATES.** A demonstrative in
> the main clause pointing to a subordinate one, or the reverse — `dōn
> **that** …`, `of … **thanne** …`, `**that** … , **that** is …`,
> `allero thingō **minnist**`. Romance welds two predications into one
> construction; Germanic juxtaposes them and links them with a deictic.

**General rule**: whenever the source language uses an analytic
construction to promote a non-agentive role, Frenkisk answers with (i)
morphology — case, `-jan`, `-or/-ost` —, (ii) the prefield, or (iii) a
second finite clause, correlated. **Never with a new periphrasis.**

⚠ **STILL OPEN**: the valency of `brestan` "to be lacking". Both
enquirers flagged it without being able to settle it — if the verb is
impersonal (dative of the person, genitive of the thing), then "the fund"
is not the subject and focus falls on the genitive. The lexicon does not
say. **Slot declared, to be instructed.**


## Essential morphology

⚠⚠ **THIS SECTION WAS REFLOWED ON 2026-08-16: IT IS FILED BY MATTER, NO
LONGER BY DATE OF RULING.** The two translators of the 30th round had
asked for it in the same words; one had had to **cross-check three places
550 lines apart** to establish a single plural imperative. **Nothing was
rewritten — the blocks were moved whole, and the check bore on the
multiset of lines, not on an impression.**

Nine matters, in this order: the article · the noun · the adjective · the
numerals · the pronouns · the verb · word formation · onomastics · the
prepositions and governments.

### ⚠⚠ THE PRONOMINAL GENITIVE DOES NOT MIGRATE — `thes wintras`, and it is right

**Ruling of 2026-08-17, on the report of the 34th round.** The translator
writes:

> *"I wrote `thes wintras`, `sīnes fader`, `thes thriddon dagas` — **one
> migrated ending and one unmigrated in the same phrase, three letters
> apart**, seven times in the text. It is the one slot I would say must be
> settled before all the rest: it is not local, it contaminates every noun
> phrase of every text."*

> **ANSWER: `thes`, `theses`, `gōdes`, `sīnes`, `hwes` — WITH NO
> migration. A noun in `-as` beside a determiner in `-es` is the correct
> form.**

⚠⚠ **AND THE REASON IS THAT THE GENITIVE ARGUMENT DOES NOT TRANSFER.**
The rule on final syllables migrates **the noun's THEMATIC ending**,
`*-as`, on a precise proof: *OE writes `dæges` and not †`deges`, so the
ending contained no front vowel at the moment of the mutation.* **The
pronominal genitive is not that ending.** It is a separately inherited
formation: Goth. `þis`, OHG `des`, OS `thes`.

> **THE DISCRIMINANT IS OLD HIGH GERMAN, AND IT IS CLEAR.** OHG keeps `a`
> faithfully everywhere — it writes `tag`, `tages`. **Had the pronominal
> genitive carried `*-as`, it would say †`das`. It says `des`.** The `e`
> of the pronoun is therefore not a weakened `a`: it is original.

**The STRONG adjective follows the pronoun** — that is what "strong"
means: its inflection is pronominal. So `gōdes`, `rehtes`, `allares` do
not migrate either.

⚠ **Status: reconstructed, high confidence, and the bound is named.**
What would lift it: a rank (a) occurrence of the determiner's genitive
before a noun, in the Wachtendonck psalter. The kit has found none.

#### ⚠⚠ `ira` AND `iro` DO NOT DECLINE — added 2026-08-21

**These are FROZEN GENITIVES of the personal pronoun, not adjectives.**
`ira` (feminine sg. possessor), `iro` (plural possessor), `is`
(masc./neut. sg. possessor): **invariable, in every case and every gender
of the thing possessed.** *ira brōther*, *ira brōtherē*, *iro fader*.

⚠ **WHY THIS HAS TO BE WRITTEN**: `sīn` is an **adjective** and agrees
with the thing possessed — it has the table below, added because two
translators had had to extrapolate `sīnes`. **`ira` is three lines away
and has nothing**, while table ④ states "agreement is with the THING
POSSESSED" **without saying that this sentence holds for `sīn` alone**.
> **T, 47th round**: *"I would have written `ira brōther` without a
> moment's hesitation, because `ira` **looks** indeclinable. It was the
> question that made me look for the table — and find that it does not
> exist. Applied to `ira`, the line 'agreement is with the thing
> possessed' **demands an agreement no table supplies**."*
⚠ *Both translators wrote `ira brōther`, each declaring it a guess.
**They were right — but by luck.*** And T adds the maxim that condemns a
partial gesture: *"the kit learned the lesson on `sīn` and did not
generalise it to the possessive next door."*

#### ⚠ THE DECLENSION OF `sīn`, WHICH WAS MISSING ENTIRELY

**Both translators of the 34th round wrote `sīnes fader` — and `sīnes`
does not appear once in the kit.** They produced it by extrapolation,
both of them, and they were right. **One does not count on luck twice.**
`sīn` agrees with the THING POSSESSED, and declines strong:

| | masculine | neuter | feminine |
|---|---|---|---|
| nom. | **sīn** | **sīn** | **sīn** |
| acc. | **sīnan** | **sīn** | **sīna** |
| gen. | **sīnes** | **sīnes** | **sīnero** |
| dat. | **sīnemu** | **sīnemu** | **sīnero** |

⚠ *The feminine dative carried †`sīneru` here, against the generated register (`gōdero`), against `unsero`, and against the kit's own calibration text (`mit allero **mīnero** mundburdi`). Corrected 2026-08-23 after the 50th round: seven phrases of that text depended on it. — **Three authorities against one hand-written table.***
| pl. nom./acc. | **sīna** | **sīna** | **sīna** |

⚠ *A SINGLE plural, **with no split by gender** — the adjective register cites `sīna kind` (neuter) as proof. Corrected 2026-08-23.*
| pl. gen. / dat. | **sīnero** / **sīnēm** | | |

⚠ **A reminder, and it is another rule**: this table is the one for
agreement with the THING POSSESSED. The choice of `sīn` itself depends on
the POSSESSOR — `sīn` exists only for a **masculine or neuter singular**
possessor; a feminine possessor takes `ira`, plural `iro`. See the
`sīn`/`is` clause.


### The article, the demonstratives and the instrumental

**Article**: the/thiu/that; acc. thena/thia/that; gen. thes/thero/thes;
dat. themu/theru/themu; pl. thia, gen. thero, dat. thēm.
**Instrumental thiu** (m./n., frozen): bī thiu "therefore", aftar thiu
"after that", fon thiu "from that", mit thiu — declared 2026-08-04: the
table did not give it although three lemmas lived on it.
**Unstressed doublet then** = thēm (dat. pl.) AND thena (acc. m.) in
unstressed position. ⚠⚠ **ANAPHORA — ruling recast 2026-08-19, double
enquiry on Behaghel 1897.** The old wording said "the bare article,
NEVER the demonstrative". **The tendency was right; the prohibition was
too strong; and above all it masked the slot that was really missing.**

⚠⚠ **THE MISSING SLOT: `the` IS USED ALONE, WITH NO NOUN.** Both
enquiries open with it, and say my question was badly put:
> *"The relevant opposition is not article / demonstrative, but
> **adjectival / substantival**. `the` is not 'the article': it is a
> demonstrative which, used alone, is **the ordinary anaphoric pronoun of
> the language** — the ancestor of pronominal German* der/die/das*."*

**This is the "this one" that two translators looked for in vain**, and
it was among the kit's forms without being declared. Behaghel §59 files
`the` among the pronouns used *"sowohl substantivisch als
adjectivisch"*; §123: *"Die anaphorische Bedeutung ist allgemein
verbreitet."*
> *Heliand* 4609–4611 — "see to whom I here give of my food into his hand
> before these men: **that one** (`the`) has treachery at heart …
> `the` will deliver me into the power of murderers". *The same bare
> demonstrative, twice.* The running subject is
> "I", and the demonstrative used alone **switches to another referent**,
> with no noun repeated and no gesture.
> Also 266 (*The skal Heliand te namon aigan*), 764, 2217, 4708, 1349.
**Forms**: those of the article — `the/thiu/that`, acc. `thena/thia/
that`, gen. `thes/thero/thes`, dat. `themu/theru/themu`, pl. `thia`,
gen. `thero`, dat. `thēm`. **No new form.**

**`these` remains the PROXIMAL, and the corrected rule holds in two
points:**
· **it is deictic by rule** — what is pointed at, or the speaker's *hic
  et nunc*: *these werold* "this world here", *thit leuht* "this life".
  Behaghel §123: *"die deiktische Function ist die Regel"*;
· ⚠ **but anaphora by `these` exists, seven times, and it is not a
  gesture** — above all in the **neuter resuming something SAID**: "that,
  you must all know" (2434); and with the noun repeated: "at these signs
  I have told you here" (4344), "those words shall come to pass" (1967,
  3919). ⚠ **Lines vouched for by their NUMBER, not by their letter —
  rule of 2026-08-19, which these two rulings had broken.**
  **Corrected rule: `these` resumes what has just been SAID, rarely what
  has just been SPOKEN OF.**

⚠⚠ **AND `these` IS SUBSTANTIVISED ONLY IN THE NEUTER** — Behaghel §59:
*"thesa ist nur ganz selten substantivisch verwendet; nur das Neutrum
thit und dessen Instrumental thius begegnen öfters."* **So there is no
†`these` as an independent masculine pronoun and no independent feminine
†`thius`.** *My prohibition kept me from that fault — but for a bad
reason, and it hid the real solution from me.*

**PARADIGM OF THE PROXIMAL, completed 2026-08-19** (`thesumu` and
`thesaro` were missing):

| | masc. | neuter | fem. | plural |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **nom.** | these | **thit** | thius | thesa |
| **acc.** | thesan | **thit** | thesa | thesa |
| **gen.** | theses | theses | thesaro | thesaro |
| **dat.** | thesumu | thesumu | thesaru | thesun |
| **instr.** | thīs ~ **thius** | — | — | — |

⚠ **TWO DEGREES, NOT THREE — and one does not add a distal.** Both
enquiries searched for `gene`, `jena`, `genes` in the 5,983 lines and in
Wachtendonck: **no occurrence** (85 % and 88 %). Proto-Germanic had
`*jainaz` and Middle Dutch has `gene`, but **neither of the first two
ranks carries it**: to introduce it would be to create a slot my best
witnesses ignore. **The distal is made analytically** — `the thār` "that
one", `the genouwar` "that one over there", attested *Heliand* 4958
(*thes the thar genouwar stēd*, Peter's denial: one points at a distance
across the courtyard).

⚠ **THE COLLISION `man` "man" / `man` "one" IS REAL, AND THE LANGUAGE
OWNS IT.** Behaghel §69: *"man = Mann, Mensch **und** die Menschen, woher
das indefinite Pronomen"* — **it is not another word.** Old Saxon has no
other form (95 %), and the *Heliand* sets them one line apart
(1868–1869). **So nothing is forged**; the three features that separate
them are recorded, and they are facts of use:
① **agreement** — the impersonal requires the **singular**;
② **position** — the impersonal is **unstressed and glued to the finite
   verb**, never separated from it by a heavy phrase, very often in a
   subordinate clause introduced by `that`;
③ **determination** — the impersonal **never** carries an article, an
   adjective, a demonstrative or a genitive. As soon as you read `the
   man`, `ain man`, `gōd man`, it is the **noun**.
⚠ And the enquiry adds this, which dissolves the problem: †*the man,
themu man gibid* was indeed unreadable — **but because of the prohibition
on the free relative, not because of `man`.** With the free relative
restored, there is no longer any need to set up a `man` head where the
source language sets up none.
**Near demonstrative** — ⚠⚠ **THIS PARAGRAPH IS SUPERSEDED BY THE
PROXIMAL TABLE, higher up in this same section** (2026-08-19). The forms
it carried — †`thesemu`, †`theseru`, †`thesero`, †`thesēm` — diverged
from the table in **four slots out of six**, and **both translators of
the 39th round reported it separately**. The table prevails: it is backed
by counts in the Saxon corpus (`thesumu` 62 occ., `thesaru` 43,
`thesaro` 66, `thesun` 28).
  ⚠ **I laid the table down that same evening, presenting it as the
  filling of a gap — and I did not sweep the paragraph it replaced, fifty
  lines away.** That is word for word the defect I had diagnosed that
  morning: *a migration that searches for the WORDS of the abrogated rule
  misses the paragraphs that say the same thing differently.* I committed
  it on the day I wrote it.


### The noun — the declension classes

**Model noun (a-stem)**: dag, gen. **dagas**, dat. **dagē**, instr. **dagu**; pl.
**dagā**, **dagō**, dagum. Feminine (ō): nom. **gebu** ~ acc. geba, dat. gebu; pl. **gebā**, **gebōnō** ~ **gebō**, **gebōm**.
Weak (n): Franko, obliques AND nom./acc. plural Frankon (-on everywhere
except the nom. sg. — see the ruling **"THE WEAK OBLIQUES ARE NOT
APPORTIONED BY GENDER"** (2026-08-17), in this same section. ⚠ **Until
2026-08-19 this pointer led to the rule that ruling REVOKED.**); weak
neuters: herta "heart", auga "eye" (pl. augon), dative themu hertan.
i-stem: gast, pl. gesti (L5).

### ⚠⚠ THE DECLENSION CLASS — four classes that had no name

**Brought in 2026-08-17.** The lexicon marked `[n] [i] [ja] [jō] [nd]`
and left **613 nouns out of 781 unmarked**, under a tacit convention:
*unmarked = `-a` stem* (masculine, neuter) or *`-ō` stem* (feminine).

> **THAT CONVENTION WAS FALSE FOR AN UNKNOWN PART OF THE LEXICON, AND IT
> IS WHAT BLOCKED THREE MIGRATIONS OF ENDINGS.** `sunu` and `fehu` are
> textbook `-u` stems and were not marked; the abstracts in `-skepi` are
> `-ja` stems.
> **A genitive migrated blind would have given †*sunuas*.**

⚠⚠ **`hraban` WAS REMOVED FROM THIS LIST ON 2026-08-21, AND THE
CORRECTION IS INTERNAL — it needs no witness.** This line gave it as an
`-n` stem and daggered †*hrabnas*; **the table of the anaptyctic vowel,
nine hundred lines above, writes `hraban`/`hrabnas` in bold, as the
correct form.** Both translators of the 44th round stumbled on it — it
was **the first word of their first sentence**.
  ⚠ **The argument that settles it is structural, and it is translator
  N's**: *"a weak masculine `[n]` would give a nominative `*hrabo`, which
  the kit writes nowhere."* An `-n` stem does not make its nominative in
  `-an`. **`hraban` is the regular outcome of `*hrabnaz` by anaptyxis** —
  an `-a` stem, exactly like `akkar` &lt; `*akraz` and `wintar` &lt;
  `*wintraz`, its neighbours in the same table. ⚠ And the dictionary, the
  declared source of truth, carries `hraban (m.)` **with no class mark**,
  that is `[a]` by the reading key. *Two instruments out of three said the
  same thing; it was the prose that stood alone.*
    ⚠⚠ **WHAT THIS CASE REVEALS AND NO REGISTER CAN SEE** — translator N
    puts it: *"the conflict is about the CLASS, not about the ending, so
    none of the generated tables can see it. A register that counts 89
    slots of the noun cannot see this one: it is not in a slot, it is in
    the ASSIGNMENT OF A LEMMA TO A CLASS."* **A register of lemmas whose
    class is asserted twice is missing** — the fourth called for in two
    rounds, after the one for unsettled uses.

**Of twenty-five lemmas listed for the genitive migration, SIX were not
`-a` stems** — one in four.


#### The four classes that were missing

| mark | class | lemmas | what grounds it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`[ō]`** | `*-ō` stem **marked explicitly** | `mulīn` | ⚠ **brought in 2026-08-17**: the mark was in use in the dictionary and absent from this list. It serves when the FORM does not show the class — a feminine that does not end in `-a` is not an `-ō` stem by default, but may be one by borrowing from a Latin word in `-a` (*molina*), the nominative being apocopated |
| **`[u]`** | `*-uz` stem | `sunu`, `fehu`, `widu`, `medu`, `lagu`, `frithu` | ⚠ **the kit already knew the thematic vowel** — it writes `*hand-u-maz → handum` — **without ever naming the class** |
| **`[wa]`** | `*-waz` stem | `saiw`, `snaiw`, `ehu`, `balu` | ⚠ **not to be confused with `[u]`**: `ehu` "horse" is `*ehwaz`, not †`*ehuz` |
| **`[r]`** | kinship nouns | `fader`, `mōder`, `brōther`, `swester`, `dohter` | a declension of its own, inherited from Indo-European |
| **`[rac]`** | root nouns, consonantal | `fōt`, `man`, `naht`, `tand` | ⚠⚠ **TWO INDEPENDENT FEATURES, and NOT "mutated plural"** (ruling of 2026-08-21): **(i) dat. sg. with ZERO ending** — rank (a): `naht` 4 times out of 5, `burg` 5 out of 9; **(ii) UNMARKED plural** — `man`, `naht`. ⚠ **`duri` withdrawn**: it is a feminine `-i` stem, often *pluralia tantum*; the root noun there is an Indo-European state, not a Germanic one |

⚠ **`duri` was first misclassified** by the automatic sort — a feminine
in `-i` normally goes to `[ī]`, but this one is a **root noun**
(OE *duru*). *A sort by form goes wrong on the words that have no regular
form: that is their definition.*

#### What the marking produced

**75 lemmas marked**, of which 43 in `[ja]` — the 16 agent nouns in
`-āri` and the neuters in `-i` — and 7 in **`[ī]`**, the deadjectival
abstract type that the abstract double grid **rehabilitated** on
2026-08-16: `audmōdi`, `daupi`, `wirdi`, `wraithi`, `stolti`, `menigi`.

**State, recounted at every generation: 349 nouns marked out of
984.** The other **635** take the default, and **the default is a
RULING, not a silence.**

> ⚠⚠ **THESE THREE NUMBERS WERE WRITTEN BY HAND, AND STALE TWICE OVER.**
> The kit carried *"244 out of 780"*, then, twenty lines lower, *"257 out
> of 784"* — two counts of the same object, both false, the lexicon then
> carrying 295 out of 892. **They are now computed by the generator.**
> *A number written by hand in a living document does not age: it lies,
> and quietly.*

#### ⚠ WHAT REMAINS UNMARKED, AND WHY WE STOP THERE

⚠ The unmarked ones **have not been checked one by one**. The marking
covered only what can be deduced from **FORM and GENDER without chaining
rules** — golden rule no. 21. A feminine in `-a` may be an `-ō` stem or a
weak `-n` stem: form does not separate them. *The mark says what is
known; its absence does not say what is unknown.*

| what remains | n | the default there is |
|---|---|---|
| **masculines ending in a consonant** | 215 | `-a` stem — **generally right**, a few `-i` stems hide there (`gast` was one, it is marked) |
| **feminines in `-a`** | 151 | `-ō` stem — **generally right**, but weak feminines also end in `-a` and **only eleven are marked `[n]`** |
| **neuters ending in a consonant** | 146 | `-a` stem — **the safest of the three** |
| **feminines ending in a consonant** | 10 | ⚠ **the default is FALSE there by construction** — see below |
| neuters ending in a vowel | 5 | to be examined |

> ⚠⚠ **A FEMININE THAT DOES NOT END IN `-a` IS NOT AN `-ō` STEM.**
> Twenty lemmas were in this case. **Ten have been marked** — they are the
> abstracts in `*-tiz` / `*-þiz` (`anst`, `thurft`, `gamund`, `gathuld`,
> `dugunth`, `angust`, `gawald`, `abunst`, `frist`), **that is, precisely
> the series the abstract double grid designated as the NATIVE STOCK of
> Germanic**, plus `kuningin` in `[jō]`.

#### ⚠⚠ Six candidate root nouns, left PENDING, and deliberately so

**`bōk`, `lernbōk`, `miluk`, `kū`, `mūs`, `lūs`** are root nouns in
Germanic — OE *bōc/bēc*, *mūs/mȳs*, *lūs/lȳs*, *cū/cȳ*, with a mutated
plural and no thematic vowel.

**They are not marked, and the kit itself holds the precedent that
forbids it**: for "sow" it **set aside the root noun `sū`** in favour of
the derivative **`suga`**, because *"rank (b) keeps the root noun and
rank (a) knows only the `-ō` derivative — that is not a silence, it is a
choice"*.

> **The same question arises for these six, and it calls for an enquiry
> at rank (a), not a deduction.** Modern Dutch has levelled everything in
> `-en` (*boeken, muizen, luizen, koeien*): it will say nothing. Middle
> Dutch is needed.
> ⚠ **And there is a second question, phonological**: the mutation of `ō`
> and `ū` calls for front rounded vowels. **To mark a class one does not
> know how to write would be to declare it without giving it** — the very
> fault just retracted on `[u]`, `[wa]`, `[r]` and `[rac]`.

#### What cannot be mechanised, and it must be said

**Telling an `-a` stem from an `-i` stem ending in a consonant requires
the etymon, lemma by lemma.** No form separates them: `dag` and `gast`
look alike, and only `*gastiz` against `*dagaz` decides.

> **361 lemmas were in this case when it was counted. It is not
> sweeping work, it is dictionary work**
> ⚠ **THE FIGURE IS DATED AND NOT RECOMPUTABLE**, audited
> 2026-09-03. It is written in the present tense and nothing
> recounts it; worse, **the sentence does not name its population
> closely enough to be recounted at all** — the lexicon today
> carries 635 nouns with no class mark, 432 masculine or neuter,
> 262 masculine, and none of the three is 361. The number is kept
> as the measurement it was, and marked as such.
>   *An undated figure in the present tense is a claim. A dated
>    one is a measurement. Only the second can stop being true
>    without becoming a lie.* — and the only honest way is an enquiry at rank (a)
> in batches, or a consultation of Seebold and Kroonen.
>
> *The mark says what is known; its absence does not say what is
> unknown.*

#### ⚠⚠ RETRACTION — the paradigms were NOT missing

**I had written here that the kit did not document the classes `[u]`,
`[wa]`, `[r]` and `[rac]`, and that their oblique slots needed an
enquiry. A translator of the 32nd round checked: THAT IS FALSE. The kit
gives all of them.**

**All are declined in full — `[u]`, `[wa]`, `[r]`, `[rac]`, `[i]`,
`[ja]`, `[jō]`, `[nd]`, the weak ones — under the heading "THE PARADIGMS
OF EVERY CLASS", in this same section.** ⚠ This table carried a
COPY of them; it is reduced to the pointer on 2026-08-17. *A copy of a
paradigm goes out of date independently of its original: that is how the kit
came to carry `strawes` in one place and `strawas` in the other.*

⚠⚠ **AND THE FALSE DECLARATION COST SOMETHING.** The second translator
writes: *"A declared gap makes you give up; an unspoken gap makes you
invent. This one is declared, and it worked as it should — I gave up on
`frithu` and on `sunu`, whose obliques I would have needed."*

> **He gave up on two words the kit knows how to decline.** A declared
> gap makes you give up — **including when the gap does not exist.** A
> false declaration of absence does not leave the system as it was: **it
> takes from it a capacity it had.**

**What was missing was not the paradigms: it was the POINTER.** They lie
scattered through the prose, none under the heading of its class. *"The
repair is a pointer, not an enquiry."* Here it is, above.

⚠⚠ **AND THIS BLOCK CONTRADICTED ITSELF, TWENTY-FIVE LINES LOWER.** It
opened with *"THAT IS FALSE, the kit gives all of them"* and closed with
*"it does not document those in `-u`, `-wa`, `-r`, nor the root nouns"* —
the retracted sentence had outlived its own retraction. **Lifted
2026-08-17.**

> **`kontrar` did not see it, and the reason is instructive**: the two
> statements share no key word. One says *"gives all of them"*, the other
> *"does not document"*. The control matches TERMS; **a contradiction may
> have no term in common.**

⚠ What remains true after the lifting: the class `[wa]` is **entirely
decided** — it is an `-a` stem and the rule on endings covers it
(migrated that day). The classes `[u]`, `[i]`, `[r]`, `[rac]` are
**declined but NOT migrated, and deliberately**: their endings do not
come from `*-as`/`*-ai`. See the bound of the rule on endings.
> **The class is named, the paradigm is given, its migration is refused
> with its reason.** *Three states, not two: "documented" and "up to
> date" are not the same word.*


### ⚠⚠ THE CITATION FORM OF THE FEMININES — reading the dictionary without going wrong

**Reported by a translator of the 32nd round**, and he is right:

> *"The paradigm says nom. `gebu` ~ acc. `geba`. The dictionary cites ALL
> its `-ō` stems in the `-a` form, that is in the ACCUSATIVE. `saga`,
> `werra`, `aira`, `māta` are accusatives presented as nominatives. **The
> first feminine nominative written from the dictionary will be wrong.**"*

> ### READING RULE: THE DICTIONARY CITES THE STEM, NOT THE NOMINATIVE.
> A feminine cited in **`-a`** and **unmarked** is an `-ō` stem. Its
> **nominative singular is in `-u`**: `aira` → nom. **`airu`** · `saga` →
> **`sagu`** · `māta` → **`mātu`** · `werra` → **`werru`**.
> · The accusative, genitive and dative keep the cited form:
> acc. `aira`, **gen. `aira`**, dat. `airu`.
> · ⚠⚠ **THIS LINE CARRIED `gen. airā` WITH A LONG VOWEL**, against the § on classes
> which gives `nom./acc./GEN. sg. -a` short a hundred and twenty lines
> below. **BOTH translators of the 35th round stumbled on it,
> separately.** Arbitrated for the SHORT on 2026-08-18, and the arbiter
> comes from that day's double enquiry on the genitive of the `[jō]`:
> **Braune §207 Anm. 3** admits the long form but writes *"es fehlt
> jedoch ein directes zeugniss"*. **Two statements out of three already
> said short; the third rested on a length its own author declares
> unproven.**
> · ⚠ **A feminine marked `[n]` is an exception**: the weak stem does have
> its nominative in **`-a`** — `sunna`, `tunga` are correct nominatives.
> Eleven lemmas are in this case, and they are marked.

#### ⚠ The 151 entries are NOT migrated, and here is why

**The kit itself declares this slot UNDECIDABLE**: *"the handbooks do not
cleanly refer OHG *geba* / *gebu* and OE *giefe* to a single etymon"*,
and the opposition `gebu` ~ `geba` is classed **[C] conjectured** by both
enquirers on the endings.

> **To rewrite a hundred and fifty-one lemmas on a slot declared
> undecidable would be to build a migration on a conjecture** — precisely
> what golden rule no. 21 forbids. **One makes the rule legible; one does
> not move the lexicon.**

⚠ **And a nominative in `-a` is not a hard fault.** The levelling of the
opposition is **attested in both continental witnesses**: Old High German
and Old Saxon both write `geba` in the nominative. A translator who
writes `Ēra is gōd` produces **the levelled state, that is the state of
the 9th century** — the same fault of date as `dages` for `dagas`,
neither more nor less grave.
> **`-u` is the kit's form; `-a` is tolerated as a levelled variant, and
> we know what it costs.**


#### ⚠⚠ THE PARADIGMS OF EVERY CLASS — they are here, and the title hid them

> **This section carries the complete declension of `[i]`, `[u]`, `[jō]`,
> `[ja]`, `[nd]`, `[r]`, the root nouns, the weak nouns and the neuters.
> It used to be headed *"What the survey turned up into the bargain"* —
> and it is the most consulted matter in the kit.**

⚠⚠ **RETITLED 2026-08-17, AND THAT TITLE HAD COST TWICE** — it is what
explains the retraction above: I declared missing paradigms the kit was
giving, a translator of the 32nd round found them, and another had
abandoned `frithu` and `sunu`. **Three people were reading the same
document.**

> *The matter was present, exact — and **unfindable**, which, for a kit
> handed alone to a translator, comes to the same thing as absent.* **The
> first fault of a grammar is not being wrong: it is being right where
> nobody looks.**


The defect is not only a convention of citation: **it is that the cited
form does not tell the class.** A feminine in `-a` may be an `-ō` stem or
a weak `-n` stem, and **only the second carries a mark.** The first is
recognised by default — that is, by a silence.

> *A convention of citation that cannot be told from a real form is a
> delayed trap: it shows only at the moment somebody declines the word.*
> **Here it took thirty-two rounds.**

**Neuter a-stems**: pl. nom./acc. = sg. (word, pl. word; jār, pl. jār).
**i-stems**: pl. gesti (L5), gen. gestiō, dat. **gestim**.
⚠⚠ **THE *-i-* OF *gestim* IS NOT AN ENDING, IT IS THE THEMATIC VOWEL**
(instruction of 2026-08-14). Germanic has only ONE dative plural ending,
\*-maz, added to the stem: \*dag-a-maz → *dagum*, \*gast-i-maz →
*gastim*, \*hand-u-maz → *handum*. **So there is nothing to level: the
distinction is inherited automatically.** It is confirmed by two branches
with no contact — Goth. *gastim*, OHG *gestim* — and **attested at rank
(a)**: *an **creftin** unsin* (Wachtendonck psalter, Ps 59:12, *in
virtutibus nostris*), *an **misdâdin** iro* (Ps 67:22). In that corpus the
distribution **-in** (i-stems) against **-on/-un** (all the others) is
**without exception inside the dative plural**.
⚠ **IN THE FRANCONIAN AREA, HIGH AND LOW, THIS DISTINCTION WAS NEVER
LEVELLED MORPHOLOGICALLY** — it was erased PHONETICALLY in the 10th/11th
c., when every unstressed vowel merged into schwa. Analogical levelling
(OS *gastiun*, OE *-um* throughout) is an **Ingvaeonic and Saxon fact,
not a Frankish one**. At 550 the distinction is alive.
⚠⚠ **`hand` — THE TWO TRANSLATORS OF THE 34th ROUND DIVERGED ON ITS
DATIVE SINGULAR, AND THE KIT SAID THREE THINGS.** One wrote *an
**hendi***, the other *an **handu***. **The first is right, and all three
statements were true — of three different SLOTS.**
> **`hand` is a `-u` stem by its ETYMON and an `-i` stem by its
> INFLECTION at 550.** West Germanic transferred its oblique singular to
> the `-i` stems: OHG `hant`, gen./dat. **`henti`**; OS gen./dat.
> **`hendi`**. **The dative PLURAL, however, stayed thematic in `-u`**:
> `handum`, not †`hendim` — and it was of that slot, and that slot alone,


| slot | form |
|---|---|
| nom./acc. sg. | **hand** |
| **gen. and dat. sg.** | **`hendi`** — mutated, like `stad`/`stedi` |
| nom./acc. pl. | **hendi** |
| gen. pl. | **hendiō** |
| **dat. pl.** | **`handum`** — ⚠ thematic in `-u`, NO mutation |

⚠ **DO NOT FILE `hand` UNDER `[u]` FOR THE OBLIQUE SINGULAR** — the
feature of class `[u]` is *"dat. sg. IDENTICAL to the nominative"*, which
would give †`handu`. **That is false for this word**: OHG and OS both
give the mutation. The `-u` of `handum` is the thematic vowel of the
dative plural, not the mark of a living class.
> *A word may belong to one class by its etymon and to another by its
> inflection. Marking it with a single letter forces a choice, and the kit
> had chosen the wrong slot to carry the mark.* **Dat. sg.**: `-i`
mutating on an `a` root (stad → stedi, kraft → krefti, maht → mehti),
zero ending on naht, `-i` without mutation elsewhere (unhugd → unhugdi).
⚠ **GEN. SG. OF THE `-i` FEMININES: IDENTICAL TO THE DATIVE** — *thero
**stedi***, *thero **krefti***, *thero **hendi***, *thero **mehti***.
The slot was missing: the kit gave the dat. sg., the nom./acc. pl., the
gen. and dat. pl., **never the genitive singular**. BOTH translators of
the 29th round reported it, one writing it by alignment on the dative,
the other **recasting his sentence so as not to have to produce it** —
which is the surest symptom of a hole in a paradigm.
⚠⚠ **THE `-i` MASCULINES FOLLOW THE a-STEM IN THE SINGULAR — AND HENCE
`-as` / `-ē`, NOT †`-es` / †`-e`** (ruling of 2026-08-21, double enquiry).
> ***gast*, gen. `gastas`, dat. `gastē`.**

**What comparison confirms is the PATTERN, not the LETTER.** The
convergence of the i-masculines on the `-a` stem is real and unanimous —
Gothic `gastis` as early as the 4th century, Old Norse `gests`, the four
West Germanic branches without exception. *"A change made independently
by Gothic, Norse and West Germanic is an easy and early change"*:
accomplished by 550, rated **85 %**.
  ⚠⚠ **But `-es` / `-e` are the endings of the a-stem IN THE 9th CENTURY,
  after reduction.** This line therefore carried, word for word, *"the
  entry 'i-Stämme, Maskulina' of the Old High German handbooks"* — and an
  enquirer says so without softening: ***"this is exactly the breach your
  rank (c) rule forbids: you took the SENSE AND the LETTER."***
    *A class that "follows" another must follow it in the state that
    other class is in at the target date. The pointer had been written
    once and had not followed the migration of its target.*
  ⚠ The second enquirer closes every way out: **there is no scenario in
  which †`gastes` is correct at 550** — either the convergence is done,
  and it is `-as` / `-ē`; or it is not, and it is `-ī` / `-ī`. †`-es` is
  neither.


⚠ **THE `-i` FEMININES, HOWEVER, DID NOT CONVERGE** — and that is where
the real class feature lies. `maht`, `stad`, `kraft` keep the proper
ending: gen. and dat. sg. in **`-ī`**. Both enquiries say so separately
(90 % each), and Old High German writes it in black and white: *"the
masculines became identical with the `-a` nouns in the singular, while
**the feminines preserved the original declension**"*. **The kit already
carried it correctly**; it was the enquiry's commission that had given
them as masculine, and both enquirers corrected the question before
answering it.

⚠ **TWO SLOTS THIS RULING OPENS WITHOUT CLOSING**, and both enquiries
raise them separately:
· **APOCOPE** — the kit writes the nominative `gast`, apocopated, whereas
  Proto-West-Germanic has `*gasti`. If law L8 ("full final vowels at
  550") holds for short `-i` after a heavy syllable too, the nominative
  ought to be `gasti`. *"What you cannot do is apocopate the nominative
  and keep reduced endings in the genitive: those are two chronological
  layers stacked in one paradigm."* **Rated 50/50. Not settled.**
· **UMLAUT IN THE PLURAL** — writing `gesti` rather than `gastī` imports
  the letter of rank (c), the mutation being at best allophonic at 550
  and written nowhere before the 8th century. ⚠ **The *Heliand* itself
  wavers**: `gastiun` (ms. M) against `gestion` (ms. C), **in the same
  line 2749**. Rated a "real problem" at 75 %. **Not settled.**

⚠ **And a rank (a bis) attestation supports the `-as`**, which the kit
already cited for another reason: the Bergakker inscription (Betuwe,
425–475), ***haþuþywas*** — masculine genitive singular in **`-as`**, on
Frankish soil, **two generations before the target date**.
  **alothes**, tehan skok **garbōnō**;
· **genitive of price** after werth: tehan penningō werth;
· **the sanction formula**: "geldē hē N **skillingō**" keeps its genitive
  plural at any number — a frozen idiom of the penal clause, not an
  application of the rule for the tens.
**ō-stems**: nom./acc./GEN. sg. -a (thero wahsunga "of the growth"),
DAT. sg. -u (theru wahsungu "to the growth").
**`-u` stems** [u]: sg. nom./acc. sunu, gen. sunes, **dat. sunu
(IDENTICAL to the nominative — that is the class's own feature)**;
pl. nom./acc. suni, gen. sunō, dat. sunum. ⚠⚠ **THIS LINE IS WRITTEN
ON A SHORT STEM AND SAYS NOTHING OF A LONG ONE** (`ford`, `hand`,
`flōd`), added 2026-09-06 after the 83rd round wrote `an themu ford`
as a "formally invisible" dative. **Both enquirers refuse the bare
form: it is the shape of c. 1200, not of 550, and the dative carries
a vowel.** Which vowel is DIVERGENT and registered, not averaged: one
keeps the inherited dative after the long stem — `fordō`, `handō`,
55 %/60 %, because the long `-au` > `-ō` survives apocope exactly as
the ō-stem dative `-u` does after a long stem (`theru wahsungu`
above); the other gives the a-stem transfer `forde` (55 %) and the
i-stem `handi` (65 %), because `-iu` > `-i` after a long syllable is
the same apocope that made the lemma `ford`. Both name the witness
that would decide: a pre-800 charter or a Heliand line with a
long-stem dative in `-u`/`-o`. Until then write `fordō` or `forde`
and declare which; never `ford`.
⚠ **READ 2026-09-07**: the five texts have no long-stem dative at all — no *ford* as a noun, no *hand* in
the dossier; the short stem gives `-u` every time, Hel. 774 *an friðu*,
Otf. 019.15 and 056.18 *mit fridu*, and **no u-stem dative in `-e`
exists anywhere in them**. That does not decide the long stem; it takes
from the a-stem transfer its only analogy in the record (both readers
65–70 %). Neuters of the same
inflection: fehu, gen. fehes, **dat. fehu, pl. nom./acc. fehu** (the
neuter does not mark its plural, as no neuter does). So too frithu, medu,
lagu; and ford, which has lost its `-u` in the nominative but keeps
dat. = nom.: themu ford. **Outside the class: alu**, a `-þ-` stem
(gen. alothes, dat. alothe).
**Kinship nouns in `-r`** (fader, mōder, brōther, dohter, swester) —
e.g. *mīnemu **fader*** "to my father", *Ansoberht is **brōther***
"Ansoberht his brother", pl. *thia **fadera*** "the fathers". — settled
2026-07-24, carried into the kit 2026-08-07: **singular INVARIABLE in
every case** (mīnemu fader, sīnemu fader — the dative in `-e` of the day-1
grammar is demoted to an archaic variant); plural fadera / faderō /
faderum.
⚠⚠ **`-nd` stems** (participial: fīand, wīgant, weriand) — **REDONE
2026-08-21, and all four slots were wrong or unmigrated.**

| slot | at 550 | why |
|---|---|---|
| **gen. sg.** | **`-as`** | remade on the `-a` stem, and **early**: OE `frēondes`, and rank (a) attests it — `fiundes` (Ps 54:4), `fiundis` (Ps 60:4; 63:2). ⚠ †`-es` was the reduced form of the 9th c. |
| **dat. sg.** | ⚠⚠ **`-i` ~ `-ē`, TWO COMPETING ENDINGS** | **this is the one place where both enquiries refuse to decide**, and they say so separately. Old English keeps the INHERITED dative with mutation — ***frīend*** — *"and that mutation can only come from a `*-i` still present at the time of the umlaut"*. Rated `-i` 55 % against `-ē` 40 %. †`-e` is wrong **on either hypothesis** |
| **nom./acc. pl.** | **`-i`** (or the apocopated form) | ⚠ †`-a` is **the innovation**, not the state of 550: early OHG has `friunt` **with no ending**, OE `frīend`, PWG `*friundi`. Rank (a) does write `fiunda` — **but in the 10th century**. Rated 75 % |
| **gen. pl.** | **`-ō`** | ⚠ †`-o` was simply **unmigrated**, as `dagō` was on 2026-08-17 |
| **dat. pl.** | `-um` | ✓ unchanged, matching PWG `*friundum` |


⚠ **The MIXED paradigm is a result, not an embarrassment**: the genitive
is remade everywhere and early, the dative keeps its archaism. *OHG shows
that the class was still losing its archaisms DURING the documented
period — so in 550 it had more of them, not fewer.*

⚠⚠ **A WARNING ON METHOD, given by both enquirers separately**: the Old
Saxon tables circulating online give †`gasties` / †`gastie` for the
i-masculines. **That is an artefact of a template, not an attestation** —
the page itself carries the note "needs documentation". *A table produced
by a model has the look of a witness and is not one.* E.g. *thesa
**fīanda** quāmun* "these enemies came", *fon thēm **wīgantum***
"by the warriors".
**FEMININES IN [jō] — BARE NOM. SG., OBLIQUES IN `-ia`.** Ten lemmas:
*bruggia*, *heggia*, *happia*, *krippia*, *sundia*, *sunnia*, *smithia*,
*skūria*, *laubia*, *ambahtia*. The dictionary cites them in the OBLIQUE
form, which is the one Merovingian Latin transmits.
· nom. sg.: **bare stem** — *brugg*, *hegg*, *happ*, *kripp*, *sund*,
  *sunn*, *smith*, *skūr*, **lauf**, *ambaht*;
· acc. and gen. sg.: **-ia** — *thia bruggia*, *thero bruggia*;
· dat. sg.: **-iu** — *theru bruggiu*;
· plural: **merged with the ō-stems** — *brugga*, gen. *bruggōnō*, dat.
  *bruggum*.
⚠ ***laubia* gives *lauf*, not †*laub***: L10 hardens the labial spirant
in absolute final position and keeps it voiced before a vowel. It is the
alternation of *gaf* ~ *geban*, applied to a noun.
⚠ **TWO HOMOGRAPHS CREATED, AND THE ARTICLE SEPARATES THEM**: *thiu
smith* "the smithy" against *the smith* "the smith"; *thiu ambaht* "the
maidservant" against *that ambaht* "the service". Gender is enough, as in
German *die Schmiede* ~ *der Schmied*.
⚠ **THE *skuldbok* CARRIES BOTH HOMOGRAPHS ON THE SAME PAGE**, and
carried them before the rule: *that ambaht thes abbates* "the abbot's
service" three lines above *thiu ambaht Rathild* "the maidservant
Rathild". **The article alone holds them apart, and it suffices.**
⚠⚠ **PROMULGATED 2026-08-16; THE SLOT HAD BEEN DECLARED UNDECIDABLE THE
DAY BEFORE.** The stem was proven, the ending not. Four pieces settled
it, and **the first was already in the kit**:
· ⚠ the kit carried this paradigm on the **feminine anthroponyms** —
  *Adalhild*, *Hrōdhild* with apocopated nominative, obliques
  *Adalhilda*, *Adalhildu*. But *-hild* IS a \*-jō-. Writing *bruggia* in
  the nominative while writing *Adalhild* made **two rules for one
  class**;
· the *Lex Salica*, at the one place where the Latin bears on a
  NOMINATIVE: ***sunnis* is almost always SUBJECT** ("si eum sunnis non
  tenuerit", "et eum **aliqua** sunnis non tenuerit", where the
  determiner confirms the feminine), and *sunnia* is NEVER subject there,
  while *scuria* and *ambascia* are objects. **The distribution follows
  syntactic function**;
· **Franck §139**: every \*-jō- is HEAVY — the West Germanic gemination
  that proves the yod also makes the syllable heavy. The rule therefore
  holds for the whole class, **with no exception to declare**;
· the same latinisation in *-is* for the bare stems of the anthroponyms
  (*Brunichildis*, *Radegundis*), and OE *synn*, *hell*, *brycg*.
⚠ **THE PLURAL IS DEDUCED, NOT INSTRUCTED — AND IT IS DECLARED.** The kit
established at rank (a) that in the plural Low Franconian **merged the
ja- and the a-stems**, the yod surviving only in the direct singular. The
clause is extended to the jō- and the ō-stems, by symmetry of structure.
**That is a deduction, not a witness**: if an enquiry contradicts it, it
is the plural that moves, not the singular.


⚠⚠ **THE STEM ITSELF WAS MIGRATED AND RESTORED ON THE SAME DAY
(2026-08-15), AND IT IS THE MOST INSTRUCTIVE REVERSAL OF THE SERIES.** In
the morning I had written †*sunda* and †*smitha* on the argument that
Middle Dutch gives *sonde* and not †*sondie*. **A second double enquiry
demolished that argument, and the two enquirers by different routes:**
· the weakening of unstressed finals into schwa neutralises *-a*, *-ia*,
  *-u* and *-e* into a single *-e* — and **Old Saxon writes *sundia* and
  still ends at *sunde***: the test discriminates nothing;
· my one counter-example, *merrie*, falls in **the only environment where
  the yod could survive** — *r* is exempt from West Germanic gemination
  (Franck §55,2). Elsewhere the yod is CONSUMED by the geminate it
  produces, so *bruggia* could not give †*bruggie*;
· ⚠ and **Heyne §30 says the opposite of what I made him say**: "in den
  Psalmen ist es überall **untergegangen**" — the *j* HAS DISAPPEARED,
  which presupposes an earlier *-ia*. **Franck §55,1 dates the loss:
  "schwindet im Laufe des 9. Jhs."** At 550 the yod is alive.
**The independent proof is Romance, not graphic**: Gallo-Romance
palatalisation requires a yod at the moment of borrowing — *crèche* <
\*krippja, *hache* < \*happja, *loge* < \*laubja, *broigne* < \*brunja,
*haie* < \*hagja. None is possible from †*krippa*, †*hapa*, †*lauba*.
⚠ **WHAT THE LATIN DOES NOT PROVE ON ITS OWN**: the ending of the
OBLIQUE. Latin borrows in the object case (*si sunniam habuerit*), and
the accusative of a \*-jō- is \*-ja whatever the nominative —
***ambascia* shows it in the negative**: Latin makes it a feminine in
*-ia* although the underlying Germanic word \*ambahtja is **NEUTER**
(Goth. *andbahti*). A Latin *-ia* may therefore cover a neuter. **That is
why the nominative is proved elsewhere — and now it is.**
⚠⚠ **CLASS `[ja]` — ARBITRATION OF 2026-08-17, AND IT HAD ALREADY BEEN
GIVEN**

**84 lemmas carried `[ja]` with no certain oblique; the two translators
of the 33rd round had made it one of their three grey zones. The slot was
not empty: it was filled three times, differently.**

| what the kit was writing | where |
|---|---|
| †`beddies`, †`kunnies`, †`rīkies` — **with** `-i-` | ja-NEUTERS |
| gen. sg. `-es`, dat. sg. `-e` — **without** `-i-` | ja-MASCULINES |
| `bruggia`, `bruggiu` — **with** `-i-` | jō-FEMININES |

> **Three genders of one class, three rules.** This is exactly the fault
> the kit had named in order to settle the nominative of the `[jō]`:
> *"writing bruggia in the nominative while writing Adalhild made two
> rules for one class"*.

**THE ARBITER HAD EXISTED SINCE 2026-08-15 AND IT IS AT RANK (a).** The
double enquiry that abrogated `-io/-ium` concluded that **in the plural
Low Franconian merged the ja- and the a-stems, the `j` surviving only in
the nom./acc. singular.** The kit drew the plural from it, and the
singular of the MASCULINE. **It left the NEUTER as it stood — and
†`beddies`, †`kunnies`, †`rīkies` are word for word the forms Gallée §301
gives for OLD SAXON**, that is, the very source this enquiry had just set
aside. *The abrogation missed by one line the word it was aimed at.*


⚠ **Corroboration at rank (c), and it points the same way**: OHG gives
`rīhhi`/`rīhhes`, `kunni`/`kunnes`, `hirti`/`hirtes`, `wecki`/`weckes` —
**no `-i-` in the oblique**. It is Old Saxon that has it (`kunnies`,
`weggies`), and Old Saxon is what the enquiry had set aside. **Frenkisk
follows Low Franconian, not Saxon** — same arbitration, same day, same
source.

> **PROMULGATED: `[ja]` masculine AND neuter, gen. sg. `-as`, dat. sg.
> `-ē`, with no `-i-`.** `beddi/beddas/beddē` · `kunni/kunnas/kunnē` ·
> `rīki/rīkas/rīkē` · `wīti/wītas/wītē` · `hirti/hirtas/hirtē` ·
> `mulināri/mulināras/mulinārē`.
> ⚠ **The umlaut does not move**: it is carried by the GEMINATE (`weggi` <
> \*wagja-, `gg` < `gj`), not by the ending. Removing the oblique `-i-`
> does not undo `weggē`.

⚠⚠ **WHAT THIS ARBITRATION DOES NOT SWALLOW, AND IT IS DECLARED**: the
`[jō]` keep `bruggia`/`bruggiu`, that is the `-i-` in the oblique singular
— hence the opposite. Their paradigm was promulgated on 2026-08-16 by a
four-piece double enquiry, and **golden rule no. 21 forbids demolishing it
by chaining**. But the kit itself writes, in this same file, that *"the
Latin does not prove the ending of the OBLIQUE on its own"*: **the oblique
slot of the `[jō]` is therefore deduced as well.** That is the next
enquiry, and it is named.
> *A class whose masculine and neuter agree against its feminine is not
> settled: it is half settled, and that must be written.*

⚠⚠ **CLASS `[ī]` — INVARIABLE SINGULAR** (ruling of 2026-08-17). The
eight deadjectival abstracts in `-ī` — `audmōdi`, `daupi`, `wirdi`,
`wraithi`, `stolti`, `menigi` — **do not inflect in the singular**: `thiu
menigi`, `thero menigi`, `theru menigi`. OHG gives them as indeclinable
(`managī` in every case) and OS likewise; only Gothic inflects them as
`-ein-` stems (*managei / manageins*), and that is an eastern innovation.
⚠ **They are not normally put in the plural**: they are nouns of quality.
*The slot had been named for two days and was empty; eight lemmas
depended on it.*

⚠⚠ **THE WEAK OBLIQUES ARE NOT SPLIT BY GENDER** — slot reopened
2026-08-16, **settled 2026-08-17 on what the kit had already
established**. The pattern masc. `-on` / fem. `-un` / neut. `-an` is **Old
Saxon**; the Low Franconian corpus varies `-in ~ -on` **across the
genders** (masc. *herrin* AND *herron*, *namin* AND *namon*).
> **A corpus that varies across a distinction does not attest it: it
> refutes it.** *So one writes `-on` in every gender, with `-un` and `-in`
> as free variants, and stops looking for a rule the witness never
> carried.*


**Genitive and dative plural of the WEAK nouns** (`-n-` stems, all
three genders): gen. **-ono**, dat. **-on** (var. *-un*), **with no split
by gender**. ⚠ The pattern masc. *-on* / fem. *-un* / neut. *-an* that
the kit applies to the obliques of the SINGULAR is **Saxon, not Franconian**; the Low
Franconian corpus varies *-in ~ -on* across the genders (masc. *herrin*
and *herron*, *namin* and *namon*). **Slot reopened, not settled.**
⚠ And ***Frankōnō* is not attested at rank (a)**: the form is High German
(Otfrid, *ubar Frankōnō lant*; *Franconofurd* 794, Rhenish area) and Old
Saxon (*Frankena vord*). The *Pactus* says *gens Francorum* — **that is
Latin**, second declension. It is kept as a morphological pattern
**declared to be rank (c)**.
**ja-neuters**: beddi, **gen. beddas**, dat. **beddē** (likewise kunni,
gen. **kunnas**; rīki, gen. **rīkas**); **nom./acc. pl. = SINGULAR, with
no ending** — *rīki* "the kingdoms" (Wachtendonck Ps 67:33, *regna
terrae*), *kunni*, *gawādi*; gen. pl. **-ō** (*kunnō*, *endō*).
**ja-MASCULINES** (**hirti**, **rīdāri**, **fiskāri**, and every agent
noun in **-āri**, which belong to this class): gen. sg. **-as**, dat. sg.
**-ē**; **nom./acc. pl. -a**, gen. pl. **-ō**, dat. pl. **-on/-un**.
⚠⚠ **NO `-i-` BEFORE THE OBLIQUE ENDING — see the arbitration of
2026-08-17, in this same section.** The kit wrote `beddies`, `kunnies`,
†`rīkies` in the NEUTER and †`-es` in the MASCULINE: **one class inflected
two ways.**
⚠ **THIS RULE ABROGATED THE ONE THAT SAID "-āri: a-stems, gen. pl. -io,
dat. pl. -ium"** (2026-08-15). The two coexisted for a few hours, thirty
lines apart, and **the two forced grids caught it first**. The abrogated
form was an **Old Saxonism**: Gallée §301 gives for the Saxon ja-masculine
gen. pl. *hirdio*, dat. pl. *hirdiun* — but the double enquiry at rank (a)
showed that **in the plural Low Franconian merged the ja- and
the a-stems**, the *j* surviving only in the nom./acc. singular. To import
*-io/-ium* IN THE NOMINATIVE-ACCUSATIVE PLURAL is to import *hirdios*.
⚠⚠ **BUT THAT RULING HOLDS FOR THAT SLOT ONLY, and it was wrongly
generalised to the DATIVE PLURAL of the ja-NEUTERS.** Its single witness,
`lêidera` "duces" (Wachtendonck Ps 67:28), is a nominative-accusative
plural of a MASCULINE agent noun. The double enquiry of 2026-08-18 gives,
for the dative plural of the neuters, **`-ium`** — Goth. `reikjam`,
`waldufnjam`; OS `kunniun`, `hreobeddion`, `bilithiun`; Franconian OHG
`kunnim` dominant (Braune §198 anm. 6); and **OE `herium`**, which shows
that `cynnum` is a PHONETIC fall and not an alignment. *A ruling drawn
from one slot does not hold for the slot next door.*
⚠ **The slot was empty, and it is filled on 2026-08-15 by a double
enquiry at rank (a)** — and both enquirers found the SAME single witness:
***lêidera*** "duces", Wachtendonck **Ps 67:28**. (⚠ Köbler indexes it
"67,8": a slip of the figure, corrected against Cowan's text.)
⚠⚠ **IN THE PLURAL, THE ja- AND THE a-STEMS MERGED.** The *j* survives
only in the nom./acc. SINGULAR (*-i*: *hirti*, *hruggi*, *endi*). Do NOT
import OS ***hirdios***: the sibilant there is Ingvaeonic, and Low
Franconian gives *-a* — Gallée §301 Anm. 4 himself says of the Saxon
forms in *-a* that they are "borrowed from the a-stems". Franconian
generalised the innovation, Saxon kept the archaism.
⚠ **The dative plural remains UNDECIDABLE at rank (a)**: the one witness
is the corrupt spelling ⟨endron⟩ (Ps 18:4), which the ONW and Quak emend
to *endion* (with a reflex of the *j*) and Cowan to *endon* (without).
Two editors, two paradigms, one stroke of the pen. One writes **-on** by
alignment on the a-stems, knowing the slot is not proven.
⚠ **Three classifications corrected in passing**: *beki* is NOT a
ja-masculine (an `-i` stem, and the ONW gives it as feminine) · *heri* is
not attested as a common noun at rank (a) · *rīki* and *kunni* are
NEUTER.
⚠⚠ **THE ROOT NOUNS — RULING OF 2026-08-21, DOUBLE ENQUIRY, AND MY
QUESTION WAS WRONG.**

**root nouns: `fōt`, pl. `fōti`.** ⚠ *The form was right; it was the
label that lied.*

**Both enquirers refuse the question in the same terms**:
> *"You are testing root-noun status by UMLAUT IN THE PLURAL. But in Low
> Franconian **umlaut NEVER touches long vowels or diphthongs** — that is
> a defining trait of Dutch. `bōk`/†`bœk`, `mūs`/†`mȳs`, `kū`/†`kȳ` are
> not 'levelled' at rank (a): **they could never have existed.** What you
> have in mind is the Old English paradigm, not a Franconian one."*
> (92 % and 100 %)

⚠⚠ **AND THE GRAVEST FAULT IS THE ONE I HAD NOT ASKED ABOUT**: the class
key promised a *"mutated plural"*. Rank (a) gives **`fuoti`**, ending
preserved, **with no umlaut**.
  > *"If your kit produces plurals of the type †`fait`, †`mȳs`, †`bēc`
  > for Frankish of 550, **it produces Old English in disguise**."*
  ⚠ **The ONLY word of the batch for which rank (a) attests a genuine
  mutated plural is `tand` → `tende`** — precisely because its vowel is a
  **short `/a/`**, the only umlautable vowel of Dutch. *That is the
  internal proof that the absence of umlaut elsewhere is PHONOLOGICAL,
  not morphological.*

~~**Weak obliques, split by GENDER** — the only paradigm where gender
governs the ending (fem. suffix *-ōn- long against masc./neut.
*-an-/-in- short): masc. **-on** (Franko, hason "to the hares");
**fem. -un** (tunga → tungun, sunna → sunnun, frauwa → frauwun, ana →

Ruling of 2026-08-05: "-on everywhere" was drawn from a masculine example
and crushed the feminine, which the adjective kept (theru rehtun sīdu)
and which the corpus never stopped writing — **sunnun endi mānon**, saga
§I: both genders distinguished within one phrase, on day 1.
The emendation of 2026-08-03 (the fall of the old -onum, a phantom form)
stands: it bore on the dative plural, not on gender. An apposed past
participle agrees (gamālōdēm). **Postposed to a prepositional phrase**, it
agrees with the noun it qualifies, in that noun's case — not with the head
of the phrase: ain hurd fon holtē **gaflohtanemu** "a hurdle of woven
wood" (strong neuter dative, agreeing with holtē). Requisition R27.
**wesan preterite**: was, pl. wārum / wārut / wārun.
**`-wa` stems**: strō, gen. strawas, dat. strawē; snaiw/snaiwas/snaiwē;
saiw/saiwas/saiwē; melo/melwas/melwē; **smero/smerwas/smerwē**.

### The adjective

**THE ADJECTIVE — generated from `cases.md`** (2026-08-20). ⚠⚠
**INCLUDING THE STRONG / WEAK CHOICE**, whose rule as carried here until
2026-08-20 had been **revoked 2,900 lines further on** — translator H of
the 41st round called it *"the most dangerous contradiction in the
document"*, because the section filed by matter is the most natural one
to consult. **The register removes the very possibility of the defect:
there is now only one place where the rule is written.**



**strong**

| slot | form | where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| masc. nom./acc./gen./dat. sg. | gōd / gōdan / gōdes / gōdemu | § The adjective |
| fem. nom./acc./gen./dat. sg. | gōd / gōda / gōdero / gōdero | § The adjective |
| neuter nom./acc./gen./dat. sg. | gōd / gōd / gōdes / gōdemu | § The adjective |
| plural, all genders | gōda / gōda / gōdero / gōdēm | ⚠⚠ **the plural is SINGLE, with no apportionment by gender — deliberately so, not a gap**: Low Franconian levelled the plural of the adjective as it did the verbal paradigm. One writes *sīna kind* (neuter) as *gōda man*. Two translators followed the table on 2026-08-09 while reporting that they expected a neuter in `-u`: **the table was right, it did not say that it had decided** |
| adjectives in -i | the `-i-` drops before a vocalic ending | *thiu **skauna** frauwa*, *the **thriddo** man*, *thiu **thridda** hōba* — and not †*thriddio*. ⚠ **the rule holds ALSO for the ordinals** |

**weak**

| slot | form | where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| masc. nom. sg. | gōdo | § The adjective |
| fem./neuter nom. sg. | gōda | § The adjective |
| obliques sg. | gōdon | § The adjective |
| fem. acc./gen./dat. | gōdun | ⚠ **the only exception** — *theru rehtun sīdu* |
| plural, all cases and genders | -on | gen. full form `-ono`, optional, a solemn style |
| ⚠⚠ WHEN to use it | **REFERENCE**, not the determiner | ⚠⚠ **RULING**: the rule “weak after a determiner, strong elsewhere” **describes modern German** and is *logically impossible at 550*, the determiner not being an obligatory category there. **`gōdo` does not mean “good” but “THE good one, the-one-who-is-good”** — an individualising `-n-` stem, hence a NOUN IN APPOSITION, which has no need of an article. Onomastic proof: *Haraldr hárfagri*, *Eiríkr rauði*. **What governs is individuation and identifiability — neither position, nor register, nor the presence of a word** |

| slot | form | where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| comparative of the ADJECTIVE, **ATTRIBUTIVE** | `-iro`, declined **weak** | § The adjective |
| ⚠⚠ comparative of the ADJECTIVE, **PREDICATIVE** | **BARE, uninflected** | ⚠⚠ **RULING OF 2026-08-14 THAT THIS REGISTER WAS IGNORING — carried here 2026-08-21.** *“The comparative has no strong inflection, and in predicate position it stays bare.”* The two forced grids showed that it otherwise had **no licit form at all**: the agreement rule imposes the STRONG form in the predicate, and the comparative is declared always WEAK — the two rules contradict each other on this phrase. Internal warrants: *thrim stapon **braidiro***, *grimmiro than…*, **bare forms**. ⚠ Translator R of the 46th round had written †`hardira` from table ⑤ and found the ruling **only by accident**, while grepping for something else |
