**Weak imperatives**: -ian → -i (**hauri!** "listen!", **weggi!**
"rock it!"), -ōn → -o (**wardo!** "keep watch!"). ⚠⚠ ~~-ēn → -e
(habe!)~~ **WITHDRAWN 2026-08-21: there are no more verbs in `-ēn`.**
The ruling on weak class III dissolved the category on 2026-08-19;
`hebbian` moved to the closed annexe, and its imperative is **`hebi!`**.
  ⚠ *The § on the moods had withdrawn the SAME example two days earlier,
  stating the maxim that condemns a partial operation — "a rule that
  enumerates its domains ages with them" — and the example survived here,
  plus twice elsewhere. Reported by translator P of the 45th round.*
Plural in **-iad / -ōd**: *hauriad!* "listen, all of you!" — ⚠ **the
plural imperative IS the 2nd person** (reversal of 2026-08-14); it can no
longer end in *-at*.
**Past participle of the WEAK verbs** — on the preterite stem, with no
linking vowel: class I in -ian (pret. -da/-ta) → ga- + stem + -d/-t
(seggian/sagda → **gasagd**; brennian/branda → **gabrand**;
strekkian/strakta → **gastrakt**; dailian/dailda → **gadaild**;
dōmian/dōmda → **gadōmd**); class II in -ōn → **-ōd** (mālōn → gamālōd,
werkōn → gawerkōd); residual class III → the dental suffix DIRECTLY, with
no linking vowel (`hebbian` → **gahabd**, `seggian` → **gasagd**,
`libbian` → **galibd**) — ⚠ and the root there is **ungeminated**.
Never an epenthetic vowel: †gadailid, †gadōmit are faulty.
Worked examples: *siu ist **gadaild*** "she is shared out",
*warth imu **gadōmd*** "it was judged for him".
**Gerund of the weak verbs in -ian**: te **sakianne** "in order to
claim", te haurianne "in order to hear" — the same formation as *te
gebanne*, on the full infinitive. E.g. *thia liudi kumand it te sakianne*
"the people come to claim it".
**Present of the weak class I in -ian** (requisition R11 bis): 1sg -iu,
2sg -is, 3sg **-id**, pl. **-iam / ⚠ -iad / -iand** — ⚠ *the 2nd pl.
`-iad` is an OPEN SLOT in the register: bounded here on 2026-08-23* —
haurian → hauriu, hauris, **haurid**, pl. hauriam, hauriad, hauriand;
likewise seggian → **seggid**, erian → **erid**, laidian → **laidid**,
dailian → dailid, dōmian → dōmid. ⚠⚠ **EXCEPT `seggian`, AND THE EXCEPTION IS DECLARED.** This verb is classified TWICE in this kit: weak I here — which gives `seggid` — and in the annexe of `hebbian` at the paradigms block, which gives **`segid`**, the form the dictionary writes. The register of slots carries the collision as OPEN; this line does not close it. *Both translators of 2026-08-30 wrote `segid`, and both said they had had to choose.*
**Weak verbs**: haurian-haurda; **preterite 2sg in -es** (preterite stem,
final -a replaced by -es): *thū **haurdes***, *thū **habdes***,
*thū **wardōdes***, *thū **erides***.
**Agreement of the predicative** (written 2026-08-09): the predicative
agrees in the STRONG inflection, with wesan as with werthan — *thia wāga
wārun sō **hauha*** "the waves were so high", *sān wurdun mī thia
wandlunga **galīka***.
The one exception is the **singular of the `-i` adjectives, which stays
bare**, because their strong nom. sg. has no ending: *the saiw was
**stilli*** "the sea was calm", *that word was **māri*** — and not
†stillir, †stilli-ø in the pl. (in the plural they inflect like the
others: *thia **saiwa** wārun **stilla***).
⚠⚠ **PLURAL CORRECTED 2026-08-15: the example carried †*seuwos*, which is
a SAXONISM.** Double enquiry at rank (a): the ending **-os** is
Ingvaeonic (OS *-os*, OE *-as*, OFris *-ar*); **Low Franconian gives
*-a***, and attests it directly — *daga* (nom. pl. Ps 72:10 AND acc. pl.
54:24), *himila*, *hunda*, *duoma*, *cuninga* (Ps 67:15, **in the very
verse that carries *sneue***). The single *-as* in all the material
classed "anfrk." is *dadsisas*, and Köbler himself notes in the apparatus
"Beleg eher as.": the lexicographer **withdraws the witness from Low
Franconian**. The *w* of the `-wa` stems reappears before any vocalic
ending — attested in the oblique singular (*seuues*, *seuue*, *sneue*,
*horouue*) — whence **saiwa**, **snaiwa**.
⚠⚠ **CORRECTED 2026-08-14: the example carried †*stillia* and
contradicted the rule it illustrated.** The two forced grids had caught
it, and had DIVERGED on it — proof that the slot was undecidable. Double
enquiry at rank (a): **the rule is right, the example was wrong.** Old
Low Franconian attests ***suota*** (Wachtendonck Ps 54:15, strong acc.
pl., *dulces cibos*) and ***uustera*** (Ps 62:2, fem. dat. sg., *in terra
deserta*), plus a dozen present participles in **-a** (*libbinda,
fehtinda, suokinda, criepinda*) and genitives in **-ero**
(*sprekenderô, blîthenderô*). **No spelling †-ia, †-ies, †-iemo, †-iero
is attested for an adjective anywhere in the corpus.** Heyne 1873 §38:
"*In den Psalmen ist sie ausserhalb des Nominativs sg.


### The numerals

**Declined numerals**: ain (strong adj.); twē — m. twēne, f. twā,
n. twē, gen. twēiō, dat. twēm; thrī — m./f. thrī (var. *thria, thrie*),
n. thriu, gen. thrīo, dat. thrim; from 4 on, invariable.
⚠⚠ **NOMINATIVE AND ACCUSATIVE ARE THE SAME FORM** — for *twē* as for
*thrī*, in all three genders. The kit prescribed "nom. *thrī* / acc.
*thria*": **double enquiry at rank (a), 2026-08-15, the distinction is
refuted.** The three variants *thrie / threa / thria* appear
indifferently in both cases in the same corpus (nom. H 593 C, acc.
H 4735 C); the variation follows the MANUSCRIPTS, not the syntax. Same
for *twēne*: nom. H 1771 M, acc. H 1263 M. West Germanic lost the
opposition — **only Gothic keeps it** (*twai/twans*, *þreis/þrins*), and
syncretism is in any case the general rule of the language (*daga* is
nom. pl. AND acc. pl.).
Rank (a) attests "two" **only once in the whole corpus** (*tuene*,
Wachtendonck Ps. 61:12) — and by luck in direct-object position, hence in
the accusative. ⚠ †*twegia* in the genitive would be an **Anglicism**
(OE *twēg(e)a*): the continent gives *twēiō*, with no stop.
**Government of the counted noun — the criterion is not indeclinability,
it is NATURE: a numeral is "large" when it is a NOUN, and a noun governs
a genitive.**
· **1 to 3** — declined: the noun agrees and keeps its case (nom. *thrī
  dagā* "three days", acc. **thrī dagā** "for three days" —
⚠⚠ *(macron restored 2026-08-21: see the ruling on UNWRITTEN QUANTITY,
below at the § on the `-a` plural)* —
  ⚠ **the numeral DOES NOT CHANGE between nominative and accusative**: it
  is the NOUN that carries the case. The earlier wording prescribed *acc.
  thria*, a form refuted on 2026-08-15: *thria* is simply a graphic
  variant of *thrī*, attested in BOTH cases in the same corpus),
  twā **gensi** "two geese");
  ⚠ **gensi, and not †gansi** — corrected 2026-08-15. *gans* is an old
  feminine root noun that passed to the `-i` stems, exactly like *gast* →
  pl. *gesti*: **L5 applies to it without reservation**, and neither of
  the law's two bounds (full syllable, comparative) covers it. The kit
  wrote †*gansi* in its own example **against its own law**, while the
  Skuldbōk was already writing *gensi*.
  ⚠ And **neither *gens* nor *gēs***: a plural with BARE mutation
  presupposes the Anglo-Frisian apocope of the *-i*, which the continent
  does not have — rank (a) keeps the ending, and proves it on a word of
  identical structure — ⚠ **and the two forms that follow are CITATIONS
  from rank (a), in Old Dutch spelling, not Frenkisk forms**: Old Du
  ⟨tende⟩ nom. pl. of *tand* (Wachtendonck Ps 56:5 and 57:7) and ⟨fuoti⟩
  nom. pl. of *fōt*. **In Frenkisk one writes *tendi* and *fōti***
  (the sequence *uo* is forbidden; see the list of impossible sequences).
  The distinction was missing, and the forced grid immediately asked
  whether the plural of *tand* was *tende* or *tendi*.
· **4 to 19** — indeclinable, but **the noun keeps its case**: they are
  not nouns (twelif muddi, sehs akkrā, fior swīn, twelif penningā);
· **tens, hundreds, thousands and indefinites** (twaintig, thrītig,
  hund, thūsund, filu, manag) → **genitive plural**: twaintig haunō,
  thrītig jārō, twē hund skillingō, thero fiskō filu. Reason: twaintig
  "two tens" IS a noun;
· the noun of **MATERIAL** after a measure keeps its genitive of
  material, whatever the numeral: twelif muddi **hwaitas**, sehs
  **sestara**
**Ordinals** — `-i` stems, hence **declined WEAK with loss of the `-i`
before a vocalic ending**, exactly like the `-i` adjectives (skauni →
skauno/skauna): 1 airisto, 2 andaro (the strong form is andar),
3 **thriddo** (f./n. thridda), 4 fiortho, 5 fimfto, 6 sehsto,
7 sibundo, 8 ahtodo, 9 nigundo, 10 tehando, 11 ellifto, 12 twelifto;
from 13 to 19 in **-tehando** (thriutehando, fiortehando…), the tens in
**-tigosto** (twaintigosto, thrītigosto).
**baithe "both"**: nom. AND ACC. m. baithe, f. **baitha**, n. baithiu ⚠ (and not †*bēthia*: **no spelling †-ia is attested for an adjective** — the table carried the daggered form, and the dictionary entry announced the correction in the past tense without its having taken place; **a ruling note that narrates the correction DOES NOT STAND IN PLACE of the correction, and what it narrates is not verified** — ⚠⚠ **a NEGATION had DROPPED OUT of this sentence, which therefore stated the exact opposite of golden rule no. 12, in bold, in the one place where that rule is applied to a concrete case.** Found by translator B of the 37th round. *A negation that drops out breaks nothing: the sentence stays grammatical, the bold stays in place, and twenty controls go green on an inverted statement.* — corrected 2026-08-15); gen. baithero,
dat. baithēm; it stands before the article (baithiu heri).
**Agreement after a SUBSTANTIVAL numeral**: the verb goes into the
**PLURAL**, by notional agreement — *twaintig skipō **sint** kuman*
"twenty ships have come", *thrītig mannō **stigun** an that skip* "thirty
men boarded the ship". Never †*ist*, †*staig*. The numeral governs the
genitive but does not command the agreement: the counted noun carries it.
**Mood after *unt that* "until"**: **subjunctive** when the term is still
to come (unt that it eft gahladan **farē**), indicative when it is
observed. The same split as *air than*.
**The conditional hinge *of… thanne***: *thanne* resumes the apodosis and
triggers **V2**, like *thō* — Of ain skip brikid, **thanne ist** that
fehu thes mannes.


### The pronouns

**Possessives unser/iuwer**: strong inflection WITH syncope — unsan,
unses, unsemu, unsa, unsero, unsēm (likewise iuwan, iuwemu…).
**THE REFLEXIVE IS GENERAL** (2026-08-09): a subject that moves or alters
of itself takes **sik** — *thō wanda **sik** the wind*, *thiu erda **sik**
kliubid* "the earth splits", *the wind **hōf sik*** "the wind rose". The
rule was stated for *wendian* alone; the two translators of the 5th test
extended it to *kleuban* and *heffian* "believing it to be general". It
is.
⚠ **THE ABSOLUTE PARTICIPLE OF THE ESCHATOCOL.** *Gidān te
Wandriscellu, thena thriddon dag…* "Done at W., on the third day…": a
participle **with no subject and no clause**, closing a legal act. It is
the one exception to the rule that an apposed participle is controlled by
the subject — there is no subject, and the genre requires it. The two
translators of the 22nd test wrote it identically, one noting that the
kit did not license it.
**the selbo** "that same one" if one wants to insist.
⚠ **THREE USES, THREE FORMS** (completed 2026-08-09):
· **determined moment** → accusative SINGULAR: *thena āband* "that
  evening", *thia naht* "that night";
· ⚠⚠ **INDEFINITE or HABITUAL moment** → **the ADVERBIAL GENITIVE, with
  no preposition and no determiner**: ***nahtas*** "by night",
  ***dagas*** "by day", ***dagas endi nahtas*** "day and night".
  ⚠ **The letter is Frenkisk's, not the witness's**: rank (b) writes
  *nahtes*, *dages*, endings **reduced by 830**; at 550 the genitive of
  the `-a` stem is **`-as`** (ruling of 2026-08-16), and **Bergakker
  attests it on Frankish soil two generations before the target** —
  *haþuþywas*, 425–475. *One takes the CONSTRUCTION from the Heliand,
  never the ending: the enquirer said it himself — "you cannot take from
  the Heliand both its syntax AND its weakened endings".*
  **RULING OF 2026-08-21, DOUBLE ENQUIRY — and it is the largest
  correction the kit has received on a point of construction.**
  > ~~*an theru naht* "by night"~~ **is withdrawn. The whole construction
  > was wrong — not just the determiner.**

⚠⚠⚠ **AND THESE THREE EXAMPLES ARE PRECISELY THE ONES THE RULING OF
2026-08-25 ADVISES AGAINST** — translator C of the 59th round saw it by
reading the two passages 4,100 lines apart. The wind that turns, the
earth that splits, the wind that rises are **anticausatives with an
INANIMATE subject**, and § *TRANSITIVE OR INTRANSITIVE* writes: "⚠ **NOT
the reflexive.** `sik` exists, but its **anticausative** use only
flourishes in Middle High German. **Both enquirers advise against it for
550.**"

> **THE BOUND, AND IT IS CLEAR: `sik` is licit with an ANIMATE AGENT**
> (*the man hōf sik* "the man rose"), and advised against for making the
> anticausative of an INANIMATE subject (†*thiu duri opanōd sik*), where
> the kit prescribes `werthan` + participle (*thiu duri wirthid opan*).

⚠ The three examples above come from the corpus and **remain true as
witnesses**; they are not models to copy for new prose. *A fresh ruling
that does not sweep the section it bounds leaves two rules alive, and the
reader takes whichever he lands on.*

  **Both enquirers name it separately as the most important point of the
  file**, and they support it at both ranks, to the identical letter:
  **The warrants — numbers only, the witness's letters are not copied
  out** *(the kit is distributed alone: an ending of 830 set under a
  translator's eyes gets copied)*:
  · rank (b), *Heliand* — adverbial genitive "by night" at ll. **425,
    755, 1178, 4715**; "day and night" at ll. **515** and **2480**;
    genitive plural of the same turn at l. **451**; "every day", some
    fifteen occurrences;
  · rank (a), **Wachtendonck Ps 1:2** — "day and night", **and that is
    where the divergence lies**: the Latin carries the ablative *die ac
    nocte*.
  ⚠⚠ **A SLOT OPENS HERE, AND IT IS NOT FILLED: `nahtas`.**
  `naht` belongs to the **consonantal stems**, whose proper genitive is not in `-as`. Yet
  rank (b) writes *nahtes* — that is, a **thematic, analogical**
  genitive, the adverb having detached itself from the paradigm (as
  English *nights* and German *nachts* did). **The analogy is complete by
  830. Is it complete by 550?**
  · if yes → **`nahtas`**, which is what the kit writes;
  · if no → the root noun's genitive, **with a zero ending**, which would
    make the adverb **formally invisible** (†*naht* "by night").

  **Rated 65 / 35 in favour of `nahtas`** — a frozen adverb usually
  resists the paradigm rather than following it, which tells *against*
  me; but the analogy is already complete in all three West Germanic
  branches, which places it early. ⚠ **No witness settles it. One writes
  `nahtas` knowing that.** *`dagas` has no such problem: `dag` IS an `-a`
  stem.*

  ⚠⚠ **AND THIS RANK (a) ATTESTATION PASSES THE DIVERGENCE TEST**: the
  Latin carries the ablative *die ac nocte*, and the glossator
  **abandons the case form of his model** for a properly Germanic turn.
  *In an interlinear gloss only divergence bears witness — this one bears
  witness.*

  ⚠ **WHY I HAD GONE WRONG, AND IT IS INSTRUCTIVE**: I was looking for
  how to render "by night" **with a preposition, because the source
  language has one.** The two translators of the 43rd round did exactly
  the same, for the same reason. *The question "article or no article"
  masked the fact that the whole construction was a calque.*

  ⚠ **For the seasons and the months the enquiry found nothing** — it
  bore only on `dag` and `naht`. *an themu wintarē* "in winter" is
  therefore **RECONSTRUCTED by extension of the principle**, not
  attested; the genitive ***wintras*** would be safer if one follows
  `nahtas`. ⚠⚠ *(†`wintres` and †`nahtes` corrected 2026-08-21: this very
  paragraph had just demonstrated, on `nahtas`, that one never takes the
  Heliand's weakened endings — and it took them four lines lower.
  Reported by translator R of the 46th round, who notes that the heading
  § THE PRONOMINAL GENITIVE DOES NOT MIGRATE carries `thes wintras` in so
  letters.)* **Slot declared: one writes the genitive, knowing it is an
  extension.**
⚠ **AND THE ITERATIVE DOES NOT QUARREL WITH THE GENITIVE OF THE TENS**:
  the two rules bear on different levels. The genitive is INTERNAL to the
  numeral group (*thrītig **jārō***, "three tens of years"); the
  iterative bears on the WHOLE GROUP, seen from outside. So one writes
  ***alla thrītig jārō*** "every thirty years", without touching the
  genitive. The two translators of the 26th round stumbled there, each
  citing a rule that was right; what was missing was the arbiter.
· **ITERATIVE** ("every…") → accusative plural: *alla herbistā* "every
  autumn", *alla jār* "every year". Use NEITHER *gahwilīk*, which stays a
  pronoun with a partitive genitive and is not used attributively, NOR
  the distributive *bī* + dat., reserved for measure. The two translators
  of the 6th test improvised here, each differently.
⚠ **THE BARE ACCUSATIVE DETERMINES.** *thia naht* says "that night", not
"by night". **The distinction is right and it is confirmed; it was the
form of the other member that was wrong.** The minimal pair, corrected
2026-08-21, is:
> ***nahtas quam ain theuf*** "a thief came **by night**" (adverbial
> genitive, indefinite)
> ≠ ***thia naht quam the theuf*** "**that night**, the thief came"
> (accusative, no preposition, determiner kept).
⚠ †*an theru naht quam ain theuf* is withdrawn: `an theru naht` is
**attested nowhere** — zero occurrences in the 5,983 lines of the
*Heliand*, zero in Wachtendonck, zero in the *Tatian*. It was the one
structural divergence of the 4th independence test (2026-08-09): both
translators had SEEN the difference, one rendered it, the other followed
the prescription to the letter while writing that the kit did not say how
to mark the indefinite.

**dual**

| slot | form | where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| 1st “we two” | wit / unk / unker | ⚠⚠ **A WHOLE CATEGORY THAT WAS MISSING** — dating audit of 2026-08-16; the two enquirers name it separately, one calling it *“probably the most glaring absence in the system”* |
| 2nd “you two” | git / ink / inker | idem |
| agreement of the verb | **plural ordinaire** | *wit gebam*, *git gebid* |
| statut | **AN ATTESTED ARCHAISM**, not a reconstruction | Gothic *wit/ugkis*, Old Norse, Old English *wit/unc*, *git/inc*, **and Old Saxon *wit, git***. Old High German alone has lost it |

**others**

| slot | form | where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| possessives unser / iuwer | strong inflection WITH syncope | *unsan*, *unses*, *unsemu*, *unsa*, *unsero*, *unsēm* ; idem *iuwan*, *iuwemu* |
| possessive: the choice | `sīn` masc./neut. sg. · `ira` fem. · `iro` pl. | ⚠ the choice is made on the POSSESSOR; the agreement, on the THING POSSESSED. ⚠⚠ **reflexive `sīn` refers to the subject of its OWN clause** |
| substantival possessive | ⚠ ****aucun**** | “his own”, “mine” without a noun: **not recorded**. One repeats the noun (*than is hros*). The two translators of the 40th round stumbled on it separately. ⚠ And GRAMMATICAL gender can contradict sex: `wīf` being **neuter**, the rule mechanically gives `sīnan brōther` for a woman — **agreement *ad sensum* is licensed nowhere** |
| reflexive | **sik**, and it is GENERAL | a subject that moves or changes of itself takes it — *thō wanda **sik** the wind*, *OS thiu erða **sik** kliubid* ⚠ **a WITNESS citation, not a model** — in Frenkisk the nominative of this `-ō` stem would be **`thiu erdu`** (cf. table ⑥: “the entry cites in `-a`, the nominative is in `-u`”). Marked 2026-08-26: translator R read a contradiction of the kit into it. The rule was stated for `wendian` alone; the two translators of the 5th test extended it *“believing it general”*. **It is** |
| demonstrative of proximity | these / thit / thius; acc. thesan / thit / thesa; gen. theses / thesaro; dat. thesumu / thesaru; pl. thesa, gen. thesaro, dat. thesun | ⚠ **it is substantivised ONLY IN THE NEUTER** (`thit`, instr. `thius`) — no †`these` as an autonomous masculine pronoun |
| demonstrative of DISTANCE | **aucun** | ⚠⚠ **two degrees, not three**: the two enquiries searched the 5,983 lines of the *Heliand* and Wachtendonck for `gene`, `jena` — **zero occurrences**. Distance is expressed analytically: `the thar`, `the genouwar` |
| impersonal | **man**, and the noun `man` is the same word | ⚠ a homography OWNED by the witnesses. Three traits separate them: the impersonal requires the **singular**, it is **unstressed and welded to the finite verb**, and it **never** carries a determiner |

⚠⚠⚠ **pron — THIS COUNT BEARS ONLY ON THE PERIMETER OF THE REGISTER**: it says how many of the 19 slots **entered** are open. ⚠⚠ It does NOT say the list is complete: a slot that is absent is not counted open, it is **invisible**, and it is the invisible one that makes people invent without knowing it.

⚠⚠ **1 OPEN SLOT out of 19**, and the register sets them side by side, which the prose scattered:
· **others, substantival possessive**


The verb goes into the ordinary plural: *wit gebam*, *git gebid*.
⚠ **This is an ATTESTED ARCHAISM, not a reconstruction**: the dual is in
Gothic (*wit / ugkis*), in Old Norse, in Old English (*wit / unc*, *git /
inc*) **AND IN OLD SAXON** (*wit, git*). Only Old High German lost it —
and at an unknown date. **A northern variety in 550 therefore very
probably still had it**, and its absence was one of the features that
dated the kit to the 9th century.
⚠⚠ **WHEN TO USE IT — RULE DOWNGRADED 2026-08-18, ON A DOUBLE ENQUIRY.**
The kit said **OBLIGATORY**. Both enquirers refuse that separately, at
**82 %** and **85 %**, and their decisive argument is structural:

> **West Germanic lost the dual on the VERB.** Gothic has a dual verbal
> inflection (Goth. *bairōs*, Goth. *bairats*); Old Saxon, Old English
> and Old High German have none. **And it is agreement that makes a
> category obligatory.** A dual surviving only on the pronoun and its
> possessive, with no agreement to enforce it, is a pragmatic choice —
> not a constraint.

*A category with no agreement is not an obligatory category.*

⚠ **And this settles the divergence of the 36th round: BOTH translators
were right.** One had written `unker mōdar`, the other `unsar mōdar`,
between two brothers. **`unker` is the better choice** — a preconstituted,
contrastive pair — **but `unsar` is not a fault**, and would even be
preferable if the two brothers speak on behalf of a wider set of
siblings. *My rule had converted a strong preference into an obligation,
and manufactured a fault where the language offered a choice.*

⚠⚠ **TWO GUARDS, rated 95 % by C1**: **no VERBAL dual** — a dual subject
takes the verb in the plural; and **no dual in the 3rd person**, nowhere
in Germanic, Gothic included. "The two of them" is said in the plural.

⚠ **The dual possessive `unker`/`inker` is indeed used ATTRIBUTIVELY and
DECLINES** — it is a `*-era-` adjective, the same series as `unsar`. C1
gives the attestation in PROSE: *"Iċ wēne þēah, ġif wit ġīet **uncru
word** tōsamne slēaþ"*, the Alfredian Boethius. ⚠ But the nominative
singular is **without an ending in all three genders** — `unker mōdar` —
which makes it look indeclinable; the obliques reveal the inflection.
⚠⚠ **And the root `unk-` is HEAVY**: by rule R-b below, it SYNCOPATES in
the obliques — `unkres`, `unkrē`. *`unker` and `aran` must behave in
OPPOSITE ways, and that is the consistency test of the new rule.*

The dual is **the default** when the referents are exactly two and the
speaker sets them up as a pair — two brothers, two parties to a suit, a
man and his wife. Using *wī* for two people is not wrong but says
something else: a group whose members are not being counted.

⚠ **WHAT THE PROJECT MUST KNOW**: no instrument of the kit could see
this absence. A forced grid fills only the slots the grammar provides
for; a translator does not ask for a category whose existence he does not
suspect. *Someone from outside had to be asked what was MISSING, not what
was wrong.*
⚠⚠ **THE REFLEXIVE OF THE 1st AND 2nd PERSONS IS THE PERSONAL PRONOUN
ITSELF** — *ik wardo **mik***, *thū wardos **thik***, *wī wardōm **uns***,
*gī wardōd **iu***; likewise in the dative: *ik nam **mī** land*. **Only
the 3rd person has a form of its own**, and it is *sik*. Germanic never
had a 1st- or 2nd-person reflexive: Gothic *ik … mik*, OHG *mih*, OS
*mī*, OE *mē* — the ordinary pronoun does all the work, and no ambiguity
arises since the person is already marked on the verb.
⚠ **THIS SLOT WAS MISSING, AND BOTH TRANSLATORS OF THE 30th ROUND
REPORTED IT SEPARATELY** (2026-08-16): "the kit devotes thirty lines to
the 3rd-person reflexive and not one word to the others". They both wrote
*iu* — correctly — but with no authority, and one entered it as a doubt.
*A rule that treats only one third of its domain reads as if the rest did
not exist.*
⚠⚠ **REFLEXIVE: ONE SLOT ONLY, THE ACCUSATIVE *sik*** (ruling of
2026-08-14, double enquiry at rank (a)). **The reflexive dative and
genitive DO NOT EXIST in West Germanic** — not one form, not one dialect,
not one manuscript, from Kent to the Rhine. The cause is
**phonological**: the fall of final \*-z gives \*miz > mī, \*thiz > thī,
hence \*siz > \*si — an unstressed monosyllable homophonous with *sī*
"she", which could not hold; the accusative \*sik survived because its
*-k* particle made it robust.
⚠ **The boundary does not run in time, it runs between the branches**:
Gothic keeps *sis* in the 4th c. **because it is EASTERN** and did not
undergo the fall of \*-z. Its testimony says nothing about Frankish. The
loss is at the node of common West Germanic — **by 550 it has been
settled for three centuries.**
**WHAT ONE WRITES INSTEAD** — the dative of the anaphoric pronoun used
reflexively, the solution of all five western branches:
in the DATIVE one writes **imu** for a masculine or neuter possessor,
**iru** for a feminine, **im** for a plural, with the optional
reinforcement *imu selbon*; in the GENITIVE one writes **sīn** in the
masculine-neuter, **ira** in the feminine, **iro** in the plural, and
*sīn selbes* for the objective genitive.
Examples: *nam **imu** land* "he took himself a piece of land" · *thwōg
**imu** thō hendi* "he washed his hands" · *quath **imu selbon*** "he said
to himself" · but *barg **sik*** "he hid himself" (a true accusative).
⚠ **NEVER WRITE** †*sir*, †*sis*, †*siz*: no such thing exists in West
Germanic. And †*sik* in the dative would be an **anachronism of a
thousand years** — German dative *sich* is a LATE extension of the
accusative (15th–17th c.; Luther still writes the old turn), and Dutch
*zich* is a **borrowing from German** whose *-ch* betrays a consonant
shift Low Franconian never underwent.
⚠ **ONE ATTESTED VARIANT, TO BE KEPT MARGINAL**: the psalter once extends
the accusative form to the dative function — *Gefestodon **sig** uuort
niuuiht* = *firmaverunt **sibi** sermonem nequam* (Ps 63:5). A glossator
with no other form to hand. Licit, but not the norm.
⚠⚠ **THERE IS NO POLITE PLURAL** (ruling of 2026-08-14, double enquiry).
*thū* addresses everyone — the king, the elder, God. **The plural *gī* is
NUMERICAL, never deferential.**
> ⚠⚠⚠ **AND THIS WARNING CHANGES SHAPE FOR AN ENGLISH-SPEAKING
> TRANSLATOR, so it is restated rather than translated.** Modern English
> has no polite plural to resist: *you* does the work of both. The reflex
> to guard against is the other one — **rendering `thū` by an ecclesiastical
> "thou"**, which in English reads as archaic and reverent and so imports
> exactly the deference this ruling says the language does not have.
> *`thū` is the ordinary word for one person. It is not solemn. It is
> what a man says to his brother, to his slave and to his king alike.*


**Proofs, five converging lines**: ⚠ **Gregory of Tours**, 19 speeches
with a royal vocative — **13 in the pure singular, 4 mixed, ZERO in the
pure plural**; and the minimal pair of *Historiae* IX,8: *Peccavi
**tibi** ac genetrici **tuae**, non oboediendo praeceptionibus
**vestris*** — the king alone in the singular, **the king AND his mother
in the plural, in the same sentence** · ⚠ the **Heliand**, 248 *thu*
against 257 *gi*, **not one *gi* to a single addressee**, the centurion
addressing Christ as *thu* (*hairro the gōdo, that **thu** an mīn hūs
cumes*) · ⚠ the **Ludwigslied** (881, Franconian, West Francia, maximal
Romance contact): ***frō mīn**, sō lango beidōn wir **thīn*** · ⚠ the
**Hildebrandslied**, where the son addresses **the old man as *thu***
although the text stresses the hierarchy of age · ⚠ and **the Latin of
550 did not yet use the plural to its kings**: there was no model to
calque. The first Germanic trace is **Otfrid, 863–871**, to a bishop, in
a latinising epistle — **three centuries too late**.
**DEFERENCE IS MARKED OTHERWISE — five attested devices, cumulative:**
1. **the titular vocative** — *frō mīn*, *drohtin mīn*, *frō the gōdo*;
2. **self-abasement** — *thīnan holdon skalk*, *ik ni bim thes wirthig*
   ⚠ **corrected 2026-08-26**: the first drafting wrote †*ik nis thes
   wirthig*, which puts a **1st**-person subject with a **3rd**-person
   contraction. The double enquiry establishes `bim` in the 1st sg.
   (Wachtendonck, *Heliand*, *Isidor* agree):
   ⚠ **one lowers the speaker, one does not pluralise the addressee**;
3. **the 3rd person** — *haurie mīn frō* "may my lord hear";
4. **the subjunctive of entreaty** — *biddiu ik thī that thū…*, *of thū
   wili*;
5. **the verbalised gesture** — *hnaig imu*, *fēl te sīnēm fōtum*.
⚠ **The Merovingian *vos* does exist, but it is a REGISTER, not a
pronoun**: chancery, embassy, written petition, the mouth of a
Gallo-Roman cleric — and it **alternates with *tu* within the same
speech**, which is the signature of a device of style that has not been
grammaticalised.
⚠ **sīn AGAINST is — the distinction is OBLIGATORY** (2026-08-09).
**sīn** is the **REFLEXIVE** possessive: it points to the SUBJECT of its
own clause. **is** (gen. of *hē*) is the **NON-REFLEXIVE** possessive: it
points to somebody else. The source language does not mark the
difference — English *his* does both — and Frenkisk requires it:
⚠⚠ **sīn HAS NEITHER FEMININE NOR PLURAL** (2026-08-13, 29th round). The
reflexive *sīn* exists only in the **masculine and neuter singular**. A
FEMININE subject takes ***ira***, a PLURAL subject ***iro*** — reflexive
or not, **the distinction is NEUTRALISED outside the masculine/neuter**,
exactly as Dutch opposes *zijn* to *haar* without having a distinct
reflexive *haar*. So "she opened her eyes" = *siu opanōda **ira** augon*,
never †*sīna augon*; "they took their weapons" = *sia nāmun **iro**
wāpan*.
⚠ **The pronoun table above already carried *siu/sia/**ira**/iru***: the
answer was in the kit, and the rule, stated without gender, covered it
over. **BOTH translators of the 29th round wrote †*sīna augon* by
following the letter**, one noting that both standards would write *ira*.
**A rule illustrated by examples that are all masculine reads as holding
for every gender.**
⚠ **THE HARD CASE: when the possessor is the subject of the NARRATIVE but
not of the CLAUSE.** "His sons spoke to him gently" — Hildiberht is the
subject of the whole paragraph, but the subject of THIS clause is *suni*.
So ***is** suni sprākun*, not †*sīna suni*. The rule never looks at the
topic, only at the grammatical subject of the clause the possessive
stands in. At the 21st independence test (2026-08-13), one translator out
of two got it wrong, and it is the only place where the rule has given
way since it was written.
· *Ratheri quam te **is** hūsē* "Ratheri came to HIS house [Ansberht's]"
  — writing *te **sīnemu** hūsē* says that he went home, and
  changes the story; ⚠ *(both forms carried a SHORT `-e` here —
  the 9th-century reduced dative, against the kit's own `hūsē`
  elsewhere. The fault was in the French too; the control could not
  see it there, and the translation exposed it. 2026-08-29.)* changes the
  story;
⚠ **AND WHERE *is* WOULD BE AMBIGUOUS, ONE NAMES.** *is* does not mark
which of two men is the possessor: in a sentence where two referents of
the same gender are present, replace the pronoun with the **genitive of
the proper name**, placed first — *hwanda **Aldrikas** hros an
**Wulfhardas** akkrē gafundan wordan was*. The kit had no rule for this
case and, at the 19th independence test (2026-08-11), one of the two
translators named where the other left *is*: both reported that the
pronoun was not enough. Naming is not heavy; it is the only way to be
clear.

· *the man, the **sīn** fehu faran lātid* "the man who lets HIS beasts
  go" — the possessor IS the subject, hence *sīn*;
· *hwanda **was is** fehu an thena akkar gagangan* "because his beasts
  had gone in" — the subject is *fehu*, the possessor is elsewhere, hence
  *is*.
The kit gave both forms without saying which to use; at the 6th
independence test, one of the two translators wrote *sīn* three times
where the sense called for *is*, and that one missing rule accounted on
its own for a third of the measured gap.


### The verb

#### ⚠⚠ THE PRINCIPAL PARTS — A GENERATED TABLE, SOURCE `konjug.md`

> **Look for your verb here BEFORE reading the prose that follows.** This table is **generated** from a register: it cannot contradict the rest of the kit without `konjug.py` saying so. The prose can.

⚠ **The `source` column is the line's degree of confidence**, not a note of scholarship. `lexicon` = the entry writes it · `kit` = harvested from the prose and confirmed by the ablaut vowel of the class · `prefix` = the simplex is documented and the preverb does not touch the ablaut · `weak II` = a regular class.

⚠⚠ **WHAT THIS TABLE COVERS, AND WHAT IT DOES NOT.** It follows the class marker of the entry — `(I)`…`(VII)`, `(strong X)`, `(weak X)`. **A verb whose entry does not write its class is not here**, and its absence therefore does NOT mean that it has no preterite: it means that it has not been inventoried. That is the case of `skauwōn`, `andwordian`, `wardōn` and some 80 others — **look them up in their entries.** The preterite-presents and the irregulars (`wesan`, `dōn`, `gān`, `witan`, `skulan`…) live in their own section.

⚠⚠⚠ **12 VERB(S) HAVE NO PRETERITE, AND IT IS WRITTEN DOWN RATHER THAN FILLED IN**: `swellan`, `skirmian`, `sellian`, `siuwian`, `īlian`, `weggian`, `brennian`, `fōgian`, `dōmian`, `fullian`, `hrōrian`, `erian`. The vowel of their class is promulgated — one could therefore have manufactured them in three lines of code. *A control that goes red calls for a declaration, not for a lemma.*

> ⚠ **And this line has already been false.** On 2026-08-25 it announced **seven**, of which **six the dictionary was writing** — a single harvesting guard, which required every form inside a parenthesis to share the lemma's initial, killed the lines whose participle begins with `ga-`. Translator D of the 59th round saw it by opening the dictionary after reading the declaration. *A false declaration has the place of the right one and the content of the wrong one: it does not make people invent, it makes them GIVE UP WRONGLY.*

⚠ **What this table does NOT carry**: the whole paradigm (the endings are in the slot blocks), the verbs whose entry does not write the class, the preterite-presents and the irregulars, and **the syncope of weak I** — `sōkta` against `nerida` depends on the weight of the root, and that rule lives elsewhere.

⚠⚠ **REFLOWED 2026-08-16: filed by matter, in seven groups.** A
translator of the 30th round had had to **cross-check three places 550
lines apart** to establish a single plural imperative. The blocks were
moved whole, verbatim, and the check bore on the multiset of lines.

⚠ The four complete paradigms — `geban`, `haurian`, `wardōn`, `wesan` —
are **higher up, in a section of their own**. What follows completes
them: the classes, the sub-classes, the moods and the compound tenses.

#### The strong classes

**Strong verb (geban V)**: pres. gibu/gibis/gibid — gebam/gebid/geband;
pret. gaf/gābi/gaf/gābum-ut-un; subj. pres. **gebē**/gebēn; **subj. pret.
— 1sg gābi, 2sg **gābis**, 3sg gābi** — worked example: *air than thū te
themu akkrē **fōris*** "before you go to the ploughland", *of thū it **sāwis***
"if you saw it" ⚠ (**and not †*sāhis***: the preterite subjunctive is
built on the root of the PRETERITE PLURAL, as *gābum* gives *gābis*; and
*sehan* gives *sah* in the singular but **sāwun** in the plural, whence
*sāwis*.

⚠⚠ **THIS LINE HAS BEEN WRONG TWICE, AND THE SECOND TIME IS WORSE.** It
first said †*sāhis*; detector J corrected it to *sāgis* on 2026-08-15, on
the preterite plural `sāgum`. **Then the Verner ruling of 2026-08-16
rejected `sāgum` in favour of `sāwun`** — the attested against the
reconstructed — **and nobody went back to the rule that taught it.** The
dictionary was writing `sāwun`, the grammar was teaching `sāgum`, and it
is the grammar one reads in order to learn. Corrected 2026-08-18 on the
report of BOTH translators of the 36th round, separately.

> *A correction that is dated, signed and credited to a detector inspires
> a confidence it no longer deserves the moment a later enquiry withdraws
> its support. **A ruling that revokes another must go back over
> everything the first was used to write.***)
(the 2nd person carries the -s, unlike the preterite-presents above):
whence *ni **wāris** thū…* "would you not be…"; subj. pret. gābi/gābīn;
imper. **gif!**/**gebid!** ⚠ (and not †*gib!*: **L10** hardens the labial
spirant in absolute final position, exactly as it gives the preterite
*gaf* of this very verb and the recorded imperative *skiuf!*; rank (a)
attests **gif** twice in the Wachtendonck psalter, Ps 59:13 and 71:2. The
†*gib!* came from OHG, **rank (c), which never gives the letter**.
Corrected 2026-08-14 on an enquiry at rank (a)); gerund te gebanne; past
part. **gageban**. **Present participle by class**: strong -andi
(gebandi); -ian → **-iandi** (hauriandi, fulliandi, libbiandi); -ōn →
**-ōndi**; -ēn → **-ēndi**. Declined weak **IF THE PHRASE
INDIVIDUALISES**, strong otherwise — ⚠ *and not "weak after an article",
which is the modern German rule (corrected 2026-08-23): a possessive* —
⚠⚠ **AND THE *-i-* IS THEN DROPPED, as with every `-i` adjective**: *that
unfarandi* gives in the weak *that **unfaranda***, never †*unfarandia*,
the spelling *-ia* being attested for no adjective at all.
**Both rules existed, four hundred lines apart, and did not cite each
other**: in the 30th round one of the two translators found the right
form, the other left the lemma bare for want of knowing which prevailed —
and he reported it as a contradiction in the kit. *There was none: there
were two sound rules with nothing linking them.* Brought together
2026-08-16; **apposed with no determiner, it stays in the bare form**
(mik fulliandi "filling me"), and it is then CONTROLLED BY THE SUBJECT of
its clause; if it must bear on another constituent, recast the clause
rather than move the participle.

Classes: I rīdan-raid; II beudan-baud; III bindan-band; IV neman-nam;
V geban-gaf; VI faran-fōr; VII haldan-hēld.
**Present of the strong verbs, by class** (requisition R2, 2026-08-07) —
the vowel of the singular follows three regimes and one only:
· **I and II**: the long vowel or the diphthong holds — `rīdan` keeps
  its `ī` throughout (rīdis, rīdid); beudan → biudis, **biudid**
  (io → iu before -i-);
⚠⚠ **THIS RAISING HOLDS FOR III(b), IV AND V ONLY.** The two forced grids
of 2026-08-14 saw the assimilation-to-*u* argument spill over onto
**class II** and hesitated between †*biudu* and *beudu*. **It does not
spill**: class II has no root *e*, its diphthong is governed by **L4**
alone — *iu* before *i/j*, **eu** elsewhere. Whence **beudu** (no *-i-*),
**biudis/biudit** (a desinential *-i-*), and in the plural **beudam /
biudid / beudand** — the *-i-* of the 2nd person triggers L4, the other
two do not. An unforeseen consequence of the morning's ruling, bounded
the same evening.
· **III(b), IV, V**: **e → i THROUGHOUT THE SINGULAR, ALL THREE
  PERSONS**: *ik **gibu**, thū **gibis**, hē **gibid***, pl.
  *gebam/gebid/geband* — likewise *ik **wirthu***, *ik **nimu***, *ik
  **quithu***, *ik **stilu***, *ik **hilpu***, *ik **wirpu***, *ik
  **bifilhu***. The plural and the infinitive keep the *e*.
  ⚠⚠ **THIS RULE WAS REVERSED ON 2026-08-14, AND THE MOTIVE FOR THE
  REVERSAL MATTERS MORE THAN THE RULE.** The kit had first bounded the
  raising to the 2nd/3rd persons, on the ground that its cause was the
  *-i-* of the ending, absent from the 1st (\*-ō) — **and it had settled
  the matter by counting ITS OWN CORPUS** (30 forms in -e against 5 in
  -i). ⚠ **A corpus one has produced oneself witnesses nothing**: that is
  a circularity, and it held for two days.
  **Instruction carried out at rank (a) alone**, by three independent
  enquiries: (1) the current reference handbook —
  Braune/Reiffenstein, *Althochdeutsche Grammatik* §30 c, 15th ed. —
  treats the *i* of *hilfu, nimu, gibu* as a **regular phonetic
  assimilation to the *u* of the following syllable**, in the same list as
  *filu* and *sibun*; the analogical position is that of Braune 1886,
  **which Braune himself abandoned as early as 1891**, and which only
  Joesten 1931 still holds;
  (2) rank (a) attests six 1st sg. in *i* across three class V verbs
  (*giuo*, *giuon*, *quitho*, *siho* in the Williram) and ***uuirthon***
  in the Wachtendonck psalter, Ps 68:18 — **not one form in *e* in that
  slot**; (3) ⚠ **Kögel explains the aberrant *i* of Old Saxon (*fihu*,
  *uuirdscipi*, *miluk*) by a *niederfränkische Beeinflussung***: he
  therefore presupposes that Low Franconian carried the raising before
  *u*, strongly enough to contaminate its neighbour. It is the only
  explicit position taken by a first-rank philologist on LOW FRANCONIAN in
  this debate, and it gives *gibu*.
  ⚠ **The weak point is still named**: raising before *-u* is CONTINENTAL
  (OHG *nimu*, OS *biru*) against the insular and Frisian forms (*ic
  beru*, *ik bere*), and Salian is the coastal fringe. There remain too
  the exceptions *ebur*, *metu*, *ernust*, the words in *-unga*, and the
  wavering *fehu/fihu*: if there is a law, it is not without flaw.
  **Certainty ~90 %, and what would close it is named** — Joesten 1931,
  not to be found online.
  In the 2nd/3rd: werthan → wirthid, neman → nimid, geban → gibid,
  quethan → quithid, stelan → stilid, geldan → **gildid**;

· **VI and VII**: **NO change**, neither umlaut nor closing — faran →
  farid, standan → standid, haldan → haldid, fallan → fallid, slahan →
  slahid, fāhan → fāhid. Settled 2026-08-05 on 28 occurrences of the
  corpus, without one exception. **REASON** (re-motivated 2026-08-07,
  requisition R33): it is not that L5 fails to apply — it applies
  everywhere else to a desinential -i, all through the noun (gast →
  gesti, and the whole `-i` series with it). It is that **Low Franconian LEVELLED the verbal
  paradigm by analogy on the infinitive and the plural**, where Old Saxon
  keeps ferid, heldid. Dutch still carries the result: hij vaart, hij
  houdt, hij vangt. The levelling is paradigmatic, not phonetic — whence
  the noun keeping its umlaut where the verb loses it.
⚠⚠ **PLURAL OF THE PRESENT — THREE DISTINCT PERSONS** (reversed
2026-08-14 on a double enquiry at rank (a)):

· **1st pl. in -am**: gebam, wertham, faram · hauriam · wardōm · **hebbiam**;
· **2nd pl. in -id**: gebid, werthid, farid · hauriad · wardōd · **hebbiad**;
· **3rd pl. in -and**: geband, werthand, farand · hauriand · wardōnd · **hebbiand**.

The **plural imperative is the 2nd-person form**: *gebid!*, *nemid!*,
*sittiad!* — rank (a) attests it in series (*geuet, quethet, spreket,
uuirkit, cumit, fangit*).
⚠⚠ **THE KIT PRESCRIBED A SINGLE PLURAL IN -at, AND THAT WAS THE OLD
SAXON SYSTEM.** Low Franconian distinguishes all three persons, and the
opposition **2nd ≠ 3rd is DIRECTLY ATTESTED** in the Wachtendonck
psalter, in glosses where the Latin is explicit: *anafallit* "irruitis"
and *uuânit gi* "suspicamini" (2 pl.) against *macunt* "concinnant",
*scauuont* "respiciunt" (3 pl.). **Not one *-at* in the whole Low
Franconian corpus.**
⚠ **AND THE HYPOTHESIS OF AN INGVAEONIC SALIAN DOES NOT SAVE *-at***,
tempting though it was to invoke it, Salian being the coastal fringe: the
single plural **presupposes the Ingvaeonic nasal-spirant law in the
ending** (\*-anþ > \*-āþ), and Dutch, **coastal Dutch included, NEVER
applied it to that ending, at any period**. The raw material does not
exist. The one coastal verbal witness — *hebban*, *umbidan uue* (*Hebban
olla vogala*, West Flemish, ~1100) — gives the Franconian system.
⚠ **One does NOT write *-unt*** (the psalter's form): its ⟨u⟩ is a
10th-century weakening of an unstressed syllable. At 550 the vowel is
full — the same reasoning as for the dative plural *-um* maintained
against the *-un* of that same psalter.
⚠ The 1st person is the weak slot: it rests on **one doubtful token** in
Low Franconian and on comparison. The 2nd and 3rd are attested.
**Preterites that were missing** (requisition R3): kuman → **quam,
quāmum, kuman**; standan → **stōd, stōdum, gastandan**; geldan →
**gald, guldum, gagoldan**; sittian → sat, sātum, gasetan.
⚠ **Citation convention** (requisition R37): the preterite plural is
always given in the **1st person, in -um** (gābum, bārum, nāmum), not in
the 3rd in -un. The first drafting of this ruling cited -un and
contradicted the whole dictionary. **The k-/qu- alternation of kuman is
not a variant**: it is conditioned by the FOLLOWING VOWEL (reformulated
2026-08-07, requisition R34). The labial element of \*kwemaną survives
before a front or low vowel — \*kwam → **quam**, \*kwēmun → **quāmun**,
\*kwimidi → **quimid** — whence the **imperative quim!** (class IV, the
imperative vowel in -i- as in *neman → nim!*), and not †kum!; the kit did
not give the form and the two translators of the 4th test diverged on it,
one reasoning by the class and being right — and it is absorbed before a
high rounded vowel, where labiality is already carried by the vowel:
\*kwum- → **kum-**, whence kuman and gakuman. Never †kwam, †quman or
†kumit.
**Participle of kuman**: the simplex **kuman** is the usual one (hē was
kuman); gakuman exists and is not a fault (arbitrated R35).
⚠⚠ **TWO CLASSES IN -ian, TWO 3rd PERSONS — DO NOT CONFUSE THEM**
(2026-08-11). The kit carried both patterns without saying what separates
them, and two translators diverged on *tellid* / *telit*:
· **STRONG with a j-present** (liggian, sittian, biddan…) → 3sg on the
  **SIMPLE** stem: *ligid*, *sitid*, *bidit*;
· **WEAK class I** (seggian, erian, haurian, laidian, **tellian**,
  dailian, dōmian…) → 3sg on the **GEMINATE** stem: *seggid*, *erid*,
  *haurid*, *laidid*, ***tellid***. ⚠⚠ **AND `seggian` IS WHERE THIS RULE MEETS ITS OWN EXCEPTION.** The same verb is filed in the annexe of `hebbian` a few thousand lines above, which yields `segid` and not the geminate — and it is `segid` that the dictionary prints. See the register of slots, where the collision is declared open. *The criterion here is the CLASS; the trouble is that this verb has two.*

The criterion is the verb's CLASS, not its ending: both series are
written -ian in the infinitive, and that is the whole trap. A verb whose
preterite is in **-da** (*talda*, *sagda*, *haurda*) is WEAK, hence
geminate; a verb with a strong preterite (*lag*, *sat*, *bad*) is STRONG,
hence simple. **The preterite tells the class.**

#### The j-presents

**j-presents** (a sub-class of the strong verbs: liggian, sittian,
biddan, heffian, hlahhian, skeppian, swerian) — the -j- geminated in the
present in Germanic and left a short stem: 3sg **-id** on the SIMPLE stem
(liggian → **ligid**, sittian → **sitid**, biddan → bidit). Requisition
R39.
⚠⚠ **THE PLURAL IS NOT UNIFORMLY GEMINATE, AND THE KIT WROTE IT WRONG
TWICE** (double enquiry at rank (a), 2026-08-16). It carried *sittiad*
and *liggiad*: an ending *-ad*, which is the single Old Saxon plural the
kit had itself set aside on 2026-08-14. **A residue of that migration,
left standing for two days.** And the geminate itself falls in the 2nd
person.
· **The distribution is PHONOLOGICAL, not morphological**: the \*j fell
  before \*i, so West Germanic gemination — which that \*j triggers — did
  not occur where the ending begins with *i*.
· Whence, ending by ending: **1 pl. -am → GEMINATE stem** (*sittiam*,
  *biddiam*) · **2 pl. -id → SIMPLE stem** (*sitid*, *bidid*) · **3 pl.
  -and → GEMINATE stem** (*sittiand*, *biddand*) · **imper. sg. -i →
  SIMPLE stem** (*siti*, *bidi*) · **pres. part. → GEMINATE stem**
  (*biddandi*).
· Rank (a) attests it directly: Old Dutch has **1 pl. *biddun*
  (geminate) against 2 pl. *bidit* (simple)**, and likewise *willun*
  against *willit*.
⚠⚠ **PRESENT SUBJUNCTIVE OF THE STRONG j-PRESENTS: GEMINATE STEM,
WITHOUT EXCEPTION** — *sittie, biddie, swerie*; pl. *sittien, biddien,
swerien*. The slot was missing entirely. **This is not a choice, it is a
mechanical consequence**: the optative ending is \*-ai-, which begins
with *a*, so the \*j is protected there in EVERY person — there exists no
environment where the subjunctive could fall on the simple stem. Checked
against Gothic, which has neither gemination nor loss of \*j:
*bidjau, bidjais, bidjai, bidjaima, bidjaiþ, bidjaina* — the *-j-* is
everywhere. Four witnesses agree, no divergence.
⚠ **AND *swerian* NEVER HAS A GEMINATE — worth writing down, or it looks
like an intruder**: *r* does not take part in West Germanic gemination.
With it the opposition is therefore not *r ~ rr* but **the presence or
absence of the -i-**: *swerid* (2 pl., simple) against *swerie* (subj.).
Strong preterite of class VI: *swōr / swōrun*, subj. pret. *swōri /
swōrin*.
⚠⚠ **NO TRANSMITTED FRANKISH OATH SETTLES IT — AND WHAT IT SAYS IS WORTH
MORE.** The two enquirers searched separately: the malberg glosses of the
*Pactus legis Salicae* are almost all NOMINAL, and the only Frankish
verbal sentence in the Merovingian corpus is the manumission formula
***maltho thi afrio lito*** "I declare: I set you free, litus"
(tit. XXVI). **And *maltho* and *afrio* are 1 sg. present INDICATIVES.**
> **THE FRANKISH PERFORMATIVE FORMULA IS IN THE INDICATIVE. The
> subjunctive serves reported speech — "let him swear" — not the act
> itself.**
That is a gain in legal register, not a gap. And the formula confirms in
passing the 1st sg. in *-o/-u*.
⚠ **THE SLOT IS DECIDED BY THE SOUND LAW, NOT BY THE WITNESS** — and that
is licit here because the law is unambiguous. It is not always so: see
the bound placed the same day on L2, where the witness is missing AND the
law leaks.

#### The weak verbs

**The preterite 2 sg. of the weak verbs in `-es`** — *thū **wardōdes***,
*thū **erides*** — **the slot was missing entirely**, and the two
translators of the 10th test (2026-08-09) worked around it by switching
to the perfect for want of a recorded ending; wardōn-wardōda (1sg wardo,
**3sg wardōd**, pl. **wardōm/wardōd/wardōnd**); **hebbian** (RESIDUAL
class III): pres. **hebbiu, hebis, hebid**, pl.
**hebbiam/hebbiad/hebbiand**; pret. **habda**, pl. *habdum*; part.
**gahabd**. ⚠ 1 sg. **hebbiu**, not †`hebbu` (both enquiries of
2026-08-19). ⚠⚠ The infinitive †`habēn` and the plural
†`habēm/habēd/habēnd` are **abrogated** — see the ruling below.

⚠⚠ ***willian* — THE WHOLE PARADIGM, AND ITS HARD SLOT** (double enquiry
at rank (a), 2026-08-16). The kit had only *williu / wili*.
· pres. ind.: **williu / wilis / wili — william / wilid / williand**;
· pres. subj.: **willie / willies / willie — willien**;
· pret. ind.: **wolda / woldōs / wolda — woldum / woldud / woldun**;
· pret. subj.: **woldi / woldis / woldi — woldīn**;
· pres. part. **williandi** (a useful adjective in law: "consenting").
⚠⚠ **THE 3 SG. IS *wili*, BARE, NEVER †*wilid*.** This is the hard
structural point, and it admits no regularisation: **the entire present
of this verb is a frozen former OPTATIVE**, and Gothic shows it naked
(*wiljau, wileis, wili, wileima, wileiþ, wileina* — optative endings, not
indicative; cf. Lat. *velim*). The verb never had a present indicative of
its own. Five independent languages keep the bare final — Goth. *wili*,
OS *wili*, Old Low Franconian *wil*, OE *wile*, OHG *will*.
**A table that produced †*wilid* would be wrong in a way any Germanist
would see at a glance.**
⚠ **THE PRETERITE IS *wolda*, NOT *welda* — THE MOST VISIBLE MARKER
AGAINST SAXON.** Rank (b) has the *e* vocalism (*welda, weldun*); rank
(a) attests *wolda, woldōs, woldon*, an analogical vocalism from
*skulda*/*solda*. The hierarchy settles it on its own. *One enquirer gave
the slot as undecidable at 50 %, the other for* wolda *at 88 %: it is the
rank, not the confidence, that decides.*
⚠ **NEITHER IMPERATIVE NOR PAST PARTICIPLE, AND THESE ARE STRUCTURAL
GAPS, NOT GAPS OF TRANSMISSION.** A former optative has no imperative —
none is attested in Gothic, OS, Old Low Franconian or OE, and it is
semantically expected: one does not order someone to want. For "be
willing", use the subjunctive *willie*. The past participle is a Middle
Dutch and Middle High German creation: **the slot stays empty**, and
*willian* is simply excluded from the periphrastic perfect.
⚠ **The subjunctive *willie* IS NOT the old optative**: that one was
absorbed into the indicative, and an entirely new subjunctive was rebuilt
on the geminate stem — a SECOND optative on top of a present that is
already one. It is therefore on the same stem as *biddie* and *swerie*:
**one rule covers all three.**
⚠ **CONTRACTED NEGATION**: *ne + willian* gives OS *nellian*, 3 sg. *ni
wil*. The contraction is pan-West-Germanic, hence probable at 550; rank
(a) attests nothing certain. **Slot declared, not proven.**
⚠ **A RULE THE KIT DOES NOT HAVE, AND WHICH IS NOT IMPORTED PIECEMEAL**:
Low Franconian raises *o* to *u* before an *i* in the following syllable
(*gold* / *guldin*), which would give pret. subj. *wuldi*. The kit writes
*woldi* **and declares the divergence** — adopting the rule would be a
general promulgation, not an adjustment on one verb. *That is exactly the
fault golden rule no. 12 names.*
⚠⚠ **THE PLURAL OF THE WEAK PRETERITE — THE SLOT WAS MISSING ENTIRELY**
(double enquiry at rank (a), 2026-08-16; both give the same endings):
**-um / -ud / -un**, in all three classes:
*haurdum / haurdud / haurdun* · *wardōdum / wardōdud / wardōdun* ·
*habdum / habdud / habdun*.
⚠⚠ **ON THE SYNCOPATED STEM *haurd-*, AND THIS IS A RECTIFICATION MADE
THE SAME EVENING.** I had first written †*hauridum*, keeping the vowel of
the suffix — hence producing *haurda* in the 3rd sg. and †*hauridun* in
the plural, **that is, the exact incoherence both enquirers had warned
against** ("whoever writes *haurida* writes *hauridun*, never
*haurdun*"). The kit has long promulgated *haurian-**haurda***, and uses
it as a diagnostic of class ("the preterite tells the class"); rank (a)
and rank (b) alike syncopate after a long root. **So the syncopated stem
is kept throughout.** *A paradigm written in one block forces a decision
where a scattered rule lets things diverge: that is what it is for.*
⚠ **The 2 sg. stays *-es* (*haurdes*, *habdes*, *wardōdes*), and the
divergence is declared**: rank (a) rather gives *-os* (Old Du
*hōrdos*), rank (b) wavers between *-es* and *-os*. The kit carries *-es*
with four worked examples since the 10th test; one does not migrate a
slot on a difference of one unstressed vowel without instruction, but one
writes down that one knows.
⚠ **THE VOWEL IS *u*, NOT *i***: in the present the 2 pl. is *-id*, in
the preterite it is *-ud* (Goth. *-uþ*, OHG *-ut*). The opposition is old
and yields a useful distinction free of charge.
⚠⚠ **THE PRETERITE DOES NOT SPLIT LIKE THE PRESENT: 1st AND 3rd PL. FALL
TOGETHER, ONLY THE 2nd STAYS DISTINCT.** The 3 pl. NEVER had a \*-nþi,
whence the structural absence of the *-nd* one has in the present. A
control witness at rank (a), the strong verb *singan*: present
**ternary** *singun / singit / singunt*, preterite **binary** *sungon /
sungot / sungon*.

⚠ **AND THE 2 PL. OF THE PRETERITE IS EXTRAPOLATED, NOT RECORDED.** Both
enquirers say so separately: the Wachtendonck psalter addresses God, hence
the singular, and the form is all but unfindable in the real Old Dutch
corpus. *Anyone who presents it as attested is cutting corners.* The slot
is carried as **reconstructed**.
⚠ **THE SINGLE *-un* OF OLD SAXON IS SET ASIDE, AND THAT IS A DECISION,
NOT A DATUM.** Rank (b) levels all three persons, in the strong verbs as
in the weak. Setting it aside is consistent with rejecting the single
*-at* of the present — but **it is setting the second witness aside twice
running on the same point**, and that must be known.
⚠⚠ **TWO ADMISSIONS ABOUT THESE FORMS, MADE BY BOTH ENQUIRERS UNASKED.**
(1) ***haurida* IS UNSYNCOPATED**: the weak I verbs with a long root
syncopate in Saxon as in Dutch — *haurda*, *sōkta*, *dailda* —, only the
short roots keeping the vowel (*nerida*, *frumida*). The full form is
defensible at 550, syncope being later, but **it is a choice, and it must
stay consistent: whoever writes *haurida* writes *hauridun*, never
*haurdun*.** (2) ⚠⚠ **THE WEAK CLASS III — RULING OF 2026-08-19.** Double
enquiry. **It is not dissolved: it is EMPTIED of five members out of six,
and the three it was missing are put back into it.**

**THERE ARE TWO OBJECTS BEARING THE SAME NAME, and I had taken one for
the other:**

| | "class III" of rank (c) | "class III" of ranks (a) and (b) |
|---|---|---|
| size | **open, productive**, hundreds of verbs | **CLOSED, exactly four** |
| contents | deadjectival statives/inchoatives (*rōtēn*, *altēn*) | **to have, to say, to live, to think** |
| present | in `-ē-` throughout: *habēs, habēt* | in **`-j-` + gemination**: *hebbiu, hebbiad* |
| preterite | `-ēta` | **the dental suffix DIRECTLY**, ungeminated root: *habda, sagda, libda, hugda* |

**My class III mixed the two: the label and the contents came from rank
(c), and the one preterite that was right — `habda` — belonged to the
other.** Rated a borrowing of the letter from rank (c) at **92 % and
95 %**.
⚠ **And ranks (a) and (b) agree here against rank (c) ALONE**: there was
not even an arbitration to make between my first two witnesses.

**THE ANNEXE, CLOSED, FOUR MEMBERS, NO ADDITION EVER:**
`hebbian` "to have" · `seggian` "to say" · `libbian` "to live" ·
`huggian` "to think" (the kit carried only the prefixed `gahuggian`).

⚠⚠ **AND THREE OF THE FOUR WERE ALREADY IN THE LEXICON WITH THE EXACT
FORMS** — `sagda`/`gasagd`, `libbit`/`libda`, `gahugda` — **without the
kit knowing they were a class.** The only one badly written is the
commonest verb in the language: †`habēn`, a High German form, replaced by
**`hebbian`** (90 % and 96 %).
  *The annexe was there. It lacked its name, and the verb "to have" was
  outside it.*

**THE FIVE THAT LEAVE**, all to class II in `-ōn` / `-ōda` — the
stative-inchoatives go there at ranks (a) and (b), rank (c) alone keeping
`-ēn` (88 % and 93 %):
`swīgōn` (only the **label** was wrong: translator B of the 38th round
had seen it before the enquirers) · `magrōn` · `raudōn` · `haltōn`
(⚠ **and this one is ATTESTED at rank (a)**, which moves it from
conjectured to supported) · and †`werēn`, **dissolved** — its sense
returns to `wārōn`, which already carried it.

⚠⚠ **TWO FORMS WERE INVENTED**, and the mechanism deserves naming:
†`raudda` (a geminate dental in a weak preterite, which exists at no rank
at all) and †`halta` (a class I preterite on a verb that is not one). And
†`werēda`, †`magrēda` are OHG *werēta*, *magarēta* **with the second
sound shift undone by hand**.
  *That is the mechanism by which the false class got in: I took a
  handbook of rank (c) and un-shifted the consonants. Law L7 protects
  against the shift; it does not protect against borrowing the paradigm
  that carries it.*

⚠ **THE INTERNAL CHECK THAT SHOULD HAVE WARNED ME**: six verbs of one
class made **five different preterites** — `habda`, `werēda`, `raudda`,
`halta`, `swīgōda`. *A living morphological class does not shatter like
that. It was not a class, it was a basket into which five distinct
problems had fallen, each solved separately at the moment it was
written.*


⚠⚠ **OPEN SLOT, NOT SETTLED — the MIXED paradigm.** The two enquiries
diverge, at 75 % each, and **one does not average**. The second holds
that Proto-West-Germanic was **heterogeneous** — `*habbju` with `-j-`,
but `*habēs`, `*habēþ` without — and that **each language levelled later,
in a different direction**: rank (b) on the root (*hebbes*), rank (a) on
the endings (*hevis*), rank (c) on the `-ē-` (*habēs*). **At 550 none of
those three levellings would yet have taken place**, and the form would
be *hebbiu / habēs / habēd*.
  ⚠⚠ **If that hypothesis is true, my "contradiction" was none: they were
  the two halves of a single paradigm.**
  **The kit lays down the LEVELLED form by default**, for three recorded
  reasons: the three relics already in the lexicon carry it (`libbit`,
  not †`libēd`) · the corpus writes it (`hebis`, `hebit`, never
  †`habēd`) · and the second enquiry itself marks its mixed 3rd sg.
  †`libēd`, †`hugēd` with a **(?) CONJECTURED**. *A default backed by
  practice against a hypothesis backed by a conjecture — but the rating
  is 75 against 75, and the slot stays OPEN.*

⚠ **SIGNAL RECEIVED, NOT ACTED ON**: one enquiry rates at 60 % that the
3rd sg. ought to be in `*-iþ` and not `-id` at 550, the shift `þ > d`
completing in the 8th–9th century. **That would touch the entire verbal
system**; it is not handled in passing inside a ruling about one class.
Recorded to be instructed separately.

⚠ **AND A RESERVATION ON MY OWN RANK (a)**, which one enquiry enters and
I retain: the language of the Wachtendonck psalters is not pure Low
Franconian — the surviving text is thought to rest on a partly relexified
Middle Franconian model. **Consequence: a form in `-ēn` found in
Wachtendonck would be suspect of being a residue of the model, not a Low
Franconian fact.** Rank (a) is not homogeneous, and one day the ONW will
have to be distinguished from the psalters within it. Strong verb with a
j-present, liggian (V): 3sg ligid, pret. lag.

#### The irregulars, the contracted verbs and the preterite-presents

**werthan (III), present**: ik **wirthu**, thū wirthis, hē **wirthid**,
pl. **wertham/werthid/werthand** — the *th* of the present against the
*d* of the preterite plural and the participle (wurdum, gawordan) is
Verner's law; the old *wirdit* of the founding grammar was a phantom
form, corrected 2026-08-04 on the auditor's requisition (the founder's
2nd correction by the judge).
**The contracted verbs gān "to go" and stān "to stand"** — promulgated
2026-07-24, carried into the kit 2026-08-07: ik gān/stān, thū gēs/stēs,
hē **gāt/stāt**, pl. **gām/gād/gānd** and **stām/stād/stānd**.
⚠⚠ **THESE ARE DEFECTIVE PRESENTS, NOT SEPARATE VERBS** (reversed
2026-08-14, double enquiry at rank (a)). The kit had declared them
"separate verbs" — **and it was that decision which deprived them of any
past**, cutting them off from the one preterite available. *gān* and
*stān* **NEVER had a preterite of their own**: this is not a merger to be
dated, it is **a constitutive gap**. The past is supplied by **gangan**
and **standan**.
· **gangan → pret. gēng / gēngi / gēng, pl. gēngum / gēngut / gēngun**;
  part. **gagangan**; imper. **gang!**; subj. pres. **gangē**.
· **standan → pret. stōd / stōdi / stōd, pl. stōdum / stōdut / stōdun**;
  part. **gastandan**; imper. **stand!**
⚠⚠ **THE WHOLE OF CLASS VII IS IN Ē, NOT IN IE** (ruling of 2026-08-15,
double enquiry at rank (a)): **hēld, fēld, fēl, hēt, lēt, rēd, slēp,
blēs, fēng, hēng, gēng** — and the preterite plural has the **same
vowel** as the singular, the class never having had a sg./pl. ablaut.
· ⚠ **AND CLASS VII SPLITS INTO TWO SERIES, OF DIFFERENT ORIGIN.** The
  kit treated them as one. **Series A** — present in *a/ā/ai*: the
  contraction of the reduplication gives **ē²**, whence the eleven forms
  above. **Series B** — present in *au/ō*: the contraction gives **the
  diphthong \*eu**, which has NOTHING to do with ē² and then follows the
  fate of every inherited \*eu, hence **our L4**: *hlaupan* → **hleup**,
  *hrōpan* → **hreup**, *blōtan* → **bleut**, *wōpian* → **weup** (and no
  *iu* in the preterite: the alternation *eu ~ iu* is a fact of the
  present alone). The proof of the split is distributional — OS *hēt,
  geng* against *hriop, wiopun*; OHG *hialt* against *liof*.
⚠⚠⚠ **THE JUSTIFICATION OF THIS RULING HAS BEEN REWRITTEN: THE DATING
DOES NOT CARRY IT.** The note said "the diphthongisation ē² > ie is of the
8th–9th c.". **That is disputed**: Quak 1997 (*Geschiedenis van de
Nederlandse taal*, §2.1) dates the change to the **6th century** and makes
it a **defining** criterion of Old Dutch, citing the class VII preterite
by name. Braune §35 describes the opposite on dated manuscripts (the
monophthong still the rule in the 8th c.). **The two enquirers diverged,
and the second retracted mid-enquiry.** Our date falls exactly on the
seam. *This slot therefore does not rest on an established fact: it rests
on a CHOICE, and it must be owned as one.*

· ⚠ **WHAT DECIDES IS THE KIT'S INTERNAL CONSISTENCY, not the date** —
  as with *gensi*, where it was L5 that decided. **ō > uo and ē² > ie are
  one and the same diphthongisation** (the *althochdeutsche
  Diphthongierung*), attested as such in the same corpus (*fluot* and
  *hiera*). And the kit **refuses** *uo* (it writes *ō*), **refuses** *ie*
  < eu (it writes *eu* by L4), and **refuses** the weakened finals (it
  writes *-un*, not *-on*). To write *hield* would be to take **one layer
  of the 9th century and three of the 6th in a single word**. If one wants
  *hield*, consistency obliges one to write *guod*, *bruother*, *fluot*.
· ⚠ What is no longer defensible, in either case: saying that *ie* would
  be **High German**. Rank (a) attests it in its own right (*giengi*,
  Ps 67:8; *behielt*, 54:17; *fiel*, 54:5), outside the dense Middle
  Franconian zone of Ps 1–3. Those forms are not suspect — they are
  merely **of a different storey from ours**.
⚠ **NOR DOES ONE WRITE †*stuont/stont***: a **double** anachronism — the
nasal of the preterite is a **later Franconian analogy** (Gothic, OE, OS
and Norse all have *stōd* without a nasal), and *uo* follows the
diphthongisation. The *stunt*/*obstuont* of the psalter are in any case in
**Ps 1–3, the MIDDLE FRANCONIAN portion** that van Helten, Quak and Köbler
exclude from Low Franconian. Middle Dutch still keeps ***stoet***, the
direct continuator of *stōd*.
⚠ **No WEAK preterite** (†*gāda*): nothing supports it anywhere in the Low
Franconian area, at any period.
⚠ **And the paradigm of 550 is LONGER than that of the 10th century**: in
the Low Franconian core the contracted root occupies only the infinitive
and the imperative; the subjunctive, the plural and the participles stay
in *gang-/stand-*. Keep the double infinitive **gān ~ gangan** and the
imperative **gang!** beside **gā!**, as Middle Dutch still does (*ganc*,
*stant*). They therefore keep their full conjugation (gangid, standid).
The two series coexist, the contracted one being the usual in the present.
### ⚠⚠⚠ RULING ON `wesan` — THE 3rd SG. IS `ist` (2026-08-26)

**Double enquiry, two independent enquirers, corpora stripped by script.**
They converge, and they converge against what the kit was writing
everywhere in its examples.

> **3rd sg.: `ist`.** Rated **70 %** and **80 %**.

⚠⚠ **RANK (a) IS UNANIMOUS, AND IT ALONE DECIDES.** The Wachtendonck
psalter, exhaustive count, two different editions: **`ist` 36 times**
(Cowan 1957) and **38 times** (Heyne 1867) — **`is` ZERO TIMES.** The only
`is` the literature knows is a **conjecture by van Helten in a lacuna**
(Ps 67:7), which Cowan restores as `ist`.

**THE ARGUMENT THAT DECIDES IS THE DIRECTION OF THE CHANGE.** The `-t` is
**inherited** — \*h₁és-**ti** > Lat. *est*, Gk. *estí*, Goth. *ist*, OHG
*ist*, Old Low Franconian *ist*. **The form without `-t` is the
innovation**, and its distribution is **coastal and northern**: OS `is`,
OFris `is`, ON `es`. And this kit defines itself as the West Germanic
**"INLAND"**, not Ingvaeonic. *An Ingvaeonic innovation first attested in
830 among the Saxons has no title to be projected back onto Frankish of
550.*

⚠⚠⚠ **AND THE FINEST CHECK IS THE SECOND ENQUIRER'S**: he separated the
Middle Franconian layer of the psalter (Ps 1–3) from the Low Franconian
layer (Ps 18–73). **Of 38 `ist`, 37 are in the Low Franconian part.**
`ist` is therefore not a residue of the model: it is the form of the
transposer himself — who writes `that`, `ik`, `thu`, `inde` throughout,
never `daz`, `ih`, `du`, `unde`. *Had he said `is`, he had everything he
needed to write it.*

**THE CONTRACTION FOLLOWS.** In the three languages that contract, it
follows the form of the simple verb exactly — OHG and Gothic give `ist` →
`nist`; Old Saxon gives `is` → `nis`. No mismatch anywhere.

---


#### ⚠⚠ WHAT THIS RULING COST, AND IT MUST BE SAID

The kit was writing **`is`** in every worked example, and **`nis`** as its
recorded contraction. **Fifteen occurrences were migrated** — and the
kit's internal consistency, which seemed to plead for `is`, in fact
pleaded only for **the Saxon witness**, on which it had aligned itself without
knowing.

    *A kit consistent with the wrong witness is consistent.*

⚠ **A fault of person found while migrating**: the kit wrote †*ik nis
thes wirthig* "I am not worthy" — a **1st**-person subject with a
**3rd**-person contraction. Corrected to *ik ni bim thes wirthig*.

---

#### ⚠⚠⚠ WHAT THE TWO ENQUIRERS REFUSE TO PROMULGATE

**① THE WESTERN FRINGE IS NOT COVERED.** Wachtendonck is localised **near
Aachen** — the Meuse end of the area. **There is no attestation of the
copula in western Old Low Franconian before ~1100.** The first enquirer
therefore rates it **80 % in the east and 58 % in the west**: *"Tournai is
not Maastricht."*
⚠ The project explicitly aims at the **common ancestor of Old Dutch AND
Ripuarian, inland** — which is exactly Wachtendonck's position, and that
is what authorises the ruling. But the isogloss crosses the area, and
**both of them declare it**.

**② THE VERY EXISTENCE OF A CONTRACTION IS NOT ATTESTED AT RANK (a).**
Wachtendonck writes **`ne ist` in two words, 5 or 6 times out of 6**, and
never contracted. ⚠ But it is a **word-for-word interlinear gloss**: it
renders *non est* by two words because the Latin has two. **Its zero is a
zero of genre, not of language.** The kit's `nist` is therefore
**derived, never attested**.
⚠⚠ And the second enquirer puts the opposite hypothesis, which he cannot
refute: *the proclitic position after `ni` is precisely where a final
`-t` falls first* — so `nis` could be **older** than `is`. **Slot left
open on that point.**

**③ THE 1st AND 2nd PERSONS OF THE PLURAL ARE NOT ATTESTED.** Zero
occurrences at rank (a). And the kit WAS WRITING `sind/sind/sind` until
2026-08-26 — a **syncretism that is a feature of the *Heliand***, whereas
rank (a) **splits its verbal plurals** (`-un` / `-it` / `-unt`). *It would
be inconsistent for Low Franconian to have the unified plural for this
verb alone.*
⚠ **DECLARED OPEN SLOT**: the three possible systems (generalised `sint`,
†*birum* / †*birut* (OHG), or weakened †*sīum* / †*sīut* (Goth.)) have
**no form common to two ranks**. Both enquirers refuse to decide, and the
first adds: *"if the project must fill the slot, let it fill it while
marking it unsettled."*

**④ WHAT IS SETTLED AROUND IT** — the two agree:
· **1 sg. `bim`**, not `bin` (the shift `-m` > `-n` is not complete until
  after 900) · **2 sg. `bist`**, with a variant `bis` still alive before
  the enclitic `thu` · **3 pl. `sint`** · **subj. `sī`** · **inf.
  `wesan`**, pret. `was` / `wārun`.
· **The suppletion boundary**: `b-` in the 1st and 2nd sg. of the present
  indicative **only**; `s-` in the 3rd sg., the plural and the
  subjunctive; `w-` outside the present. ⚠ The plural in `b-` (†*birum*,
  †*birut*, OHG) is **confined to Old High German**: do not project it.

⚠⚠ **AND THE TWO `-t` HAVE NOTHING TO DO WITH EACH OTHER.** The one in
the 3rd sg. is inherited; the one in `bist` is **secondary**, born of the
enclisis of \*þū — which rank (a) still shows in action (`bis thu` ~
`bistu`) and which the baptismal vow shows in the pure state
(`gelobis-tu`). **Internal proof: the *Heliand* lost the `-t` of the 3rd
sg. and kept the one of the 2nd, 24 times out of 24.** One and the same
final consonant cannot both fall and stay in the same language. *Nothing
decided about the 3rd sg. constrains the 2nd.*

⚠⚠ **`is` IS NOT A HOMOGRAPH OF THE COPULA.** `is` is the 3rd-person
possessive; the 3rd sg. of `wesan` is **`ist`**. No collision — do not try
to tell them apart by position.

**Irregulars**: wesan (**bim / bist / ist** — ✓ *settled by the double enquiry of 2026-08-26, see the ruling above*),
pret. was — pl. wārum/wārut/wārun, subj. sī; the whole paradigm is at QUATRE PARADIGMES); witan-wait; magan-mag;
skulan-skal;
⚠ **2nd PERSON OF THE PRETERITE-PRESENTS: ending -t, not -s** — *thū
**waist***, *thū **maht***, *thū **skalt***, *thū **mōst***; it is the
class's own mark, inherited from the old perfect, and it surprises
because every other verb has -s. The line was missing from the tables
entirely; both translators of the 8th independence test (2026-08-09)
wrote *waist thū* and both entered it as a doubt.
willian-williu/wili; dōn: pres. **dōm/dōs/dōt**, pl. **dōm/dōd/dōnd** — ⚠ **the 1st sg. and the 1st pl. are HOMOPHONES** (*dōm*),
pl. dedum, part. gadān.
**Preterite-presents — their preterites** (promulgated 2026-08-04; the
table gave none and forced the fault): magan → **mahta**, witan →
**wissa**, skulan → **skolda**, willian → **wolda**, thurfan →
**thorfta**, aigan → **aihta**, mōtan → **mōsta**, unnan → **unda**;
subj. pret. **mahti, wissi, skoldi, woldi, thorfti, aihti, mōsti, undi**.
⚠⚠ **THIS LIST WAS INCOMPLETE ON BOTH SIDES, AND IT IS THE INVENTORY OF
SLOTS THAT SAW IT** (17th control, 2026-08-18): it gave **six**
preterites and **four** subjunctives, for **eight** verbs. `mōtan` and
`unnan` were missing in the preterite; `thorfti`, `aihti`, `mōsti` and
`undi` in the subjunctive. Translator A of the 36th round had found
`unnan` by needing it for a sentence; **`mōtan` was found by nobody**,
because the round's text had no use for it.

> *That is exactly what an inventory is for: a translator sees only the
> slots he MEETS. A list of six where eight are needed does not denounce
> itself — it looks like a list.*
pl. of the present **of the preterite type, split -um/-ut/-un** (witum/witut/witun, mugum/mugut/mugun, skulum/skulut/skulun) — the preterite-presents have the endings of the PRETERITE, not those of the present.
**werthan as copula** + predicative adjective: "to become" (sān wurdun mī thia
wandlunga galīka "at once the vicissitudes became indifferent to me")
**ausnahmsloses Gesetz*** ». **No disagreement among specialists.**
⚠ **Sievers’ law creates NO split here**: in West Germanic it
fossilised as **gemination of the root** (*kunni*, *middi*), which stays
in every case — but the desinential *-i-* goes in the short stems and
the long alike (*cunni* → gen. *cunnis*, pl. *cunno*).
⚠ **A reservation kept**: OHG still preserved relics in *-ia/-ie* in
the 8th c. (*festea*, *hornohteo*), and nothing from the 6th documents
the point. †*skaunia* at 550 is not absurd — **it is simply without
support in the Low Franconian domain**, and to write it would be to
choose against the only witness of the area.
⚠ **And the *-iu* of the fem. nom. sg. is NOT thematic**: it is the
PRONOMINAL ending, which the `-a` stems carry too (Braune §250).
The psalter does not have it at all — do not write †*skauniu*.
— does NOT admit an agent in fon + dative, reserved for the passive of
process.


#### The moods — subjunctive and imperative

**PRESENT subjunctive** — the table gave only the preterite. Worked
examples: *that wī te themu stathē kuman **mugin*** "so that we may reach
the shore"; *air than the dag **kumē*** "before the day comes";
***Haldē** gahwilīk sīn rōthar* "let each man hold his oar" (jussive in
the 3rd person, verb first). Formation:
· strong and weak: **1 and 3 sg. -ē** · **2 sg. -ēs** · **-ēn** in the
  pl. (**gebē** / **gebēs** / **gebē** — gebēn) ⚠ all three carry the
  LONG vowel; the 2 sg. **gebēs** was added 2026-08-16, the table having
  promulgated the 1st and the 3rd without saying what became of the 2nd,
  **one single ending for all three persons of the plural**;
· ⚠⚠ **EXCEPT THE VERBS IN -ōn AND -ēn, whose stem already ends in a long
  vowel**: the subjunctive vowel is **absorbed** there, never added
  (†*skauwoe*, †*wardōēn* are impossible). Whence **wardo / wardōs /
  wardo — wardōn**. The plural form is then **homophonous with the
  infinitive**, and context decides — as *dōn* is both infinitive and
  plural subjunctive.
  ⚠⚠ **THE SECOND EXAMPLE, †`habe / habēs / habe — habēn`, IS WITHDRAWN
  2026-08-19: there are no more verbs in `-ēn`.** The ruling on weak class
  III dissolved the category; `hebbian` moved to the closed annexe, whose
  subjunctive is **`hebbie`**, regular for the `-j-` presents. *A rule
  that enumerates its domains ages with them: this one said "the verbs in
  -ōn AND -ēn", and one of the two sets became empty without the sentence
  moving.*
· ⚠⚠ **AND EXCEPT THE WEAK VERBS IN -ian, whose subjunctive is in -ie**:
  **haurie / hauries / haurie — haurien**, with contraction admitted after
  a dental cluster (*festian* → *feste*). The *-i-* of the stem absorbs
  the mood vowel exactly as the long vowel of the *-ōn* verbs does.
  ⚠⚠⚠ **FIFTEEN LINES BELOW, I COMMITTED THE FAULT I HAD JUST NAMED.**
  The warning above says: *adding a rule without removing the old one
  digs a bigger gap than the one it fills*. I had excepted *-ōn* and
  *-ēn* — and **never *-ian***, although the kit already carried "pres.
  subj. of the -ian verbs: -ie (haurie)" and a worked example, *haurie
  mīn frō* "let my lord hear". The two rules lived side by side for an
  hour on **the best-stocked verbal class in the dictionary**, and it was
  the second forced grid that saw it. *A lesson written in the very
  paragraph where it is broken is not a lesson: it is an ornament.*
  ⚠ **That hole was dug on 2026-08-15 by the correction just above**: I
  promulgated *-es/-ēn* without excepting the class the kit already
  excepted in the jussive, and **both forced grids ranked it first** — it
  is the best-stocked verbal class in the dictionary. *Adding a rule
  without removing the old one digs a bigger gap than the one it fills.*
  ⚠ **CONFIRMED AGAINST ME on 2026-08-15.** I thought I had read three
  distinct endings in the psalter — *-an* in the 1st (*antkennan*
  Ps 66:3), *-in* in the 3rd (*uuerthin*, *louin*, *mendin*) — and I was
  about to abrogate the *-ēn*. **The double enquiry demolished the
  reading**: Psalm 66 writes *-an* in the 3rd person as well (*begian*
  66:4 and 66:6, *forthan* 66:8), three verses after *antkennan*. **The
  distribution follows the PSALM, that is the copying campaign, not the
  person** — the psalter confuses *a/e/i* in a final syllable (*thinin*
  31× against *thinan* 5×). Old Saxon likewise has a single plural form
  (Gallée §379, Holthausen §408).
  ⚠ **And *-ēn* is not a High Germanism**: Gallée §379 Anm. 6 and
  Holthausen §408 Anm. 1 both derive the attested *-en* from **\*-ēn**
