  adverbial *air*) — both translators of the 7th test came to it
  separately;
· **tense of the subjunctive in reported speech**: Germanic has no future,
  and the present subjunctive carries what is to come — *that hē umbi thia
  saka **dōmie*** "that he would judge on the matter". The preterite
  subjunctive *dōmdi*, by formal concord with the introducing verb, is
  licit too; the present is sharper;
· **accusative + infinitive after a verb of PERCEPTION**: *nihain man ni
  **sah it kuman*** "no one saw it come", *hē **haurda sia singan*** — a
  common Germanic turn, which the kit attested only for the causative
  *haitan*; both translators of the 11th test used it, each noting that it
  was documented nowhere;
· **the comparative in *than* IS EXTRAPOSED** — it is not a constituent
  governed by the verb, and R28 therefore does not hold it back: *that
  Hildegises akkar thrim stapon braidiro **wāri than hē was, thō ira man
  starf***; keeping it before the verb makes the sentence unreadable, and
  both translators of the 10th test reported it independently;
· **DISTANCE goes into the accusative of extent**: *the sīn land **aina
  rasta** fon thār hebit* "whose lands are a rasta from there". Not to be
  confused with the complement of DIFFERENCE, which is dative (below): the
  difference measures a gap between two compared terms, the extent
  measures a space traversed. Both translators of the 15th test diverged
  here for want of a rule, one writing *aina rasta*, the other *ainero
  rastu ferro*;
· **complement of difference in the DATIVE**: ***thrim stapon** braidiro*
  "three paces broader", *ainemu jāre aldiro* — dative-instrumental,
  written identically on both sides;
· **government of *hrīnan*** "to touch": **accusative** (*thiu haga, thiu
  thena flōd **hrīnid***);
· **government of *sunnian***: the verb suffices to itself — *noh ni
  **sunnida** hē* "and he pleaded no impediment"; do not calque the
  vernacular with †*mit nihaineru sunniu*;
· **to exact an oath**: ***aith niman*** (+ dative of disadvantage) is the
  LEGAL idiom — one takes an oath from someone; ***dwingan*** "to compel"
  carries the violence in the verb and suits when it is the violence one
  means to name. Both are right, they do not say the same thing;
· **presentative** "here is why, here is the reason": ***sus ist thiu
  saka*** = ***nū seggiu ik, hwī*** — Frenkisk has no presentative, these
  two turns stand in for one;
· ***thanne* as a subordinator "when" takes the FINAL verb**, like the
  other subordinators (*thanne man thia airistun stunda ubar thia bruggia
  **līthid***); it was not in the list, and both translators of the 12th
  test built it so while reporting it;
· **to add to a writing**: ***legda thār te*** "he added thereto" — a
  particle turn, built entirely on recorded material; or *hēt auk skrīban*
  "he had it written as well";
· **to hold good for, to be in force**: ***geldan furi*** (+acc) — *that
  selba reht gildid furi that munistri* "the same law holds for the
  abbey"; an extension of the recorded *geldan*, resting on rank (a) (Du
  *gelden voor*) and on the already recorded use *Hwat gildid that beddi?*;
· **more often**: ***mair stundono*** (partitive genitive after *mair*,
  like *mair ubiles*) — ⚠⚠ **AND NO LONGER BECAUSE THE ADVERBIAL
  COMPARATIVE WOULD BE MISSING: THAT CLAUSE IS ABROGATED (2026-08-16,
  double enquiry at rank (a)).** The kit said here "Frenkisk has no regular
  adverbial comparative, †*oftor* would be a forge" — **that was false, and
  it is that prohibition alone that produced the deadlock of both
  translators of the 30th round**. Germanic has the series `-or` / `-ost`,
  inherited and productive (see "THE DEGREE OF THE ADVERB"). *mair
  stundono* remains good, but **as another construction, not as a
  makeshift**; and *oftor* is henceforth a reconstructed form, not a
  forbidden forge;
· **quethan or seggian** to introduce speech: *thō **quath** the grāfio* =
  *thō **sagda** the grāfio*;
· **perfect or preterite** for a completed past fact: *hwī ik air **ni
  skraif*** = *hwī ik air **ni gaskriban hebbiu*** "why I did not write
  earlier". The perfect *hebbian* + participle and the plain preterite are
  both licit; the preterite is drier, the perfect ties the fact to the
  speaker's present;
· **optional article** before a noun of a moment used alone: *air than
  midnaht quāmi* = *air than thiu midnaht quāmi*.
⚠ A language in which two competent writers produce the same text word
for word is not a language. These variants ARE NOT faults and no auditor
is to count them as such.
Impersonal "one" = man (man skal, man sihid — a constant usage of the
corpus). Questions: verb in front, words in hw-. Genitive: proper names
and kinship terms preposed (Godes sunu), phrases postposed (kuning thero
Frankōnō).
Subjunctive: sanctions (geldē hē), wishes (Libbe the kuning!), the
irrealis, reported speech.

⚠⚠⚠ **THE IRREALIS — its forms, entered 2026-08-28.** This list named it without illustrating it, and it is the only one of the four that requires a CONSTRUCTION, not a form.
· **protasis**: `of` + **preterite subjunctive** — *Of ik wissi…* "if I had known…". ⚠⚠ **This is an exception to the rule "`of` as a protasis keeps the indicative"**, which aims at the REAL protasis: the indicative would give as real what a counterfactual declares false. *The kit itself supplies the reason, stated for the neighbouring case: "the question is open, the indicative would close it."*
· **apodosis**: **plain preterite subjunctive** — *ni quāmi ik* "I would not have come", *wāri noh kwik* "would still be alive", *stōdi noh* "would still be standing".
· **variant attested as licit**: `wesan`/`hebbian` pret. subj. + participle — *ni wāri ik kuman*, *gahlosōd habdi*. **The two turns are equivalent**; the plain one is safer.
⚠ **THE LANGUAGE DOES NOT DISTINGUISH** "I would not come" from "I would not have come": a single preterite subjunctive carries both, just as a single preterite carries the imperfect and the simple past.
⚠⚠ **`reported speech` is the other term without an example** — see the ruling on the mood of the indirect question, an open case.
Passive of PROCESS: werthan + part. (wirthid gahaldan "is [being]
preserved"); passive of STATE: wesan + part. (ist gahaldan "is
preserved"); agent = fon + dative.
**Passive in the PERFECT and the PLUPERFECT** (requisition R10,
2026-08-07): the process passive takes **wordan** — the participle of
werthan **without the preverb**: *ga-* is perfectivising, and an
auxiliary carrying its own mark of completion would be pleonastic (Ger.
*ist gegeben **worden***, Du *is gegeven **worden***). The full
participle **gawordan** remains that of the autonomous verb (hē ist
gawordan "he has become"); arbitrated R44, as kuman/gakuman was by R35.
With the auxiliary wesan — ist gageban **wordan** "has been given", was
gageban **wordan** "had been given". The state passive has no need of it:
was gahaldan "was preserved". **The difference is not one of tense but of
aspect**, and it is TESTED (R46):
· compatible with a punctual adjunct (an themu jārē) → **process** (that
  korn was mī gageban wordan "that grain had been given me");
· compatible with **noh** (adv.) "still" → **state** (thiu roda nio ni
  gawerkōd was "the assart had never been worked" — *siu was noh ni
  gawerkōd*).
⚠ The dative of the person is NOT an agent: the agent is *fon* + dative
(the example above has *mī* as recipient, not agent). That is the sole
criterion by which the adjunct is classed there.
**Extraposed finite subordinate clause** (requisition R28): a COMPLETE
clause governed by the verb NEVER counts in the final-verb rule — it is
placed obligatorily after it, in every register, law included, a clause
being unable to embed before what governs it. **The rule aims only at
clauses GOVERNED BY THE VERB**: a relative governed by a NOUN stays in
its place, before the verb of its main clause (Thiu wōstinna **the westar
ligid** ni gildid niowiht). Example: sō that nihain man an twīflē ni **sī**
hwat thiu hōba geldan skal. Licences N9 and N10 concern only
constituents, not clauses.
**Comparison of equality**: correlative **sō … sō** — sō filu sō "as much
as", sō lango sō "as long as", sō hauh that "so high that". **half sō
filu** "half as much" belongs here: it is NOT a Romance calque (which
says "half less/as much" without a correlative) but the common Germanic
turn — Du *half zoveel*, Ger. *halb so viel*.
**gahwilīk + partitive genitive** forms an apposed clause WITHOUT a
relative: fior swīn, **thero gahwilīk** tehan penningō werth ist

⚠⚠ **THE RELATIVE HAS TWO MEMBERS** — ruling of 2026-08-19, which
**REVOKES the rule "NO POSSESSIVE RELATIVE" of 2026-08-11**. That rule
held that *the* is invariable **therefore** that it has no genitive. The
second half does not follow from the first. The particle is indeed
invariable, but it is not the only relative: **the system does mark
case**, and the gap was of my own making.

**① THE INVARIABLE PARTICLE *the*** — subject, direct object, and
unmarked circumstantial relation. It is the commonest member.
· **subject** — *the man, **the** sakid*;
· **direct object** — *that word, **the** hē sprak*;
· **unmarked circumstantial** — *an themu dagē, **the** hē sprak* "the day
  **on which** he spoke": ⚠ **no dative mark at all**, and that is
  regular.
⚠ **The warrants are given by LINE NUMBER, not by letter** — *Heliand*
120 (a relative in `the` whose verb is in the **1st person**: the particle
therefore carries neither case nor person), 1706 (direct object), 587
("the day when", without a dative mark).

**② THE DEMONSTRATIVE INFLECTED IN THE REQUIRED CASE**, alone or followed
by the particle. ⚠ **There is not one new form to create**: it is the
paradigm of the article, already tabulated above — *the/thiu/that · acc.
thena/thia/that · gen. thes/thero/thes · dat. themu/theru/themu · pl.
thia, gen. thero, dat. thēm*.
· **genitive, free relative** — ***thes** hē tharf* "what he needs". ⚠ It
  is the direct relativisation of the example the dictionary **already**
  gives under `thurfan`: *hē tharf braudas*. The turn requires **no
  antecedent**, and it is the most idiomatic.
· **genitive, with a head** — *that word, **thes** hē swīgōda* "the word
  he kept silent about" — likewise, `swīgōn` + genitive is already in the
  dictionary (*hē swīgōda thes wordas*).
· **dative** — *the man, **themu** thū hauris* "the man whom you obey".
· **accusative** — the inflected demonstrative, reinforceable by the
  particle; the project's own corpus already writes it (see below).
⚠ **The warrants, again by line number** — *Heliand* 2639-2641:
antecedent in the genitive, relative in the genitive, and inside the
relative a verb **that governs the genitive**. ⚠⚠ **The construction the
revoked rule declared unwritable is written.** Then 2525 (free relative
in the genitive, on the very verb `thurfan` answers to), 3404 (**dative**
relative followed by the particle, on the very verb `haurian` answers to
— the dative wall falls by the same tool as the genitive one), 2218
(accusative followed by the particle).

⚠ **IT IS NOT A FREE CHOICE BETWEEN THE TWO MEMBERS.** The enquiry that
went through the 6,000 lines reports a **negative result**: *no* case
where the bare particle relativises a genitive object. Every relative in
bare `the` whose verb governs the genitive has that genitive filled by a
full noun (*Heliand* 3344, 3502, 3497) — the particle there is the
**subject**. Member ② is therefore not a variant of taste: it is
**required** as soon as the relative carries an oblique case governed
*inside* the relative clause.

⚠⚠ **AND THE PROJECT'S PRACTICE WAS ALREADY RIGHT AGAINST ITS OWN RULE.**
Twice, with nothing to point it out:
· *Hairro Fohs, **thena the** stank galokkōd habda* — in the texts;
· *thiu haga, **thiu** thena flōd hrīnid* — the example for the government
  of `hrīnan`, a few pages above, inflected feminine and not `the`.
  *A rule that none of my own examples follows was not a rule: it was a
  sentence.*

⚠ **THE ARGUMENT FROM DATE TELLS AGAINST THE REVOKED RULE, NOT FOR IT.**
Gothic, the oldest state (4th c.), has a **fully inflected** relative —
*saei, þanei, þammei, **þizei***. The bare particle is an **Ingvaeonic
reduction**, at its maximum in Old English. To prescribe for 550 a system
less case-marked than the Old Saxon of 830, the Old High German of 870
and the Gothic of 350 is to project an innovation backwards. **Both
enquiries of 2026-08-19 say so separately, without having read each
other.**

⚠ **Wachtendonck serves here only for the inventory.** It renders *cuius*
by `thes` one for one; it attests that the **form** existed in Low
Franconian, it validates **no turn**. That is exactly the rank 5 the
syntactic hierarchy assigns it, and we hold to it.

**THE WORKAROUND BY EXTRAPOSITION — demoted, not deleted.** "of the man
who has the land over which it runs": ***thes mannes, the that land hebit,
thār it ubar rinnid***. It remains **licit**; it is no longer
**prescribed**, the adnominal genitive relativising directly by `thes`.
⚠ The two enquiries diverge here — one wants it withdrawn entirely (no
witness requires it), the other keeps it as a variant. **One does not
delete on a divergence**, and its provenance is kept: two translators of
the 13th test came to it separately, **before** the rule existed, each
giving that passage as the most fragile part of his translation.
  ⚠⚠ But it **does not transpose** to a genitive governed by a VERB — the
  two enquiries confirm this at 90 % and 95 %. The device converts a
  relation between two NOUNS into two predications; a verbal genitive
  object has no second noun to extract, the case being assigned to an
  argument slot that exists only inside the relative. *I had devised a
  remedy for the one case where a remedy is possible — and concluded from
  it that the language had no gap.*


**WHAT IS NOT INTRODUCED** — the three turns set aside, with their
rating:
· **the resumptive genitive** ("the name, that you keep silent **about
  it**") — rated 30 % by the enquiry that went through the corpus, which
  writes "do not prescribe it", and 60 % by the other. Nothing in the
  *Heliand*. ⚠ We take the low rating: it is backed by a survey, the other
  is not. It remains attested for the POSSESSIVE in Old English (`se þe …
  his`), not for a governed object;
· **the switch to the accusative under relativisation** — 8 % against
  35 %. No old Germanic language changes a verb's government because it
  is relativised; the drift genitive → accusative is a **diachronic**
  fact, not an alternation conditioned by syntax;
· **the R-pronoun** (*daer … van*, Du *waarvan*) — 12 % against 45 %.
  ⚠ The reason settles it: this turn presupposes that genitive objects
  have **already** given way to prepositional ones, a fact of the end of
  the first millennium at the earliest. **Anachronistic for 550.**

⚠ **THE DIVERGENCE ONE DOES NOT AVERAGE.** Old Saxon says "particle very
frequent, inflected form available"; Old High German says "inflected form
normal, particle residual". The Frankish of 550 is **between** the two
geographically and **before** the two chronologically. What the two
witnesses have in common — and the only thing entered — is that **the
inflected relative exists and is available in every case**.
⚠⚠ **We do NOT know the ratio of frequency between the two members for
550, and we do not claim to.**

⚠⚠ **CASE CLOSED THE SAME EVENING, AND THE MORNING'S ENQUIRY HAD LED ME
ASTRAY.** I had been told that `sō hwilīk sō` was "a different lemma from
`hwē`" — **that is true, and it left me believing `hwē` was absent.** The
two evening enquiries show that it is on the contrary **the principal
device** for the person, with a complete paradigm (see the ruling on the
free relative of person). *An exact distinction can mislead as much as an
error, when it answers beside the question asked.*
`sō hwilīk sō` remains licit for "whichever it may be", and the survey of
the *Heliand* gives an indefinite relative in the dative (l. 1539 *liudio
sō hwilicum, sō that lēstien wili*), in the accusative (l. 3207) and in
the dative again (l. 3507).

⚠⚠⚠ **AND THE SENTENCE THAT STOOD HERE FELL ON 2026-08-25.** It said
that the neighbouring rule "sets aside only ~~sō hwē sō~~" and that "we do
not decide here" — **whereas the ruling printed six lines below does
decide, and the other way.** `sō hwē sō` is **licit**: four attestations
in the *Heliand*, a complete paradigm, the prohibition rated false at
96 % and 97 % by two independent enquiries. The paragraph therefore
carried a lapsed prohibition three lines from its own revocation, and
**three lines above its own contrary assertion** — "it is on the contrary
the principal device".
*Found by `kontradi` on the day it was born, out of 71 phrases examined:
it is the only one, and it had been there six days without any of the
thirty controls seeing it.*
⚠⚠ **THE FREE RELATIVE OF PERSON EXISTS — ruling of 2026-08-19, which
REVOKES the rule "NO FREE RELATIVE FOR THE PERSON" of 2026-08-09.** That
rule declared ~~the the sakit~~ "unreadable" and ~~sō hwē sō~~
non-existent. ⚠ **These two forms are STRUCK THROUGH here and not
daggered, and the change is of 2026-08-25:** the dagger marks a faulty
form, and these two are **licit** — it is the RULE that set them aside
that is dead. A translator who comes upon †*sō hwē sō* while skimming the
kit refuses the form without reading the sentence that restores it.
*The strikethrough says "we have stopped writing this"; the dagger says
"never write this". Confusing them inverts the ruling.*
**Both are in the *Heliand*.** A double enquiry, each on Behaghel, *Die
Syntax des Heliand*, 1897, full text: **rated false at 96 % and 97 %.**

**THREE TURNS, ALL ATTESTED:**

**① the inflected demonstrative + the particle** — the type case, and it
is the very sentence the revoked rule declared unreadable:
> *Heliand* 1869-1871: ***thene** … **the** im te hebenrīkea thene weg
> wīsit* — "he who shows them the way to the heavenly kingdom".
The first element carries **the case of the main clause**, the particle
is invariable (Behaghel §468). Attested in every useful case: acc. `thena
the` (2218), dat. `themu the` (2047, 3404, 4092), gen. `thes the` (4958,
5126), dat. pl. `them the` (1348), gen. pl. `thero the` (2786), nom. pl.
`thia the` (1317).

**② the BARE particle, with no head at all** — Behaghel §465 A.I:
> *Heliand* 659: *sie frumide **the mahte*** "he did it who was able" ·
> 3961: *gilaubda **thie wolda*** "who would, believed" · 4806: *cuman
> mid craftu, **the mi farkaupōd habad*** · 293: *sagda **them siu
> welda*** · 2358: *bōtta, **them thar blinde wārun***.

**③ `sō hwē sō` "whosoever"**, with a **complete paradigm** the kit did
not have at all:

| case | form |
|---|---|
| **nom.** | **sō hwē sō** |
| **acc.** | **sō hwena sō** |
| **gen.** | **sō hwes sō** |
| **dat.** | **sō hwemu sō** |

Behaghel §485 makes of it *"a **characteristic of West Germanic**"*.
Attested 900, 902, 957, 1145, 1276, 1433, 1437, 1515, 1788, 2607, 3920.
⚠ **And the correlation**: the subordinate clause in `sō hwē sō…`, then a
resumption by the `the` series **in the case of the main clause** —
*Heliand* 1433-1436, 3079-3081. *Old Saxon handles the case mismatch
exactly as the modern vernaculars do, by a resumptive pronoun.*

⚠⚠ **WHAT THAT RULE COST, AND IT MUST BE SAID.** It stood for ten days.
Both translators of the 38th round suffered it on **three sentences**, and
one of the enquirers puts a figure on the bill: *"it cost you three
passives and nine needless occurrences of the word 'man'"*. Sentence 9 of
the round — ten words of the source — is now written in ten words:
> ***Sō hwemu sō man gibid, the skal thes gahuggian, the imu gaf.***
> "He to whom one gives must remember him who gave."
**No passive, no repetition of `man`.** The two enquiries render this
sentence separately, to within one word.

⚠⚠ **AND THE PROJECT'S CORPUS WAS ALREADY WRITING IT, AGAINST THE RULE.**
In the **Salic Law**, the project's flagship text: ***The** ina lathōd,
**the** skal kuman te sīnemu hūsē mit gawiton* "he who summons him, he
must come to his house with witnesses" — the correlation `the… the…` of
*Heliand* 1314-1315. And in the saga: *ain wīs man, the was gahaitan
Aridius. **The** quath*.

  *The fourth time today that practice is right against the rule, and
  none of the twenty-one controls could see it: not one of them reads
  syntax.*

⚠ **WHAT I RECORD AGAINST MY OWN METHOD.** The enquiry was commissioned
on my doubt about `these`. One enquirer replies: *"The point where you
mistrusted yourself was the least bad of your points. It is the
prohibition you were not questioning that is flatly false."*
  *Well-aimed doubt is not the sharpest doubt: it is the doubt one turns
  on the rule one has never reopened.*

⚠ **ONE REAL GAP REMAINS, AND IT IS DECLARED**: the free relative in the
**FEMININE SINGULAR** is attested nowhere — neither in the 5,983 lines,
nor in Wachtendonck. `thiu the` (nom.), `thia the` (acc.), `theru the`
(dat.) are **RECONSTRUCTED** — 88 % and 75 %, the two enquiries diverge
on the rating and we do not average. *The silence is an accident of corpus
— the* Heliand *puts very few women at the head of a relative — not a
fact of the language. We write them, we label them.*
⚠ **BUT THE NEUTER IS FREE**: *that* is both neuter relative AND
indefinite, and nothing stops it from doing without an antecedent —
***that hē antfēng*** "what he received", ***dō that ik seggiu*** "do what
I say". The first drafting of this rule forbade both; it was motivated
only for the person, where the doubling *the the* makes the sentence
unreadable, which *that* does not. Corrected 2026-08-09 on the reading of
a translator who saw it better than the Academy.
"four pigs, each of which is worth ten deniers". **The ground of the rule** (R51): the
partitive genitive already occupies the position the relative would come
to fill; doubling it would be pleonastic. Adding *the* is therefore
faulty.
Agent nouns in -āri: **ja-stems** (see the noun table), nom./acc. pl. -a
(witāra), genitive plural -o, dative plural -on/-un ⚠ (and not †-io,
†-ium: those endings are Old Saxon, abrogated 2026-08-15).
Relatives: **TWO MEMBERS** — the invariable particle `the` (with `that`
neuter and indefinite, `thiu` f. sg.) **and the demonstrative inflected in
the required case**; see the ruling of 2026-08-19. All with a final verb.
**OBLIQUE relative — and TWO obliques must be distinguished, which this
paragraph did not do:**
· **bare case assigned by the VERB** — the dative of `haurian`, the
  genitive of `thurfan` — → **the inflected demonstrative**: *the man,
  themu thū hauris*.
· **PREPOSITIONAL oblique** — "that from which it is drawn", "the night
  when it happened" — → **thār … + the preposition at the end**: that
  niowiht, thār hē fon gatogan ist. ⚠⚠ **AND THE EXAMPLE †*thiu naht,
  thār it an gaskah* IS WITHDRAWN ON 2026-08-21**: it carried a relative
  of TIME, whereas the ruling on the adverbial relative establishes that
  **`thār` is SPATIAL and does not extend to time** — it is the one point
  on which the two enquirers of that file agree. **One writes `the`**:
  *thiu naht, **the** it an gaskah*, on the same pattern as *an themu
  dagē, **the** hē sprak*.
    ⚠ Reported by translator K of the 43rd round, who notes that **the
    table of questions sends the reader here FIRST**: *"a translator who
    opens the kit at the line 'writing a relative' reaches the § OBLIQUE
    relative first and writes `thār`"*. *A contradiction placed on the
    way in costs more than a contradiction buried deep.* ⚠ **It is here,
  and here only, that the prohibition †fon themu hē gatogan ist holds**
  (a Romance calque): one does not relativise a prepositional phrase by
  the declined article preceded by its preposition.
Promulgated 2026-08-04: the kit offered no means of relativising an
oblique, and so manufactured the fault it punishes.

⚠⚠ **THIS PARAGRAPH SAID "NEVER THE DECLINED ARTICLE" FOR FIFTEEN DAYS,
WITH NO BOUND AT ALL — and BOTH translators of the 38th round reported it
separately** (2026-08-19), one naming it *"the most dangerous
contradiction in the document, because it is categorical, because it is
argued, and because it bears on exactly what makes this text what it
is"*. It moreover listed the available relatives **without the inflected
member**, so that a reader who stopped there wrote none of the sentences
the ruling of 08-19 makes possible.
  ⚠ **Corrected on the very day of the ruling it contradicted — and it
  had escaped the migration sweep**, which was looking for "possessive
  relative", "invariable", "does not go into the genitive".
    *A migration that looks for the WORDS of the abrogated rule misses
    the paragraphs that say the same thing otherwise. It is golden rule
    no. 8 caught out by its own application, on the day it was being
    invoked.*
⚠ **And there is NO contradiction with the rejection of the R-pronoun**
pronounced the same day: what is set aside at 12 % is **converting** a
genitive object governed by a verb into a prepositional government in
order to relativise it. Relativising a phrase that **is already
prepositional** in the source sentence is another operation, and `thār` +
preposition performs it.
Numerals: hund (n.) "hundred",

thūsund “thousand” — both invariable; the tens run twaintig…niguntig.

### ⚠⚠ N1 AND N2 ARE DOWNGRADED FROM RULE TO TENDENCY (2026-08-16)

Double enquiry on the article and on word order, at the close of the
dating audit. The diagnosis of the two is identical, and it is gentler
than the audit had led one to fear:

> **The kit's V2 is not FALSE. It is OVER-GRAMMATICALISED.** The orders
> the ten norms produce are, one by one, **all attested at 550**; they are
> simply OPTIONAL where the kit makes them OBLIGATORY. **It is a
> difference in the status of a rule, not in the grammaticality of the
> output.** That is what makes the repair cheap.

**The argument that decides — and it is not the one I was given.** The
four oldest continuous West Germanic corpora — *Isidor* (~790), *Tatian*
and *Heliand* (~830), Alfredian prose (~890) — all present a **mixed**
system: V2 is dominant there but **not categorical**, and main clauses
with a late verb are attested. It becomes categorical only in Middle High
German and Middle Dutch, in the 12th–13th century.

> **A system cannot be MORE categorical in 550 than it is in 830-890,
> only to loosen afterwards and then harden again.** A categorical V2 at
> 550 is therefore anachronistic *a fortiori*.

⚠⚠ **AND BOTH ENQUIRERS DISQUALIFY GALLEHUS, which the audit invoked.**
`ek HlewagastiR HoltijaR horna tawido` is indeed SOV, but the witness is
**inadmissible three times over**: it is a **genre formula** ("I, X, made
Y" — frozen in every language on earth), it is **alliterative verse**, and
it is ~400 **in Denmark**. The same remark for Pforzen. *"Whoever writes
'Gallehus proves that 550 is SOV' is extrapolating."*
· **We have no continental West Germanic prose before the *Isidor*
(~790).** The file on word order is not to be pleaded on runes.

### N0 — THE BASE IS OV, and fronting the verb is PRAGMATIC

> **The unmarked order of any clause — main AS WELL AS subordinate — is
> the finite verb LATE.** It is the default regime, the one applied when
> nothing draws the clause away from it. Fronting the finite verb is not a
> structural requirement: it **marks the sentence type and the
> articulation of information**.

The non-finite verb precedes the finite auxiliary: **`hē it gadān
habda`**, preferable at 550 to *hē habda gadān*.

#### The scale of fronting — to be applied IN ORDER

| rank | context | verb |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | yes-no question · imperative · wish, optative, formula · **conditional protasis WITHOUT a conjunction** | **V1 obligatory** |
| **2** | **narrative inversion** — opening of an episode, presentative sentence | **V1 frequent, optional** |
| **3** | interrogative word in *hw-* · fronted deictic-resumptive adverb (*thō, thanne, thār, sō, nū*) · fronted *ni* | **V2 very strongly preferred** |
| **4** | ordinary topicalisation of an object, a prepositional phrase, a non-deictic adverb | **V2 optional, ~50/50** |
| **5** | clause with an initial subject | **V2 and late verb COEXIST** |

⚠ **At rank 4, both orders are equally good at 550**: *That swerd nam hē*
**and** *That swerd hē nam*.

⚠ **At rank 5, the register decides**: a **late** verb in legal, solemn
and sententious prose; **V2** in narration and dialogue, above all with a
heavy object.

⚠⚠ **RANK 3 IS THE HISTORICAL HEART OF V2, AND BOTH ENQUIRERS SAY IT IS
THE KIT'S BEST SYNTACTIC ASSET.** *thō* + verb is pan-West-Germanic — OE
*þā cōm*, OHG *thô quad*, ON *þá mælti* — therefore earlier than 450,
therefore licit. **We do not touch it.**

**Two free additions, of high yield, which the kit did not have:**
· **the conditional protasis without a conjunction** — ***Quimid hē,
thanne…*** "if he comes, then…". Attested in OE, OHG and ON;
· **the narrative V1** — ***Quam thō man te thingē…*** "Then came a man to
the court…". A living usage, not an archaism.

⚠ **N2 becomes a STRONG TENDENCY, not an absolute rule**: the final verb
in a subordinate clause is in the majority, never categorical — the
*Hildebrandslied* offers subordinate clauses with a non-final verb. **And
both enquirers ask that the extraposition licences N9/N10 be WIDENED, not
restricted**: heavy prepositional phrases, relatives, comparatives, second
coordinated conjuncts, complement clauses and infinitives are regularly
extraposed.

### ⚠⚠ THE SECOND-POSITION CLITICS — what the kit was really missing

Both enquirers give it as **the costliest gap in the grammar, and the one
whose repair pays best.**

⚠ **But they also correct the SCALE: a Wackernagel law of the
Indo-European type is no longer available at 550.** What Gothic still has
and what CANNOT be borrowed, because West Germanic has already lost it:
· the coordinating enclitic **`-uh`** (Goth. *qaþ-uh*) — West Germanic says
*endi*;
· the enclitic interrogative particle **`-u`** — West Germanic asks the
question by V1.
*Here the check by Old English works, and in the restrictive direction: it
protects against a seductive but illicit Gothic archaism.*

**What remains, and it is indeed the want:** the place of the unstressed
pronouns and the proclisis of *ni*. It is narrow, but it is what gives an
old Germanic language its grain.

⚠⚠ **THE EMPHATIC PRONOUN — THERE IS NO EMPHATIC SERIES, AND THAT IS THE
ANSWER** (ruling of 2026-08-17). The rule on clitics says *"all the
NON-EMPHATIC personal pronouns"* — and the kit said nowhere how an
emphatic one is produced. **There is nothing to produce: the form is the
same, it is the POSITION that carries the stress.** Three means, and they
can be combined:

| means | example |
|---|---|
| **the head of the clause** — a clitic cannot occupy it; to stand there is to be stressed | ***Hē** gaf thia bōk, ni ik* "**he** gave the book, not I" |
| **`self`** — bare after the noun, weak **`selbo`** after the determiner | *hē **selbo*** "himself" · *the kuning **selbo*** |
| **coordination** — two coordinated pronouns cannot be clitics | *ik endi thū* "you and I" |

> *A language without an emphatic series is not deprived of emphasis: it
> puts it into the word order.* **It is the same principle as focus
> without a cleft, and the kit said so already there — it had not carried
> it over to the pronouns.**


⚠⚠ **TWO PRONOMINAL HOMOGRAPHS — AND THE FIRST DRAFTING SAID "AND IT IS
THE ONLY ONE", WHICH WAS FALSE** (corrected 2026-08-25, 62nd round,
reported by a translator who carried both values three lines apart in his
copy).

① ***sia*** is at once **accusative 3 sg. FEMININE** *and* **nominative
AND accusative 3 PLURAL** — three values for one form, and the pronoun
paradigm prints all three. In *ni konsta **sia** neoman lesan*, only the
singular agreement of the verb prevents the reading "no one could read
**them**". ⚠ **It is the verb that disambiguates, not the pronoun.**

② ***is*** is at once the **genitive of `hē`** (*Ansoberht **is** brōther*
"Ansoberht his brother") and the **3rd sg. of `wesan`** (*that **is**
sauth* "that is true"). **Nothing in the form separates them; the position
separates them entirely** — the genitive is adnominal and precedes its
noun, the copula is the inflected verb and occupies second place.
*Reported 2026-08-17: it had been present since the first day and had
never been named to the translator.*

> **Are CLITIC — never stressed, never standing alone:** all the
> non-emphatic personal pronouns, in every function, and the reflexive
> *sik*.
> ⚠⚠ **AND THE PROHIBITION ON THE HEAD POSITION IS BOUNDED TO THE
> OBLIQUES — an amendment entered here 2026-08-17.** This statement said
> "never at the absolute head of a clause, in every function"; the
> arbitration of the 31st round bounded it to the **oblique** clitics two
> hundred lines below — **and had not amended the line it was
> arbitrating.** A translator who read this one first applied the
> abrogated rule. *Found by the sweep of categorical statements; it is
> exactly golden rule no. 20, committed on its own remedy.*
>
> **Placement.** Immediately **after the first stressed constituent** of
> the clause. ⚠⚠ **In a subordinate clause: immediately AFTER THE
> COMPLEMENTISER, before even a nominal subject** — ***that imu the kuning
> that land gaf***, not †*that the kuning that land imu gaf*. **It is the
> most immediately archaising trait that can be added.**
>
> **Internal order: nominative first, then accusative, then dative.**
> ***hē it imu gaf***, not †*hē imu it gaf*.
>
> ⚠⚠ **TWO PROHIBITIONS — AND THE FIRST IS REWORDED ON 2026-08-21,
> BECAUSE ITS DRAFTING CONTRADICTED ITSELF.**
>
> **① An unstressed pronoun is NEVER put at the end for WANT of a place**
> — that is, one does not throw it to the end of the clause to avoid
> placing it. ⚠ *But when the verb rises to second position and carries
> the clitic group behind it, the last clitic of the group finds itself AT
> THE END, and that is the arbitrated form*: `thō gaf hē it imu`,
> `thō swīgōdun sia`. **The group stays welded and keeps its order; it
> follows the verb instead of preceding it.**
> **② No unstressed pronoun separated from second position by a full
> nominal or prepositional phrase.** *That one is without exception.*
>
> ⚠ **The previous drafting said "no unstressed pronoun at the end of a
> clause, EVER" and gave, twenty lines above, an arbitrated example that
> breaks it.** Both translators of the 43rd round reported it separately,
> and **both wrote the arbitrated form against the prohibition** — K
> noting that he did so *"knowing that I was violating a prohibition
> called absolute, because the kit tells me to"*.
> **A prohibition one is taught to break is not a prohibition: it is a
> badly bounded rule.** *The prohibition bore on an INTENTION (throwing it
> back for want of a place), the arbitrated form is a RESULT (the group
> follows the verb) — that had to be said, not answered with the
> announcement of a double enquiry to come.*

⚠ **The pronominal order is the REVERSE of that of full nominal phrases**,
which is dative before accusative: *the kuning gaf themu manne **thia**
bōk*. This pronoun / noun inversion is an old Germanic fact, kept down to
modern German and Dutch — *er gab es ihm*, *hij gaf het hem*.
⚠ **But the order acc. before dat. is a DECLARED CONVENTION**: the second
enquirer points out that between objects the order is not fixed in the old
texts. We lay it down for consistency with the later Germanic record; we
do not prove it.


#### The subject pronoun and inversion — the ban on V3 is LIFTED

· after *thō* / *thanne* / *ni* / a word in *hw-*: **inversion always**,
including with a subject pronoun — *thō quam hē*, *thō quam the kuning*;
· after **any other** initial element: the **nominal** subject inverts, the
**pronominal** subject does not —
  *an themu jārē fōr the kuning* (noun → V2)
  ***an themu jārē hē fōr*** (pronoun → **V3**).

⚠⚠ **A TEMPERING, AND IT IS THE SECOND ENQUIRER WHO IMPOSES IT.** This
contrast is **categorical in Old English**, but only **tendential in OHG
and OS**, where *thô quad er* is frequent. **We therefore allow BOTH
orders, with a preference for the preverbal clitic as the conservative
form.**
> *"Do not make an absolute rule of it — you would commit again, in
> reverse, the error you are being reproached with."*

**And here is why the whole file reconciles**: Germanic V2 **arises
historically from the reanalysis of this clitic second position as the
position of the finite verb**. By laying down the clitics and a V2 that is
only triggered, we are not stepping back from one system to another: **we
are describing the real intermediate stage, the one V2 will come out of.**

### ⚠⚠ THE ARTICLE — the one point where the grammar said something FALSE

**It is not the FORM that is at issue, it is the STATUS.**

**The form is UNDECIDABLE, and we keep it.** Gothic, Norse and Old English
have the *s-* suppletion in the nom. m. and f.; the whole continent has
levelled to *th-*. But the levelling of a suppletion in **two cells out of
twenty-four** is the fastest analogical change there is, it happened
**independently** in English too (*þe* around 1150), and between 450 and
790 the continent is **mute**. 550 falls in the middle of a 340-year
interval with no anchor point at all.
> **We keep `the / thiu / that`, `thena`, and we write it as a
> CONVENTION, not as a reconstruction.** *"You must never pay a corpus
> cost for a nil gain."*

⚠ **A FREE BONUS, proposed separately by BOTH enquirers**: `sa` (m.) and
`siu` (f.) survive as **archaisms of the legal and formulaic register** —
openings of articles of law, incipits, proverbs, alliterative verse.
***sa man the…*** "he who…". It costs nothing, it creates a texture of
register, and it covers one philologically in both directions.

**The status, on the other hand, is false.** At 550, this word is an
**anaphoric demonstrative of OPTIONAL use**. It is not an article.
Therefore:
· there is **no empty cell** — a noun phrase without a determiner is not
"indefinite", it is **unspecified**;
· there is **no privative opposition** definite / indefinite: *man*
translates "a man", "the man", "men", "man in general";
· it **carries meaning**, so using it where there is nothing to point at
produces a **parasitic deictic insistence**.

Wulfila's Gothic **massively omits** *sa* where the Greek has ὁ — in a
near word-for-word translation, the omission is significant. The *Heliand*
and the *Tatian* use it far more sparingly than modern German. **If it is
still optional in 830, it cannot be obligatory in 550.**


#### The translator's procedure — to be applied in order

**STEP 1 — OBLIGATORY OMISSION.** Never a determiner before:
**1.** ⚠⚠ ~~a **unique** — god, sun, moon, sky, earth, sea, day, night~~ →
**THIS LINE IS WITHDRAWN ON 2026-08-21. It was empirically false, and both
enquirers refute it with the same lines.** The *Heliand* writes ***thiu
sunna*** (5625), ***thiu erða*** (5799), ***an themu mânon*** (4310),
***an thesoro erðo*** (1657), ***an thene sêo*** (4 times); the *Muspilli*
writes ***der himil*** (53) and ***diu suona*** (6).
  > **The determiner is NOT blocked by the lexeme.** What is bare is the
  > **adverbial** use and the **generic** use — the *Heliand*'s *an
  > himile*, *an erðu*, some thirty bare occurrences.
  ⚠ *A rule that classifies by TYPE OF NOUN where the corpus shows a
  property of CONSTRUCTION will be broken indefinitely, because it is
  false.* **That is exactly what happened in the 43rd round: both
  translators broke it, and they were right.** The right criterion is in
  **STEP 2** — anaphora, deixis, antecedent of a relative, contrast,
  ordinal/superlative, weak adjective — plus the condition on the weak
  adjective, below;
**2.** a **generic** or a species — *wolƀos sint grimme*;
**3.** an **abstract or a mass noun** — law, peace, gold, blood, truth;
**4.** a **proper name** — ⚠ the determiner before a proper name is a later
innovation of Dutch and of the German dialects: **it is an immediately
visible marker of late dating**;
**5.** a **nominal predicate** — *hē ist kuning*;
**6.** a **vocative**;
**7.** a **common prepositional phrase** of place, time or manner — *te
hūs*, *an thing*. ⚠⚠ **AND †*an naht* IS WITHDRAWN ON 2026-08-21, AT THE SAME TIME AS FROM TABLE ⑧**: "by night" is not a prepositional phrase, it is the **adverbial genitive** `nahtas`. *The morning's correction had withdrawn †`bi nahti` from this line without seeing that the neighbouring construction had just been abrogated by the same double enquiry.* ⚠⚠ **†*bi nahti* IS WITHDRAWN
ON 2026-08-21: it is a modern Germanism (*bei Nacht*), not a form of the
corpus.** Both enquirers say so separately — **zero occurrences of `bi`
before a noun of time in the 5,983 lines of the *Heliand*, zero in the
*Tatian*, zero in Wachtendonck**; `bi` there is **local and causal** (*bi
thiu* "that is why"), never temporal. ⚠ And the ending was wrong too: the
Old Saxon dative of `naht` is **always `naht`**, with a zero ending;
`nahti` occurs only **once** in the whole of rank (a), Wachtendonck Ps
18,3, **in the *figura etymologica* `nox nocti` calqued word for word** —
that is, in the least reliable context in the corpus.
   *The one BARE example on this line was the one to throw away.*
⚠ It is exactly there that the modern Germanic languages still keep the
bare noun: **it is a fossil, and we exploit it**;
**8.** a **kinship term** or a **part of the body** whose possessor is
obvious;
**9.** the **casuistic legal enumeration** — *of man swīn stilid*.
⚠ No determiner anywhere: this is also the style of the Latin *Pactus
legis Salicae*, **anarthrous from end to end**. A perfect fit.
⚠ **And never `the` + a possessive.**

**STEP 2 — OUTRIGHT USE.** It is put down if, and only if:
**1.** **anaphora** — the referent has been named in the two or three
preceding sentences. *This is the central use, the one that justifies the
word*;
**2.** **deixis** — one points, physically or textually;
**3.** **antecedent of a restrictive relative** — *the man the that swerd
nam*. The most stable context, strong in Gothic and in OE;
**4.** **identifying contrast** — this one and not that one;
**5.** before an **ordinal** or a **superlative**;
**6.** with an **individualising weak adjective**, when the referent is
already established.
**7.** ⚠⚠ **a DEFINITE DESCRIPTION on FIRST mention — AN OPEN CASE,
declared 2026-08-21.** "the field **of the neighbour**", "the servant **of
the king**", "the wife **of the smith**": a noun determined by an adnominal
genitive, without having been named before. **It is the commonest turn of
narrative prose in the source language, and step 2 does not contain it.**
⚠ A correction of 2026-08-21 had written that "the right criterion is in
STEP 2 — anaphora, deixis, **definite description**": *the clause was
named in the correction and absent from the procedure.* Reported by
translator T of the 47th round.
  · **what the two translators did**: both **omitted** the determiner, by
    STEP 3 ("the rest: we omit") — `feld thes gabūras`, `skalk thes
    kuningas`. **Convergence, not warrant**;
  · ⚠ **and one of them raised the contrary argument**: for an `[n]` like
    `grāfio`, the oblique `-on` is **identical to the nominative and
    dative plurals** — without a determiner, `brōther grāfion` is
    ambiguous. *But the ruling of 2026-08-21 on `naht` withdrew precisely
    that inference — "the language tolerates a formally invisible dative"
    — so the argument is set aside by the kit itself.*
  **We omit, and we know that we do not know.**

**STEP 3 — THE REST: WE OMIT.** ⚠ **The fallback rule is not neutral, it
is omission.**


⚠ **Calibration**: about **one determiner where the source language puts
three or four**; in the legal register, almost none. **This figure is a
convention of the language, NOT a published count** — both enquirers
propose it and both declare it an unverified heuristic.

⚠ **Position**: the demonstrative is still **tonic and mobile**. It may
follow the noun in the high or formulaic register — *man the* — and the
possessive likewise: ***fader mīn***, of the type Goth. *atta unsar*, OE
*fæder ūre*.

⚠ **The argument from Gallo-Romance contact, and its limit.** The Frankish
of 550 is spoken **in Gaul**, in contact with a Latin that is
grammaticalising *ille*. A Frankish article more advanced than in Germania
is therefore *contact-plausible*. **But in Gregory of Tours *ille* is still
massively demonstrative**, and the Romance grammaticalisation is dated to
the 7th–9th century. *"Contact buys you a little advance, not an obligatory
paradigm."* To be invoked as a **labelled conjecture**, never as proof.

⚠⚠ **AND THE MEROVINGIAN FILE CONTAINS NO SYNTACTIC DATA WHATEVER.** The
Malberg glosses are isolated and corrupt phrases, without one sentence;
Bergakker, the continental runes, Gregory and the charters yield no
assured determiner. **Neither for, nor against.** *Saying so is a strength,
not a confession.*

### ⚠⚠ THE WEAK ADJECTIVE — the kit's rule was Middle High German

**The rule "weak after a determiner, strong elsewhere" describes modern
German.** It is **logically impossible at 550**, since the determiner is
not an obligatory category there: *one was conditioning an inflection on
something that does not yet exist.*

> **The weak adjective is originally an INDIVIDUALISING `-n-` STEM — a
> device for nicknames, hence a NOUN IN APPOSITION.** `gōdo` does not mean
> "good" but **"the good one, the-one-who-is-good"**. And an apposed noun
> has no need of an article at all: *gōdo man* = "the man, the good one —
> the one you know".

The most legible proof is **onomastic**: *Haraldr hárfagri*, *Eiríkr
rauði*, *Ívarr beinlausi* — **weak adjective, no determiner**. Gothic uses
the weak form in the **vocative** without an article; poetic OE and the
*Heliand* use it commonly without a determiner.

> **What governs the choice at 550 is REFERENCE: individuation and
> identifiability. Neither position, nor register, nor the presence of a
> word.**

**WEAK obligatory**: the **comparative, always and without exception**
(stem in *-izon-*) ⚠ — *this is the clearest proof that the determiner is
not the conditioning factor, and if the kit inflects a comparative as
strong, that is a hard error* · the **vocative** · the **ordinal** · the
**epithet attached to a proper name**, with or without *the* · after a
demonstrative or a possessive, **but as a CONSEQUENCE of individuation,
not as an automatism**.

**WEAK normal, with no determiner at all**: a unique or already identified
referent · superlative · formulaic style, heroic epithets, titulature.

**STRONG obligatory**: the **predicative** use, without exception — *the
man is gōd*, never †*gōdo* · indefinite or non-specific reference · after
an indefinite quantifier · the apposition that brings **new** information
rather than identifying information.

⚠⚠ **AND THE TWO NEW RULES COLLIDE — here is the resolution.** "The
comparative is ALWAYS weak, without exception" and "the predicative is
ALWAYS strong, without exception" contradict each other the moment one
writes *he is better*. **It is the comparative that wins, and not by
arbitration:**

> **The comparative HAS no strong inflection.** The stem in `-izon-` is an
> `-n-` stem: it knows only the weak declension, in Gothic as in OHG and in
> OS. The rule on the predicative therefore cannot apply to it — there
> exists no form for it to bite on.
> **It is not an exception, it is an empty cell.**

⚠⚠ **AND THE KIT'S PRACTICE WAS ALREADY RIGHT AGAINST THE KIT'S RULE.**
The dictionary carries ***ain betaro man*** — a **weak** comparative after
the indefinite *ain*. Under the old rule "weak ⇐ determiner", that form was
**faulty** and should have been †*ain betar man*. It never was: the twelve
comparatives of the lexicon are **all** weak, without exception, including
where the old rule required the strong form.
> *A false rule can live for months beside the right practice that refutes
> it, without any control confronting them: they contradict each other only
> through a theory the kit did not state.*


**The minimal pair every translator must be able to handle:**
| | |
|---|---|
| **`gōd man quam`** | strong, bare — **a** good man came. *One classifies.* |
| **`gōdo man quam`** | weak, **bare** — **the** good man came. *One points at that one.* |
| **`the gōdo man quam`** | weak + deictic — **that** man, the good one, came. *Licit, but heavier and less frequent than today.* |
| †*the gōd man* | **proscribed** |

⚠ **The cost is ALMOST NIL, and that is a real piece of good news: the
correction is purely ADDITIVE.** Everything already written stays
grammatical — a prohibition is **lifted**. The only point to audit:
**strong** adjectives before a definite and known noun **without** a
determiner, which ought to be weak. And **all the comparatives**, to be
forced to the weak form.

### ⚠⚠ NEGATIVE CONCORD — THE AUDITOR WAS WRONG

**Both enquirers say so separately, and it is the second point on which
the dating audit went astray.** Two things had been confused:

**1. Negative concord** — preverbal *ni* + a negative indefinite — is
**old and pan-Germanic**: attested in Gothic (*ni manna mag*), in Old
English from the earliest texts, in Norse, in OHG and in OS. The
indefinites themselves are compounds in *ni-* of **parallel formation in
all three branches** — *ni+ain* > OE *nān*, *ni+aiwiht* > OS *neowiht* —
therefore earlier than 450.
> **It is LICIT at 550. The only adjustment: from OBLIGATORY to LICIT AND
> FREQUENT.** *ni sah ik man* and *ni sah ik nihain* are both correct.
> **The corpus remains valid.**

**2. Bipartite negation of the Jespersen type** — *ni* + a postverbal
reinforcer that is **obligatory and without an argument role**, of the type
*ne… niht* — is an innovation of the **11th–13th century**. **That is the
one that would be anachronistic, and it is the one I was being told
about.**
> **FORBIDDEN**: any postverbal reinforcer without an argument function
> (stage II); any negation without *ni* (stage III).

⚠ **A free addition of very high yield in authenticity: the CONTRACTIONS
of *ni* + verb**, attested everywhere — OE *nis, næs, nolde, nāt, nabban*;
OHG *nist, nibu*; OS *nis, nist, nebu*.
⚠ **The five forms are in TABLE OF USE ⑦ and are no longer repeated here**
(2026-08-21): they were written there twice, and `kitlint` reported the
five duplicates. *A table of use gives the form; the prose that grounded it
refers to the table. The reverse manufactures a duplicate the source does
not have.*
⚠ The enquirers' brief wrote *weit*: **that is their spelling, not the
kit's, where L4 has already given `wait`.** The control *migro* caught it —
**for the fourth time.** *An enquirer writes in the language of his
sources; the kit writes in its own.*

### ⚠⚠ L10 IS LICIT — it is the RIGHT hardening

**Both enquirers confirm L10 and one of them dissolves the inconsistency I
thought I had.** The term *Auslautverhärtung* improperly covers two
phenomena:

**(a) The alternation of the FRICATIVES — old, West Germanic, LICIT.** In
West Germanic, the fricatives from `*ƀ` and `*ǥ` have a voiced allophone
only between voiced segments; elsewhere they are voiceless. Old English
attests it plainly: *wīf* / *wīfes* [-v-], *hūs* / *hūses* [-z-]. **`wīf` /
`wības` is exactly that case. It is the Old Saxon rule, as it stands. We do
not touch it.**

**(b) The devoicing of the STOPS in final position — late, ILLICIT.** This
is the *Auslautverhärtung* of Middle High German and Middle Dutch:
*tac/tages*, *lant/landes*, *hant/handes*. It appears in the spellings in
the **10th–11th century**. **Old English NEVER has it**: *land*, *god*,
*dæg* stay voiced. **Forbidden at 550.**

> **Final hardening applies ONLY TO THE FRICATIVES.** It **never applies to
> the stops**: `dag`, `land`, `gōd`, `kind`, `sang` in final position,
> without devoicing. ⚠ **Neither after a nasal, nor in a geminate**, where
> `*ƀ`, `*đ`, `*ǥ` were stops: *lamb* stays *lamb*, *bindan* → **`band`**
> and not †*bant*.

⚠⚠ **AND THERE CAN BE NO ALTERNATION `þ ~ d` IN FINAL POSITION.** West
Germanic hardened `*đ` to a stop **d** everywhere and unconditionally,
before 450 — OE *fæder*, *word*, *mōdor*, never †*faþer*. **One enquirer
gives this point as "the most probable error" in the corpus: to be
checked.**


⚠ **And the kit's apparent inconsistency is not one.** The kit notes the
alternation for the labial (*wīf/wības*) and keeps `g` everywhere for the
velar (*dag*, *berg*, not *dah*, *berh*). **That is exactly Old Saxon
practice**: (i) for the labial, (ii) for the velar. We declare it a
**graphic convention inherited from the Old Saxon model** — the velar
alternation is allophonic and **not written** — and the point is closed.

### ⚠⚠ THE VERDICT ON COST: MARKED, NOT UNINTELLIGIBLE

Both enquirers conclude separately, with the same confidence (0.85-0.9),
and they make it **the soundest point of their answer**:

> **The kit's case morphology is correct for 550. Now, when CASE marks the
> roles, WORD ORDER carries no grammatical information.** A speaker who
> hears *Ik sah thena man* identifies the object by the ending `-a`,
> exactly as in *Ik thena man sah*. **The permutation creates no ambiguity
> of parsing. It creates an impression of style.**

What a speaker of 550 would hear, concretely:
· **a superabundant article** → speech that is **insistent, laboured,
pointing a finger without pause**. The legal register bears it; narrative
would find it heavy;
· **systematic V2** → a **quick, oral, modern** delivery — *"he speaks
fast"*, not *"he speaks badly"*. And over-regularising an available order
produces **monotony**, not opacity;
· **a restricted weak adjective** → a language simply **less expressive**;
· **negative concord and L10** → not even faulty.

⚠⚠ **ONE THING ALONE WOULD SOUND TRULY FOREIGN, AND NOT MERELY MODERN: THE
PLACING OF THE PRONOUNS.** *"Clitic prosody is what is learnt earliest and
transgressed least; an unstressed pronoun at the end of a sentence is the
kind of error that makes one hear an ACCENT, not a period."*

> ### NO REMIGRATION. Nine traits out of eleven are sound.
> What changes is **editorial**; the corpus undergoes only **two targeted
> passes**: the unstressed pronouns — the only *audible* fault — and the
> nine contexts of obligatory omission of the determiner, which are a
> **finite and searchable list**.

⚠⚠ **AND THE RECONCILIATION THAT CLOSES THE FILE.** The outside reader who
praised the syntax and the auditors who condemned it **are both right**:
> **"Your syntax SOUNDS ancient because it IS medieval."** A cultivated
> reader calibrates his notion of "old Germanic" on what he has read —
> sagas, the *Nibelungenlied*, Middle Dutch, Luther — that is, **exactly
> the 9th–13th century**. The compliment was sincere and it measured a
> successful literary effect. **It simply did not measure a date.**

⚠ **The real price is not in the migration, it is in everything one will
write afterwards.** A categorical rule is followed; a tendency demands a
judgement at every sentence. **We compensate by assigning the variants to
REGISTERS** — legal: late verb, rare determiner, archaising forms;
narrative: frequent V2, opening V1. *That is the only way to turn an
indecision into a stylistic resource.*

### What is declared a CONVENTION, and not a reconstruction

· the **form of the nominative** of the determiner (`the/thiu` against
`sa/siu`) — no continental data before ~790;
· the **target frequency** of the determiner — a calibration heuristic;
· the **internal order of the object clitics** (acc. before dat.) — not
fixed in the old texts;
· the **graphic notation** of the velar alternation;
· the **degree of categoricity of the pronominal V3** — categorical in OE,
tendential on the continent, **unknown in Frankish**.

*Declaring them as conventions is philologically sounder than deciding in
the name of an authority that says nothing.*


### ⚠⚠ THE SEVEN COLLISIONS OF THE 31st ROUND — and their arbitration

Two independent translators tested the recast syntax on two registers.
**Their verdict is that the latitude costs nothing** — *"a tendency is
costly only when it is left BARE; yours are backed by a register, a finite
list or a minimal pair"* — and that **the cost lies elsewhere**:

> **"A declared tendency made me think. An absolute rule contradicted by
> another absolute rule made me lose time and made me write, whatever I
> did, a false form."**

Here are the seven, arbitrated.

#### 1. The superlative: the article against the weak adjective

⚠⚠ **THE TWO TRANSLATORS DECIDED IN OPPOSITE DIRECTIONS**, on the same
three words of the same sentence — *the eldest, the second, the youngest*.
One wrote `aldisto`, `andaro`, `jungisto` **bare**; the other `the
aldisto`, `the andaro`, `the jungisto`. *"On the one ground where the bare
weak form was supposed to shine, the other rule dresses it again. The two
sections do not cite each other."*

> **AND THE COLLISION IS NOT ONE: IT IS MY DRAFTING THAT WAS AT FAULT.**
> Step 2 states "it is put down **if, and only if**" — a **necessary**
> condition, never a sufficient one. It does not list obligations, it lists
> the **only contexts in which the determiner is PERMITTED**. The founding
> principle of the file says as much in so many words: **it is NEVER
> required by the syntax.**

**Arbitration: the weak adjective ALREADY carries the individuation. The
determiner adds only deixis or contrast to it, and stays optional.**
· **bare `aldisto`** is the unmarked form, and the oldest;
· **`the aldisto`** is licit, and says "that one, as against the others";
· the **register** decides: bare in sententious and formulaic prose,
determined in narrative when the resumption calls for it.
⚠ The first translator drew from it, without knowing, the very effect the
rule aims at: a **bare series** (`aldisto`, `andaro`, `jungisto`) then a
**determined resumption** (`the lesto`) — *"the contrast alone makes the
articulation of the paragraph"*.

> *Two readers who read "if and only if" as an obligation are not both
> mistaken: it is the sentence that is badly made.*

#### 2. The clitic at the head of a clause — the ban was too wide

The ban said "never at the absolute head of a clause", **for all the
unstressed pronouns, in every function**. ⚠ **Yet the kit itself writes `hē
it imu gaf` and `an themu jārē hē fōr`**, where `hē` is at the absolute
head. Both translators reported it.

> **The ban aims only at the OBLIQUE clitics — accusative, dative,
> reflexive.** A **subject pronoun** may open the clause: it occupies the
> first stressed position there, and it is what gives the clitic group its
> support.
⚠ A practical consequence, noted by the first: he had fronted `all` in
*All it an wunskianē līthid* **solely to give the subject clitic a
support**. The turn is no longer necessary — *it līthid* is enough.

#### 3. Where do the clitics go when the VERB occupies second position?

A case not provided for, reported by both, and it is the cell they most
often worked around. `thō quam hē` shows that they pass behind the verb,
but the **internal order** was prescribed nowhere.

> **The clitic group stays welded and keeps its order: it simply follows
> the verb instead of preceding it.** `thō gaf hē it imu` — nominative,
> accusative, dative, unchanged.

⚠ **The group is never broken to lodge the verb inside it**, and there is
no need to distort the sentence to avoid that: the second translator had
manufactured a heavy prefield — *mit stillero endi swārero wīsu sagda siu
imu* — in order to keep the group welded. **It was unnecessary.**

#### 4. N10 against the late verb of the sententious register

N10 opens extraposition "in REASONING prose", that is, in the very register
where the scale prescribes the late verb. *"Two rules of the kit pull in
opposite directions on the same register."*

> **They do not bear on the same thing.** The late verb settles the place
> of the **finite verb**; extraposition settles what goes off **to its
> right**. **Extraposing does not move the verb forward** — it displaces a
> heavy phrase behind it, whatever its position.
· *Skuld raginburgiono **is**, reht te dōmianne* is **correct**: the verb
stays late with respect to the subject, and the governed infinitive goes to
the right.

#### 5. N0 against narrative V2, at every compound tense

N0 lays down *hē it gadān habda* — non-finite before the finite auxiliary.
The scale lays down V2 in narrative. They collide at every pluperfect.

> **N0 describes the BASE order, that is, the order of clauses with a late
> verb. When the finite verb rises to second position, it necessarily
> leaves the non-finite one behind it**: the verb phrase splits, and that
> is the behaviour attested everywhere in old Germanic.
· late verb: *hē it gadān **habda***;
· V2: *sān **habdīn** sia that alōd **gaetan***.
**Both are right, each in its own regime. There is nothing to choose.**

#### 6. The calibration of the article fails in narrative — and it is the calibration that is wrong

The two translators give **the same figures**: sententious **1 in 4**,
narrative **1 in 2.4**. And both say that narrative is legitimately denser:

> **"Anaphora is the central use, the one that justifies the word — and a
> NARRATIVE is continuous anaphora. Step 2 and the calibration pull in
> opposite directions, and the kit does not say which gives way. I cut
> AGAINST the procedure, not with it."**

> **Arbitration: the calibration is APPORTIONED by register, like V2.**
> · **legal and casuistic**: almost none — beyond 1 in 6;
> · **sententious, gnomic**: about **1 in 4**;
> · **narrative**: about **1 in 2.5**, and that is normal;
> · **dialogue**: ⚠⚠ **THIS LINE IS ABROGATED ON 2026-08-17.** It said "like narrative", that is 1 in 2.5 — and **both translators of the 32nd round found it false separately**. The narrative calibration is justified by *"a narrative is continuous anaphora"*; **a dialogue of REASONING has almost no referent to resume.** *"The line was written with the dialogue of a folk tale in mind, and the rule on direct speech made it void the next day without abrogating it."* **We now follow the rule on direct speech: a reply takes the calibration of the REGISTER OF WHAT IT SAYS** — narrative for the dialogue of a tale, sententious for a dialogue of ideas.
⚠ **A global figure pushed one to cut anaphors, which are precisely the
right usage.** *A calibration that is not apportioned fights the rule it is
supposed to serve.*

#### 7. `te Gode faran` — the rule overruns its ground

Applied to the letter, it makes the miller's son say *"I shall have to go
to God of hunger"*. One translator applied it while declaring it bad, the
other refused to apply it. *"It christianises the complaint of a starving
miller."*

> **It is the CHRISTIAN REGISTER that commands; the person of the speaker
> only reinforces.** Outside that register — tale, fable, profane
> complaint, pagan narrative — one writes **`sterban`**.

### ⚠ Three cells the round found empty

· **THE SUBSTANTIVISED ADJECTIVE** — *that falska*, *that gamāl*. The kit
attests *that endilausa* and *that unstilli*, so the determiner there is
**constitutive** of the substantivisation — but none of the nine cells of
omission and none of the six outright uses mentioned it. **It is a seventh
outright use**, and it is obligatory, not optional.
· **DIRECT SPEECH** had no regime. **A reply follows the register of WHAT
IT SAYS, not that of the narrative framing it**: a character who reasons
speaks in the sententious register inside a tale.
· **THE 2nd SG. OF THE SUBJUNCTIVE** — the table promulgated the 1st and
the 3rd without saying what became of the 2nd. It is **`gebēs`**, now
entered.


### Appendix — the alliterative line

⚠ Isolated here on 2026-08-16 at the request of a translator of the 30th round: one may want to translate into PROSE, and this doctrine has no business standing in the way of someone looking for N9.

**THE ALLITERATIVE LINE — the norm** (2026-08-10, it was missing entirely,
and a translator had to apply it "with no authority from the kit to do
so"): two half-lines to a line, separated by a caesura; the **head of the
second half-line carries the letter** and commands the alliteration; one or
two heads of the first half-line answer to it. **All the vowels alliterate
with one another** (*erdu / andara*), **hw- alliterates with h-**, and
*sk-, st-, sp-* alliterate only with themselves. The line dispenses with NO
rule of morphology: at the 11th test, word order diverged by 18 % between
two independent poets while the FORMS used diverged by only 10 % — **that
is the signature of verse, and that particular freedom is licit**.

### ⚠⚠ THE FOUR CELLS THE 38th ROUND LEFT EMPTY — filled 2026-08-19

A double enquiry. Both enquirers **went through the corpora themselves** —
the complete *Heliand* (5,983 lines), the *Muspilli*, Streitberg's Gothic
Bible — and did not cite from memory. Three cells out of four are settled
by a single line of verse.

#### ① THE FUTURE — **the present indicative carries it, and nothing else**

**Rated 97 % and 99 %.** It is not a makeshift, it is the rule. The
*Heliand* lines up ten verbs in a row in the present for the Last Judgement
(2591-2606), and the *Muspilli* does the same throughout the poem.
⚠ **And Gothic DIVERGES from its model in order to do it**: where the
Greek has a future, Wulfila writes a present (*Mk* 4,25; 6,22-23; *Jn*
11,22).

· †**`skulan` + infinitive is NOT a future** (90 %): it is obligation or
  **fixed destiny** — *Muspilli* 1 *daz er touwan scal*.
· †**`willian` + infinitive is NOT a future** (88 %, and 30 occurrences out
  of 30 volitive in the *Heliand*).
· ⚠⚠ †**`werthan` + present participle DOES NOT EXIST** — the two enquiries
  inventoried **every** participle in `-andi` of the 5,983 lines: **zero
  periphrases**, zero in the *Muspilli* (90 % and 92 %).
  *It is a Latin translator's construction, and the kit rejects it.*

⚠ **The finest piece in the file**: at the Beatitudes (*Heliand*
1300-1320), the Latin lines up **six futures** — *consolabuntur,
possidebunt, saturabuntur…* The Saxon poet takes neither `skulan`, nor
`willian`, nor even the bare present: **six times in a row he writes
`mōtun` + infinitive**, "it is **granted** them to".
  *He refused six times running to render a future by a future. One cannot
  prove better that he had none — and that he did not miss it.*

#### ② CASE ATTRACTION — ⚠⚠ **THE TWO ENQUIRIES DIVERGE, WE DO NOT AVERAGE**

The question: when the main clause and the relative assign **two different
cases**, which one wins?

**One** answers "the case of the **MAIN CLAUSE**" — 92 %, four examples out
of four (*Hel.* 1276, 2270, 5807, 892), all in `sō hwē sō` whose internal
role is subject.
**The second** answers "**neither of the two: the language DOUBLES**" —
93 %, and it produces the direct counter-example:
> *Heliand* 1567-1569: antecedent ***thero** sacono endi **thero**
> sundeono* — **genitive** plural, governed by the main clause — and
> relative pronoun ***thea*** — **accusative** plural, object inside the
> relative. **No attraction at all.**
> Gothic *Lk* 7,43: ***þana** gawenja **þammei** managizo fragaf* — `þana`
> accusative of the main clause, `þammei` dative of the relative.
> **Two forms, two cases, each assigned by its own verb.**

It moreover rereads the first enquirer's four examples as **light heads +
the particle `sō`** (70 % against 30 %), with a criterion one can test: *in
the whole* Heliand *there is no case where a single form carries the case
of the main clause while serving as accusative or genitive inside.*


⚠⚠ **AND IT IS RIGHT ON AN INTERNAL POINT, WHICH I HAVE CHECKED.** The
example the kit already gives — *thes hē tharf* — carries a genitive
**assigned by `tharf`, which is INSIDE the relative**. Both translators of
the 38th round wrote "case of the main clause" **against the example in
their own kit**.

**WHAT THE KIT PRESCRIBES — and the two enquiries agree here, with the
same lines.** One does not write an inflected relative pronoun that would
have to carry two cases: **one doubles.**
· **correlative + complement clause** — ***ni fargitid hē thes, that thū
  **dādi***** — ⚠ **the 2nd sg. preterite of `dōn` is `dādi`**, attested at
  saga §II, and not †`gadedos`: the ending `-os` is neither that of the
  strong preterite (`gābi`) nor that of the weak one the kit retains
  (`haurdes`). *Written †`gadedos` on 2026-08-19 while correcting a
  prefix, reported the same evening by both translators of the 39th round:
  **I introduced a fault while correcting another, and in a sentence given
  as a MODEL.*** "he will not forget what you have done". Pattern: *Hel.*
  299, 1420, 1574; *Muspilli* 48-49;
· **head in the case of the main clause + the INVARIABLE particle** —
  ***gahugda thero wordo, the hē sagda*** (pattern *Hel.* 4997-4998): the
  genitive is on the head, the particle carries nothing.
⚠ **The divergence remains open for the BARE free relative in a conflict
of case** — 92 % against 93 %, and we do not decide. *The turn prescribed
above makes the question moot; that is why it is prescribed.*

#### ③ THE ABSOLUTE USE — **licit, free, and it is the ENTRY that was lying**

**93 % and 92 %, and both open by saying the question was badly put.** One
writes: *"'Two **obligatory** arguments' is a lexicographer's fiction. None
of the witnesses knows saturated valency."* The other: *"The two
translators looked for a way out because **the entry was lying to
them**."*

`biddan` — accusative of person + genitive of thing, the best attested verb
of its class — appears in **six configurations**:
· both arguments (*Hel.* 2756) ·
· ⚠ **the GENITIVE alone, person absent** — ***helpono biddean*** (3743),
  ***ferahes biddan*** (5412) ·
· the genitive + the person in **`te` + dative** (1566, 1573; Goth. *Mk*
  6,25 *du þamma þiudana baþ*) ·
· the accusative of person alone (690, 1794, 2990, 3500, 3574) ·
· **wholly absolute**, no argument at all (481, 1613) ·
· the genitive replaced by a **complement clause `that` + subjunctive**
  (481-82, 3743-44, 5599-5600).

**RULE: every argument of a valency class is OMISSIBLE.** The classes
V1–V5 describe a **maximal valency**, not a constraint.
· the **person argument** may be realised as `te` + dative;
· the **thing argument** may be realised as a complement clause `that`
  + subjunctive.

#### ④ THE MOOD AFTER A VERB OF OPINION — **SUBJUNCTIVE**

**`wānian` + `that` → subjunctive: 9 out of 9 and 11 out of 11**, two
independent surveys, **no counter-example** (92 % and 95 %). And `wānian`
**governs the genitive**: the full turn is ***ik wāniu thes, that …
[SUBJ]*** (88 %) — *Hel.* 299, 1420; *Muspilli* 48 *doh wānit **des** …
daz Elias … arwartit **werde***, the same construction in two original
poems in two languages.

⚠⚠ **THE MINIMAL PAIR, AND IT IS DECISIVE.** *Heliand* 4081-4083: Martha,
one single speech, four lines apart:
> **l. 4081** — "I **do think** the smell comes from it": the mood of
> **non-commitment**;
> **l. 4082-83** — "for I can tell you **in true words**, there is no doubt
> about it": **INDICATIVE**.
**What she supposes goes in the subjunctive, what she vouches for goes in
the indicative.** ⚠ And the subjunctive form of l. 4081 stands against the
**26 indicative occurrences** of the same verb in the same poem: it is not
a graphic variant, it is a mood.
  ⚠⚠ **The letter of the witness is not reproduced here**, by the rule of
  2026-08-19: *a line of verse is a warrant by its NUMBER, never by its
  form — a kit distributed on its own must not offer Old Saxon for
  copying.* I broke that rule on the very day I laid it down, and it was
  the controls that reread it for me.


⚠ **The distinction is SEMANTIC, and it is not "the content is false".**
At *Hel.* 213-214 the neighbours say *sō ik wāniu, that … god … selbo
**sendi*** — and in the narrative **the content is true**. Subjunctive all
the same. **The trigger is the speaker's NON-COMMITMENT.**

⚠ **A test of the divergence, checked on both sides**: at *Jn* 11,39
Gothic writes a bare assertion in the indicative (*ju fuls ist*). The
*Heliand*, at the same passage, **inserts a verb of opinion that its
source does not have** and puts the complement clause in the subjunctive.
*It is not a calque: it is an addition, running against the model.*

⚠⚠ **CONSEQUENCE FOR THE 38th ROUND**: "but I do not vouch for it" is
**redundant in Frenkisk**, the subjunctive says it already. The translator
who used the indicative made the speaker say that he vouches for it, and
then that he does not. *His intuition was right; he overrode it because
the cell was empty.*

#### ⚠ AND A CORRECTION TO THE HIERARCHY ITSELF

An enquirer points out, and I accept it: **the syntactic hierarchy
confuses "Old High German" with "an Old High German translation".** The
*Muspilli* is rank (c) by its letter, but it is **original poetry with no
Latin model** — it is therefore **immune to the objection of calque**,
exactly like the *Heliand*. For SYNTAX it is worth **rank 1 bis**, not rank
3 alongside Otfrid and Notker.
  *A witness's rank must be apportioned by the GENRE of the text, not by
  its language: it is the calque one fears, not the dialect.*


## GOVERNMENT, TEMPORAL CONJUNCTIONS AND THE ADVERBIAL RELATIVE

Double enquiry at rank (a), 2026-08-16, on the eight cells the two
translators of the 30th round had had to improvise.

⚠⚠ **AND BOTH ENQUIRERS OPEN WITH THE SAME WARNING, WHICH IS A LIMIT OF
THE HIERARCHY ITSELF: RANK (a) IS STRUCTURALLY MUTE ON SYNTAX.** The
Malberg glosses are an **exclusively lexical** material and yield not one
subordinate clause; the Wachtendonck psalter is an **interlinear gloss
calqued word for word on the Latin**, hence a near-null syntactic witness.
For everything that follows, the effective hierarchy becomes **(b) Old
Saxon > (c) Old High German**, checked against Gothic — outside the
hierarchy, but the only old Germanic language with an abundant and dated
syntax of subordination.

> **The kit's hierarchy of standards is made for the LETTER and the
> LEXICON. It has no usable rank (a) for SYNTAX, and that must be said
> instead of letting the contrary be believed.**

### ⚠⚠ WHAT THE ABSENCE OF A GOVERNMENT MEANS — ruling of 2026-08-18

**The translator of the 34th round raised the costliest objection in the
file**, and it did not bear on a form:

> *"A government missing from a lemma that is present is not declared — it
> is guessed in silence, and two translators will guess it differently
> without ever knowing that they diverged."*

### ⚠⚠ THE OBJECT GENITIVE — FOUR ENQUIRIES, AND THREE OF MY RULES FALL

**Ruling of 2026-08-18, on a double enquiry into the genitive (G1, G2) and
a double enquiry into four open cells (C1, C2).** None of the four
enquirers opened a file of the project.


#### 1. The object genitive is PRODUCTIVE around 550 — and my fear was groundless

G1 rates it **85 %**, G2 **92 %**, separately. It is neither residual nor
lexicalised: it alternates according to **grammatical** conditions —
partitive object, indefinite, under negation, pronominal — so it works as a
**rule**, not as a closed list.

⚠⚠ **AND MY PREMISS WAS FACTUALLY FALSE.** I wrote that "Middle Dutch shows
the collapse". G2 **checked Stoett**, *Middelnederlandsche Spraakkunst.
Syntaxis*, 3rd ed. 1923, **§§ 167-171**: nine classes of genitive verbs are
**still productive there in the 13th century**, and **§ 167 VI** explicitly
states the frame *"accusative or dative of the person + genitive of the
thing"*.

> *My witness for the collapse attests, seven centuries after my date, the
> two classes I feared I had lost. I had frightened myself with a
> second-hand reading.*

#### 2. ⚠⚠ "VERB X GOVERNS THE GENITIVE" IS ALREADY FALSE IN 550

The two enquiries converge on the substance:

> **The genitive is triggered jointly by the VERB and by the properties of
> the OBJECT. The verb sets a predisposition; the object decides the
> occurrence.**

Factors, in order of force: **quantification** (partial object → gen.;
wholly affected → acc.) · **definiteness** · **negation** · ⚠ **the form of
the object — a PRONOMINAL object strongly favours the genitive**, and that
is its last stronghold: Stoett notes that in Middle Dutch the pronominal
genitive `des`, `dies` spreads **even to verbs that do not govern it**.

**The kit therefore now writes a PREFERENTIAL FRAME, not a cell.**

#### 3. My three classes are not the right fault lines

Both enquirers say so, by two different routes — and it is their
disagreement that instructs:

· **G1**: the predictor is the **semantic role** of the genitive. Ablative
  and privative hold out longest; the propositional stem gives way first,
  **and it gives way to the complement clause, not to the accusative**.
· **G2**: what predicts it is **which cell the accusative occupies**. In V1
  the accusative is free, hence an immediate competitor; in V4 and V5 it is
  already taken by the person, so the genitive is **structurally
  protected** and can give way only to a preposition.

**The two predict the same ranking: V5 > V4 > V1.** Two independent lines
of reasoning, one conclusion.

⚠ **V1 is to be split in four** — privation/need (the most stable),
memory, affect, mockery. *A class confidence aggregated at 76 % would mask
four very different states.*

#### 4. Four corrections of content, one of them a deletion

| verb | what the kit carried | what the enquiries impose |
|---|---|---|
| **to choose** | acc. pers. + gen. thing | ⚠⚠ **TO BE WITHDRAWN.** `*keusan-` is a verb with a full accusative; the genitive there is only the ordinary partitive ("to choose from among"). G1 rates it false at 70 %, G2 names it *"the only one of the eighteen to be withdrawn outright"* |
| **to fill** | dat. pers. + gen. thing | **acc. of the CONTAINER + gen. of the CONTENT** — there is no dative of the person |
| **to deprive of** | class V1 | **class V5**: acc. of the person + gen. of the thing |
| **verbs of affect** | nominative subject + gen. | ⚠⚠ **IMPERSONAL or REFLEXIVE** — acc./dat. of the experiencer + gen. of the stimulus. **The kit did not have that frame**, so those verbs were ill-formed in it |

#### 5. ⚠ Behaghel gives the INVENTORY, never the WEIGHTS

Both enquirers warn against the work I was leaning on without having
opened it. G2 is the sharper:

> *"Behaghel is a collector of attestations, not a statistician. A verb
> that governs the genitive in 5 % of its occurrences appears there exactly
> like a verb with a near-obligatory genitive. If you build on his lists,
> you will produce a language **in which the genitive is two to three
> times too frequent**."*


⚠⚠ And they diverge on the rating itself: G1 gives volume I as the right
one; G2 rates it at **80 % only** and asks that volume II, section
*Verbum*, be opened **as well**. **Neither of the two was able to verify a
single paragraph number.**

#### 6. Gothic: my suspicion was well founded, and it can be neutralised

Both say so: Wulfila calques the Greek, so his genitive proves nothing
**where the Greek has one**. But the objection falls as soon as **Old
Norse** is brought in, which never saw a Greek text and which carries the
same frames — `biðja e-n e-s`, `krefja e-n e-s`, `fylla e-t e-s`, `synja
e-m e-s`.

> **PROMULGATED: Gothic enters the file only (a) confirmed by a non-Greek
> witness, or (b) when its genitive STANDS AGAINST a Greek accusative.** A
> departure from the model cannot be a calque — it is the same test as the
> ruling on the syntactic hierarchy.
